# Paper Set Evidence Digest

Each item below is a compact paper-level analysis digest. Use only this digest.

- source: Salahshour, M. (2021). Evolution of cooperation and consistent personalities in public goods games. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper finds that in two-round public goods games, both reward-based and assortative mechanisms can promote the evolution of cooperation and the emergence of consistent cooperative or defective personalities. In the 'reward dilemma' scenario, only first-round cooperators are allowed to play a second PGG, which can incentivize cooperation if the second game's enhancement factor is high enough. In the 'assortative' scenario, both cooperators and defectors play a second PGG in separate groups, and competition between these groups can further promote cooperation and personality consistency. The model shows that cooperation is maximized at intermediate values of the second-round enhancement factor, and that consistent personalities (cooperating or defecting in both rounds) naturally evolve. However, the paper does not model punishment or sanctions, and does not directly report efficiency or group payoff as a main outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It is highly relevant for understanding how reward and assortment mechanisms (and the possibility of repeated or conditional interaction) can promote cooperation and personality consistency, but it does not address punishment, sanctions, or their efficiency effects. The findings may be indirectly informative for prediction tasks involving reward or assortment, but not for punishment. The model's outcomes are primarily behavioral (cooperation rates, personality consistency), not efficiency or group payoff, so it should not be used as a primary source for predicting efficiency effects of punishment.

- source: Wei, M., Wang, X., Liu, L. Z., Zheng, H. W., Jiang, Y. S., Hao, Y. J., Zheng, Z. M., Fu, F., & Tang, S. T. (2025). Indirect reciprocity in the public goods game with collective reputations. *Journal of the Royal Society Interface*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that in public goods games with collective reputation and indirect reciprocity, all of the leading-eight social norm strategies can sustain cooperation in social dilemmas when the group assessment criterion (social strictness) is set at a moderate level. Too relaxed or too strict assessment criteria undermine cooperation. The evolutionary success of different strategies and assessment criteria depends on the payoff environment: stricter criteria are favored in low-payoff (high-dilemma) environments, while relaxed criteria are favored as payoffs increase. The model shows that collective reputation can mitigate social dilemmas and that the interplay between game structure and reputation structure is crucial for the evolution of cooperation. However, the model does not include explicit punishment or reward mechanisms, and efficiency is not directly measured.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence for the prediction task by showing that, in public goods games with indirect reciprocity and collective reputation (but without explicit punishment), cooperation and expected payoffs are maximized at moderate levels of social strictness. The findings suggest that the structure of reputation assessment (a design dimension not in the standard list) can play a similar role to punishment in sustaining cooperation, but the absence of explicit punishment means the results cannot be directly transferred to predict the effect of enabling punishment. The paper is most useful for understanding how non-punitive, reputation-based mechanisms affect cooperation and payoffs, and for contextualizing the importance of social norm design. It does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Chen, Q., Chen, T., & Wang, Y. J. (2019). Cleverly handling the donation information can promote cooperation in public goods game. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: Publishing the donation list partially (i.e., only for those who contribute above a threshold) increases total contributions to the public good compared to publishing all or none of the list. The optimal threshold for publication can be set dynamically as the mean of recent contributions, which is both effective and convenient. The model shows that this approach increases the sum of contributions and reduces the variance in contributions, indicating more uniform and higher cooperation. The effect is robust to changes in network connectivity and the window of historical data used. Gossip and self-assessment of face act as indirect social sanctions, motivating higher contributions. There is no explicit punishment or reward mechanism with costs and impact parameters as in standard PGG punishment treatments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that indirect social sanctions (via partial publication of contribution information and gossip) can increase total group contributions in a PGG-like environment. While it does not study explicit costly punishment, it suggests that reputational mechanisms can substitute for or complement formal punishment in promoting cooperation and group welfare. For prediction tasks, the findings imply that introducing a reputational/gossip-based sanction (by publicizing contributions above a threshold) can increase group payoff, especially if the threshold is set adaptively. However, the absence of explicit punishment cost/impact parameters means the results are not directly transferable to standard punishment treatments. The sum of contributions is a close but not exact proxy for efficiency.

- source: Smaldino, P. E., & Lubell, M. (2011). An Institutional Mechanism for Assortment in an Ecology of Games. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that capacity constraints (limits on the number of players per game) are a simple and effective institutional mechanism for promoting positive assortment of cooperators in an ecology of public goods games. When group size is limited, cooperators can cluster together and achieve higher payoffs relative to defectors, even without punishment or reputation. Budget constraints (limits on the number of games per player) are less effective. The model does not include punishment, but discusses it as a more complex and potentially costly alternative. The main outcome is the relative cooperator payoff, not group efficiency or total welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not model punishment or sanctions. However, it is relevant for understanding how institutional design (e.g., group size limits) can promote cooperation and higher payoffs without the need for punishment. For the downstream prediction task, this paper suggests that capacity constraints can increase the relative payoff of cooperators, but does not inform the effect of punishment on efficiency. It is not directly useful for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions plus control efficiency in the context of punishment.

- source: Gao, J., Li, Z., Wu, T., & Wang, L. (2010). Diversity of contribution promotes cooperation in public goods games. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that allowing cooperators to preferentially contribute more to groups with higher cooperation levels (high-quality groups) significantly promotes the evolution of cooperation in public goods games, both on regular lattices and scale-free networks. The mechanism works by enabling cooperators to cluster and avoid exploitation by defectors, and by increasing the wealth/payoff of cooperators for a given cooperation level. The effect is stronger as the preference parameter (alpha) increases. The paper interprets the reduction of contributions to low-quality groups as a form of indirect, costless group-level punishment, but does not model or analyze explicit costly punishment. Group efficiency or total group payoff is not directly reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is relevant for understanding how endogenous contribution patterns (where cooperators can direct more resources to cooperative groups) affect cooperation and the distribution of payoffs in public goods games. However, it does not provide direct evidence on the effect of explicit, costly punishment on efficiency or group payoff. The findings suggest that indirect, costless group-level punishment (via reduced contributions to low-quality groups) can promote cooperation and increase the payoffs of cooperators, but the implications for overall group efficiency are not quantified. For prediction tasks focused on explicit punishment interventions, this paper provides only adjacent evidence, mainly relevant for understanding alternative mechanisms to punishment.

- source: Arenas, A., Camacho, J., Cuesta, J. A., & Requejo, R. J. (2011). The joker effect: Cooperation driven by destructive agents. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that introducing destructive agents ('jokers') into a public goods game leads to robust cycles in which cooperation periodically surges. The mechanism is that jokers harm defectors more than cooperators, creating a dynamic where cooperators can invade jokers, jokers invade defectors, and defectors invade cooperators. The cycles are robust to mutation and population size. The model provides explicit payoff expressions for each strategy, but the main focus is on the evolutionary dynamics of strategy frequencies, not on group efficiency or total payoff. The findings are relevant for understanding how destructive threats can promote cooperation, but do not directly inform the effect of punishment on efficiency in standard PGGs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is not directly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not study punishment or sanctions per se. Instead, it analyzes the effect of introducing a destructive agent type that harms all non-jokers. The model is a theoretical extension of the PGG and provides insight into how external threats or destructive forces can promote cooperation through population dynamics. However, it does not provide empirical or quantitative evidence about efficiency changes due to punishment, nor does it manipulate or analyze standard punishment mechanisms. Its main value is in highlighting that destructive forces can, under certain conditions, increase cooperation, but this is not equivalent to the effect of punishment in PGGs.

- source: Moreira, J. A., Pacheco, J. M., & Santos, F. C. (2013). Evolution of collective action in adaptive social structures. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that when individuals can adapt their social ties—specifically, break links with defectors more quickly than with cooperators—cooperation can be sustained at higher levels than in well-mixed or static networks. The evolutionary dynamics shift from defection dominance to a scenario with stable coexistence of cooperators and defectors, depending on the group size, enhancement factor, and network adaptation rates. Stochastic effects (e.g., mutation, exploration) further help populations escape defection traps and maintain cooperation. However, the paper does not analyze the effect of punishment or sanctions, nor does it report group efficiency or total payoff as a primary outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is useful for understanding how endogenous partner selection and adaptive network structure can promote cooperation in PGGs, which may indirectly inform predictions about efficiency in environments where such mechanisms are present. However, it does not address punishment or sanctions, nor does it provide direct evidence on efficiency or group payoff. For the downstream prediction task, this paper suggests that adaptive partner selection can increase cooperation rates, but it does not provide quantitative estimates of efficiency changes due to punishment or other interventions.

- source: Takács, K., & Janky, B. (2007). Smiling contributions:: Social control in a public goods game with network decline. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, reward_exists
  findings: The paper develops a theoretical framework for public goods games with endogenous network change and social control mechanisms. It predicts that clustered networks reduce collective action and that the ability to delete ties leads to segregation between contributors and defectors. Social control is operationalized as the ability to send happy or sad smileys, which are intended to induce conformity or selective incentives. The experimental design is described, but no data or results are presented. The main dependent variable in the proposed experiments is contribution rate, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical insights into how network structure and non-costly social control (smileys) might affect contribution behavior in public goods games. However, it does not provide empirical evidence on efficiency or payoff outcomes, nor does it study costly punishment as typically defined in the literature. The model suggests that social control (even if non-costly) and network clustering can influence cooperation, but the lack of empirical results and the focus on non-payoff outcomes limit its direct utility for predicting efficiency effects of punishment interventions. The design dimensions are discussed in the context of the model and proposed experiment, but not tested empirically.

- source: Yang, R., Chen, T., & Chen, Q. (2020). Promoting cooperation by reputation-based payoff transfer mechanism in public goods game. *European Physical Journal B*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The reputation-based payoff transfer mechanism, which redistributes payoffs from low- to high-reputation players, can significantly increase the fraction of cooperators in a spatial public goods game. The effect depends on the parameters controlling the proportion of players who lose or gain payoffs (transfer ratios) and the strength of the transfer. When both the proportion of punished (transferred) and rewarded (accepted) players are moderate and balanced, cooperation can quickly dominate the population. The mechanism is most effective when all players are either punished or rewarded (transfer ratio = 1). However, the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, so the effect on efficiency is not directly assessed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that mechanisms combining endogenous punishment and reward based on reputation can increase cooperation rates in public goods games, especially when the redistribution is balanced and covers the whole population. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency. The findings may be useful for predicting cooperation rates, but not for predicting efficiency outcomes as required by the downstream task.

- source: Toriumi, F., Yamamoto, H., & Okada, I. (2020). A belief in rewards accelerates cooperation on consumer-generated media. *Journal of Computational Social Science*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The study finds that in a public goods game with only rewards and meta-rewards (no punishment), cooperation can be sustained if players can observe and respond to the individual meta-rewarding rates of others. When players base their reward-giving on the observed likelihood of receiving a meta-reward from a specific cooperator, stable cooperation emerges. If players cannot condition on this information, cooperation collapses. The benefit-to-cost ratio of rewards and meta-rewards must be sufficiently high for cooperation to be maintained. The model does not include punishment, and efficiency or group payoff is not directly analyzed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It is relevant for understanding the role of reward and meta-reward mechanisms, and highlights the importance of observability and benefit-to-cost ratios for sustaining cooperation. However, since punishment is not modeled, and efficiency or group payoff is not a reported outcome, this paper should not be used as direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency. It may be useful for understanding adjacent mechanisms (reward-based), but not for the core prediction task.

- source: Szolnoki, A., & Perc, M. (2010). Reward and cooperation in the spatial public goods game. *Epl*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The study finds that introducing costly reward in a spatial public goods game can promote cooperation, particularly when the synergy factor (MPCR) is low. However, the effect is non-monotonic: moderate rewards can be more effective than high rewards due to the emergence of cyclic dominance among defectors, cooperators, and rewarding cooperators. At high synergy factors, network reciprocity alone can sustain cooperation, and reward mainly determines which cooperative strategy prevails. The paper notes that, compared to punishment, reward is less efficient at promoting cooperation, especially because rewarding cooperators always pay a cost, making them vulnerable to second-order free-riders. The main reported outcomes are the stationary frequencies of each strategy and the phase diagrams of their coexistence, not group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the effect of reward (not punishment) on cooperation in spatial public goods games. It does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes, so it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. However, it suggests that costly reward can promote cooperation under certain conditions, but is generally less effective than punishment. The findings are most relevant for understanding the role of reward mechanisms and the importance of spatial structure, synergy factor (MPCR), and cost-benefit ratios in shaping cooperation. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is only adjacent: it does not provide direct evidence on punishment or efficiency, but may inform expectations about the relative effectiveness of reward versus punishment and the importance of game parameters.

- source: Du, C. P., Jia, D. Y., Jin, L. B., & Shi, L. (2018). The impact of neutral reward on cooperation in public good game. *European Physical Journal B*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The study finds that introducing a neutral reward mechanism, where the majority strategy in a group is rewarded by the minority, promotes the evolution of cooperation in a spatial public goods game. As the reward factor increases, cooperation becomes more prevalent, and for sufficiently high reward, the system can reach full cooperation. The main outcomes are the frequency of cooperators and the spatial distribution of strategies. The paper does not report group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that reward mechanisms (even when not perfectly targeted) can promote cooperation in spatial public goods games. However, because the outcomes are limited to cooperation rates and do not include efficiency or group payoff, the findings are only adjacent to the prediction task. The game design dimensions are described in the model, but the absence of payoff-based outcomes limits the paper's direct utility for predicting efficiency effects of punishment or reward. The results may suggest that reward mechanisms can increase efficiency if cooperation translates to higher group payoff, but this is not demonstrated in the paper.

- source: Zhang, H. R., & Perc, M. (2016). Evolution of conditional cooperation under multilevel selection. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper demonstrates that in public goods games with continuous contribution and multilevel selection, the presence of conditional strategies (modeled as piecewise linear response functions) enables the emergence and stability of high cooperation levels. The dominant strategy is robust to initial conditions and parameter changes. The study does not include explicit punishment or reward mechanisms, and does not directly report efficiency or group payoff, focusing instead on average contribution and strategy prevalence.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that conditional cooperation strategies can sustain high contribution levels in public goods games under multilevel selection, even without punishment or reward. However, it does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff, nor does it manipulate or analyze punishment-related dimensions. Its findings are most relevant for understanding baseline cooperation dynamics in the absence of explicit sanctioning mechanisms, and for interpreting the role of conditional strategies as an alternative to punishment. It is not directly informative for predicting the efficiency impact of enabling punishment.

- source: Sutter, M., & Untertrifaller, A. (2020). Children's heterogeneity in cooperation and parental background: An experimental study. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The study finds that allocating taxes and rewards based on memory reputation generally increases the cooperation rate in spatial public goods games, especially in environments with high taxes or high synergy factors. In low-tax, low-synergy environments (favorable to defectors), equal allocation of rewards and taxes is more effective at sustaining cooperation. The model shows that the effectiveness of reward mechanisms depends on the balance between tax and reward costs, memory length, and the synergy factor. However, the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, and does not include punishment or sanctioning mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include punishment or efficiency outcomes. Its findings are only indirectly relevant: it suggests that the structure of incentive mechanisms (tax-funded rewards, reputation-based allocation) can affect cooperation rates, and that the effectiveness of such mechanisms depends on environmental parameters. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is not directly informative, but it may provide contextual insight into how non-punitive incentive mechanisms interact with game design dimensions.

- source: Liu, L. J., & Chen, X. J. (2024). Evolutionary Dynamics of Preguidance Strategies in Population Games. *Ieee Transactions on Computational Social Systems*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper introduces and analyzes two forms of preemptive guidance strategies (peer and pool guidance) in a one-shot public goods game with a pregame negotiation phase. Both forms of guidance can reduce the prevalence of defectors and second-order free riders (cooperators who do not guide). Pool guidance is more effective than peer guidance, especially at higher guidance costs. The main reported outcomes are the steady-state frequencies of each strategy, not group efficiency or payoff. The model does not include standard punishment or reward mechanisms, and does not report on group payoff or efficiency as an outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and simulation evidence that preemptive guidance strategies can reduce free riding and increase the prevalence of guidance strategies in PGGs, especially when guidance costs are low. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment or sanctions on efficiency in PGG-like environments. The guidance strategies studied are adjacent to, but not the same as, standard punishment or sanctioning mechanisms. The findings may be useful for understanding the dynamics of pre-commitment or preemptive intervention, but are not directly transferable to the prediction of efficiency outcomes from punishment in standard PGGs.

- source: Tomassini, M., & Antonioni, A. (2020). Public Goods Games on Coevolving Social Network Models. *Frontiers in Physics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that allowing agents to rewire their social connections when unsatisfied (i.e., when their payoff is less than their contribution) leads to higher average contributions in public goods games on networks, compared to static networks. The transition from low to high contribution occurs at lower enhancement factors when rewiring is allowed, and higher rewiring probabilities further increase average contributions. These effects are robust across both synthetic and real social network structures. However, the model does not include explicit punishment or reward options, and does not report efficiency or group payoff as outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that enabling a form of costless partner switching (exit) can increase average contributions in networked public goods games, which may be relevant for predicting the effects of punishment-like mechanisms that allow players to avoid or sanction defectors. However, because the model does not include explicit costly punishment, and does not report efficiency or group payoff, its direct value for predicting efficiency effects of punishment is limited. The findings are most relevant for understanding the impact of dynamic network structure (partner choice) rather than punishment per se.

- source: Szolnoki, A., & Chen, X. J. (2017). Alliance formation with exclusion in the spatial public goods game. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in spatial public goods games, the introduction of an excluder strategy (which monitors and excludes defectors at a cost) can lead to the elimination of defectors when excluders form alliances with other cooperative strategies (cooperators or punishers). This effect is robust even when the cost of exclusion is extremely high, provided the population structure allows for the formation of sufficiently large clusters of excluders and cooperators/punishers. The main outcomes are the extinction probabilities of defectors and the conditions for the survival of excluders, not direct measures of group efficiency or payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the idea that exclusion-based sanctions (a form of punishment) can be highly effective in structured populations, especially when combined with other cooperative strategies. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. Instead, it suggests that under certain spatial and strategic conditions, sanctioning mechanisms can eliminate defection, which would likely increase efficiency, but this is not quantified. The findings are most relevant for understanding the qualitative impact of exclusion and alliances in spatially structured games, rather than for quantitative prediction of efficiency outcomes.

- source: Liu, L. J., & Chen, X. J. (2022). Indirect exclusion can promote cooperation in repeated group interactions. *Proceedings of the Royal Society A-mathematical Physical and Engineering Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that indirect exclusion—where a game organizer probabilistically selects cooperators for future participation—can promote the stable coexistence of cooperators and defectors or even lead to the dominance of cooperation in repeated public goods games. The effectiveness of indirect exclusion increases with higher identification probability and more repeated rounds. The model avoids the second-order free-rider problem associated with costly direct exclusion. However, the main reported outcomes are the equilibrium fraction of cooperators and the stationary distribution of strategies, not group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that indirect exclusion mechanisms can increase the equilibrium level of cooperation in repeated public goods games, especially as the number of rounds and the probability of correctly identifying cooperators increase. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment (or exclusion) on efficiency. The results are most relevant for predicting changes in cooperation rates rather than efficiency, and the exclusion mechanism studied is costless and indirect, differing from standard costly punishment treatments.

- source: Xiao, J. F., Liu, L. J., Chen, X. J., & Szolnoki, A. (2023). Evolution of cooperation driven by sampling reward. *Journal of Physics-complexity*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the sampling reward mechanism can effectively increase the average cooperation level in both PGG and CRD models. Higher reward thresholds and higher reward intensities promote cooperation, but also increase the cost to the institution. The effect of reward intensity is non-linear: above a critical threshold, further increases yield little additional cooperation. Sample size has a non-monotonic effect on cost, with intermediate sizes minimizing cost. The findings are robust across both game types. However, the paper does not report on group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, and does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment or sanctions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that reward mechanisms (specifically, sampling reward) can increase cooperation rates in PGG-like environments, with effects moderated by reward threshold, intensity, and sample size. However, it does not report group efficiency or total payoff, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency. The paper is most useful for understanding how reward design parameters affect cooperation rates and institutional costs, but not for predicting efficiency outcomes when punishment is enabled. It does not provide empirical or theoretical results on punishment, nor does it analyze efficiency as defined by group payoff relative to full cooperation.

- source: Battu, B. (2023). Co-evolution of conditional cooperation and social norm. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The model finds that high initial cooperation and low contribution costs favor the emergence and stability of high cooperation and moderate norm sensitivity. When contribution costs are high or initial cooperation is low, cooperation fails to establish. The noise parameter (imperfect information about past cooperation) can modulate but not destroy cooperation unless it is very high. The model does not include explicit punishment or reward actions; instead, norm enforcement is internalized as psychic costs for free-riding, which depend on the number of cooperators, the cost of contribution, and individual norm sensitivity. The model does not report efficiency or group payoff, so its findings are not directly translatable to efficiency prediction tasks.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, mechanistic insight into how internalized norm enforcement (psychic costs) and conditional cooperation can sustain cooperation in repeated PGGs, especially under low contribution costs and high initial cooperation. However, it does not model or report the effects of explicit punishment mechanisms, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff. Therefore, it is not directly informative for predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency in PGG-like games. Its main value is in highlighting the importance of initial conditions, contribution cost, and norm sensitivity for the emergence of cooperation, but not for quantifying efficiency changes due to punishment.

- source: Chen, T., Wu, Z. H., & Wang, L. (2018). Disseminators or silencers: The effect of information diffusion intensity on cooperation in public goods game. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that the presence and type of information dissemination strongly affect the speed at which full cooperation is achieved in a spatial public goods game with reputation and conformity. Negative disseminators (who spread information about defectors) most often lead to the fastest convergence to full cooperation, followed by neutral and positive disseminators. Silencers (no dissemination) are least effective. The effect of dissemination type is most pronounced when conformity synergy, link-neighbor degree, population size, or environmental noise are high, or when the synergy factor is low. However, all models eventually reach full cooperation, so there is no analysis of efficiency, group payoff, or welfare differences between models.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include a punishment or sanctioning mechanism, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. However, it is informative about the role of information diffusion and reputation in accelerating the achievement of cooperation. The findings suggest that mechanisms which amplify reputational consequences for defectors (akin to negative gossip or public shaming) can speed up the transition to full cooperation, especially in certain parameter regimes. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency or payoff, this paper is only indirectly relevant, as it does not address whether the final payoffs or efficiency differ between conditions.

- source: Wang, Z., Szolnoki, A., & Perc, M. (2014). Rewarding evolutionary fitness with links between populations promotes cooperation. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that rewarding players whose utility exceeds a threshold by granting them external links to another population can significantly promote cooperation in both public goods and prisoner's dilemma games on structured networks. The effect is maximized at intermediate threshold values, where rewarded players are rare but able to percolate, acting as catalysts for cooperation. If the threshold is too low or too high, the effect diminishes. The main reported outcome is the fraction of cooperators; efficiency or group payoff is not directly analyzed. The results are robust across network types and game details. Punishment is not modeled, only discussed as background.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It models a reward mechanism (external links for high-fitness players) and analyzes its effect on cooperation rates, not on efficiency or payoff. While it discusses the relative merits of reward and punishment in the introduction, all results pertain to reward. Therefore, it is not directly informative for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment and efficiency, except as context for how reward mechanisms can promote cooperation under certain network and threshold conditions.

- source: Zhou, T. W., Ding, S., Fan, W. J., & Wang, H. (2016). An improved public goods game model with reputation effect on the spatial lattices. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that embedding a reputation effect into the utility function of agents in a spatial PGG model leads to a substantial increase in the stationary fraction of cooperators, especially as the reputation factor increases. The critical synergy factor required for cooperation to emerge is reduced, and the region of parameter space supporting full cooperation is expanded. The model does not include explicit punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. The findings are based on Monte Carlo simulations and are relevant for understanding how reputation mechanisms can promote cooperation, but do not directly inform predictions about efficiency or payoff effects of punishment in PGG-like environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that reputation mechanisms can promote cooperation in spatial PGGs, but does not address punishment, sanctions, or efficiency outcomes. For the downstream prediction task—predicting efficiency effects of punishment—this paper is only weakly informative. It suggests that non-monetary social mechanisms (like reputation) can increase cooperation, but does not provide data or analysis on how punishment affects efficiency, nor does it report group payoff or welfare. The game design dimensions are described in detail for the reputation mechanism, but not for punishment or efficiency.

- source: Zheng, J. J., Ren, T. Y., Ma, G., & Dong, J. H. (2021). The emergence and implementation of pool exclusion in spatial public goods game with heterogeneous ability-to-pay. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that introducing pool exclusion, where excluders pay a cost to exclude defectors from the public good, can significantly increase the level of cooperation in a spatial public goods game. The effectiveness of exclusion depends on parameters such as the enhancement factor (r), the probability and effectiveness of exclusion, and the contribution rate to the exclusion pool. Cooperation is more likely to prevail when exclusion is effective and not overinvested. However, the main reported outcome is the fraction of cooperators and excluders, not group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the idea that exclusionary sanctions (pool exclusion) can increase cooperation in spatial public goods games, especially under certain parameter regimes. However, because the main outcome is cooperation level (not efficiency or group payoff), and because the sanction is exclusion rather than classic costly punishment, the findings should be used with caution for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGGs. The results suggest that effective, not overly costly exclusion can help sustain cooperation, which may translate to higher efficiency, but this is not directly demonstrated.

- source: Chen, Q., Chen, T., & Wang, Y. J. (2017). Publishing the donation list incompletely promotes the emergence of cooperation in public goods game. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: Publishing the donation list incompletely (only for those who contribute above a threshold) leads to higher total contributions and more equal contributions among players in a public goods game on a network. There is an optimal threshold for publication that maximizes these effects. The per capita number of neighbors and the amount of historical data players consider also moderate the effect, but the main result is robust: partial transparency outperforms full transparency or secrecy. The model is agent-based and does not include explicit punishment or sanctioning mechanisms; instead, it relies on social reputation and face loss as motivators.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that social information mechanisms (such as partial publication of contribution lists) can increase cooperation in public goods games, but it does not study monetary punishment or its effect on efficiency. The findings are relevant for predicting the effect of information disclosure or reputation-based interventions, not for predicting the effect of adding costly punishment. The sum of contributions is a proxy for group welfare, but efficiency (as defined by payoff relative to full cooperation) is not directly reported. Use this paper as informative for the effect of social/reputational incentives, not for monetary punishment treatments.

- source: Yang, Z. H., Li, Z., Wu, T., & Wang, L. (2013). Effects of adaptive dynamical linking in networked games. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that adaptive dynamical linking—where agents lengthen or shorten social ties based on whether their partners cooperate or defect—can dramatically increase the prevalence of cooperation in both the prisoner's dilemma and public goods game. The effect is stronger when agents adjust their links more rapidly (higher adjustment rate, Δ), and under weak selection. The mechanism does not involve any explicit punishment, reward, or reputation system. The outcome measured is the frequency of full cooperation (all-cooperator absorbing state) in repeated simulations. There is no direct measurement or discussion of group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the prediction task of how punishment affects efficiency in public goods games. It is relevant as a demonstration that network adaptation (partner switching) can promote cooperation, but it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, nor does it report efficiency or payoff outcomes. Therefore, it should not be used as direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency. It may be contextually useful for understanding alternative mechanisms (partner choice) that can promote cooperation, but not for quantitative prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment.

- source: Hu, D., Liu, J. Z., Liu, C., & Chu, C. (2025). Dynamic incentives and environmental feedback in public goods games: Promoting cooperation through critical thresholds. *Chaos*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A

  findings: The study finds that introducing dynamic, group-level environmental feedback (bonuses or penalties based on collective contribution thresholds) in a spatial public goods game strongly promotes cooperation, especially when the synergy factor is low. The effect is not due to individual punishment or reward, but to a group-level adjustment of payoffs. The main reported outcomes are cooperation rates and the distribution of strategies and incentives. The model shows that higher expectation thresholds for group contributions can, counterintuitively, make cooperation more likely, and that the system transitions from defection to cooperation as the dynamic incentive increases. However, the paper does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include a punishment mechanism. Instead, it models a group-level dynamic incentive (environmental feedback) that is not targeted at individuals and is not costly to assign. The findings may be indirectly relevant for understanding how group-level feedback mechanisms (distinct from punishment) can promote cooperation, but they do not inform the prediction of efficiency outcomes when punishment is enabled versus disabled. The results are most relevant for adjacent mechanisms (dynamic group-level incentives) rather than direct punishment or sanctions.

  findings: The study finds that increasing the threshold required to initiate a public goods game (i.e., requiring more cooperators for the game to proceed) leads to higher stable levels of cooperation in a spatially structured population. Introducing a reputation adjustment factor, where defectors lose reputation and cooperators gain reputation, further increases cooperation rates. The model shows that even small reputation effects can shift the population toward cooperation. The effect of noise in strategy updating is complex: higher noise can promote cooperation under high thresholds. However, the paper does not analyze or report group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare; all main results are in terms of the fraction of cooperators.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that mechanisms which increase the cost of defection (here, via reputation loss) or require a threshold of cooperation to initiate public goods production can increase cooperation rates in spatial PGGs. However, because the model does not include explicit punishment (costly sanctions) and does not report efficiency or group payoff, it is not directly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. The findings may be useful for understanding how threshold and reputation mechanisms affect cooperation, but should not be used as direct evidence for the efficiency effects of punishment interventions.

  findings: The study finds that introducing conditional participation (interpreted as a form of punishment) in a spatial public goods game can promote cooperation, but only within an optimal range of aspiration levels. If aspiration is too low, the effect is minimal; if too high, cooperation is suppressed. The transition from defection to cooperation can be abrupt or gradual depending on aspiration and synergy factor. The main reported outcome is the fraction of cooperators, not group efficiency or payoff. The findings may be useful for understanding how conditional participation or abstention can affect cooperation, but should not be directly used to predict efficiency effects of standard punishment interventions.

- source: Li, Y. X., Jin, X. G., Su, X. C., Kong, F. S., & Peng, C. B. (2010). Cooperation and charity in spatial public goods game under different strategy update rules. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that charity (modeled as payoff transfer from egalitarians to the lowest-payoff neighbor) does not enhance cooperation under random selection update rules, but does promote cooperation under threshold selection rules (where only the lowest-payoff individuals update their strategies). The effect of charity is measured by the frequency of cooperation, not by group payoff or efficiency. The model shows that the presence of charity can lower the threshold for the multiplication factor (r) needed to sustain cooperation under threshold selection. The frequency of charity is positively correlated with the variance of payoffs in the population. The paper does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is not directly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include punishment or report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. However, it provides indirect evidence about the effect of a redistribution mechanism (charity) on cooperation rates under different update rules. The findings suggest that the effect of a second-stage redistribution mechanism (distinct from punishment) on cooperation depends on the selection dynamics, but the lack of efficiency or payoff reporting limits its direct utility for the prediction task. The game structure and update rules are described in detail, which may be useful for mapping to similar simulation studies.

- source: Sun, X. P., Zhu, H. R., Kang, H. W., Bi, Y. Z., Shen, Y., & Chen, Q. Y. (2025). The impact of memory reputation-induced tax and reward allocation on spatial public goods games. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.

  findings: The study finds that introducing a reputation-driven mechanism for adjusting local synergy factors in a spatial public goods game can significantly promote cooperation. The effect is strongest when the maximum possible synergy factor is high, the minimum is low, the adjustment step size is small, and the reputation threshold is moderate. Both excessively high and low reputation thresholds hinder cooperation. The model does not include explicit punishment or reward actions; instead, reputation indirectly affects payoffs by modulating the local multiplier. The main reported outcome is the proportion of cooperators, not group efficiency or total payoff.

However, it is relevant for understanding how endogenous, reputation-driven changes to the payoff structure (via the synergy factor) can affect cooperation rates. The findings suggest that indirect, reputation-based incentives can substitute for or complement explicit punishment/reward mechanisms in promoting cooperation, but the results are reported in terms of cooperation rates, not efficiency or group payoff. For the downstream prediction task, this paper provides only adjacent, indirect evidence.

- source: Wang, M. Y., Pan, Q. H., & He, M. F. (2020). The interplay of behaviors and attitudes in public goods game considering environmental investment. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  findings: The introduction of an alliance strategy, where players pay an aid fee to help exploited allies, leads to a dramatic increase in the prevalence of cooperative behavior (C + A) in spatial PGGs. Alliance clusters are robust to defector invasion and can expand to dominate the population, especially for intermediate values of the synergy factor (r) and aid intensity (beta). The cooperation level is a non-monotonic function of both r and beta, with optimal intervals for each. Average payoffs per strategy are reported, showing that alliance players eventually outperform defectors and cooperators. However, the paper does not report group efficiency or total welfare as a function of the alliance mechanism.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that mechanisms analogous to mutual aid or alliance (distinct from punishment) can promote cooperation and increase the prevalence of cooperative strategies in spatial PGGs. However, because the model does not include a punishment or sanctioning mechanism, and does not report group efficiency or total welfare, it is only adjacent to the prediction task of estimating efficiency effects of punishment. The findings suggest that payoff redistribution among cooperators can be highly effective in suppressing defection, but the results cannot be directly transferred to settings with explicit punishment. The paper is most useful for understanding the effects of mutual aid or alliance-type mechanisms, not punishment.

  findings: The study finds that in the OPGG, increasing the complexity of behavioral strategies (i.e., the ability to condition actions on more finely-grained reputational information) increases the proportion of cooperative actions, especially under social norms that assign different reputations to defectors and loners. However, this effect plateaus after a certain level of complexity. Cooperation is higher in smaller groups, and the benefit of strategy complexity is most pronounced in these settings. The model does not include punishment or reward mechanisms, and does not report efficiency or group payoff as outcomes. The results suggest that, in the absence of punishment, reputation-based mechanisms combined with complex behavioral strategies can sustain cooperation, but the findings are limited to behavioral outcomes rather than efficiency or welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include punishment or report efficiency or payoff outcomes. Its findings are relevant for understanding how reputation systems and behavioral strategy complexity affect cooperation rates in OPGG settings without punishment. For the downstream prediction task, this paper suggests that, in the absence of punishment, increasing behavioral strategy complexity and using social norms that distinguish defectors from loners can increase cooperation rates, especially in small groups. However, it does not inform how punishment would affect efficiency or payoff, nor does it provide control or treatment efficiency values.

- source: Liu, Q., Xu, Z. J., & Zhang, L. Z. (2018). Evolutionary Public Goods Game on Evolving Random Networks. *Journal of the Korean Physical Society*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that allowing informed cooperators to rewire their network connections at a cost can substantially increase the prevalence of cooperation in public goods games on random networks. Higher rates of network rewiring (by informed cooperators) lower the threshold for cooperation to emerge, especially under adverse conditions (low synergy factor). The system exhibits distinct phases (full cooperation, full defection, coexistence, and segregated sub-networks), and the transition between these phases depends on the synergy factor and rewiring rate. However, the paper does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, and does not include explicit punishment or sanctioning mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is not directly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not implement or analyze punishment or sanctioning mechanisms. Instead, it studies the effect of costly information and network rewiring on cooperation rates. While the findings suggest that network adaptation can promote cooperation, they do not provide evidence about efficiency or payoff outcomes, nor about the effect of punishment. Thus, this paper should not be used as direct evidence for the prediction task focused on punishment and efficiency, though it may be contextually relevant for understanding alternative cooperation-promoting mechanisms.

Reputation-based partner selection (interpreted as costless exclusion of defectors) is more effective at sustaining cooperation than degree-based selection. The model does not include explicit costly punishment or reward mechanisms. The main reported outcome is the stationary fraction of cooperators, not group efficiency or total payoff. Analytical calculations show that larger group size increases the payoff advantage of cooperators over defectors, but these are not translated into reported efficiency outcomes.

It is relevant for understanding how group size and partner selection (especially reputation-based exclusion) affect cooperation rates, but not efficiency or payoff. The reputation-based exclusion mechanism is a form of costless, indirect punishment, which differs from the costly punishment typically studied in experimental PGGs. Therefore, the findings should not be directly transferred to predictions about the efficiency effects of enabling costly punishment. The paper may be useful for understanding the role of group size and partner selection in cooperation, but not for quantitative prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment.

- source: Morsky, B., & Akçay, E. (2021). False beliefs can bootstrap cooperative communities through social norms. *Evolutionary Human Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that inaccurate, overly optimistic beliefs about the level of cooperation can bootstrap and sustain high cooperation in communities governed by conditional cooperation norms, even in anonymous settings. The model shows that the dynamics of joining, learning, and leaving the community can create bistability, abrupt transitions, or cycles in cooperation rates and community size. High cooperation can be achieved and maintained if naive (optimistic) individuals are present in sufficient numbers, and if learning the true (lower) level of cooperation is slow or if disillusioned individuals can easily leave. The model does not include explicit punishment or reward, but discusses how mechanisms that increase transparency or allow for punishment may interact with these dynamics. The findings are relevant for understanding how social norms and belief management can foster cooperation, but do not provide direct evidence on efficiency or payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical insight into how belief management and social norm dynamics can affect cooperation rates in public-goods-game-like environments, especially in the absence of explicit punishment or reward. It suggests that mechanisms which slow learning about true cooperation levels or allow for easy exit can paradoxically increase cooperation by maintaining a pool of optimistic, conditionally cooperative individuals. However, because the model does not include punishment, reward, or explicit efficiency/payoff outcomes, it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency. The discussion of punishment is conceptual and highlights possible trade-offs between transparency (which may reduce cooperation by revealing low compliance) and incentive mechanisms (which may increase or decrease cooperation depending on beliefs). For the downstream prediction task, this paper is most useful for understanding the role of belief dynamics and social norms as moderators, but not for quantitative prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment.

- source: Wang, W. X., Lai, Y. C., & Armbruster, D. (2011). Cascading failures and the emergence of cooperation in evolutionary-game based models of social and economical networks. *Chaos*.

  findings: The study finds that in evolutionary games on networks (including PGG), introducing a survival threshold (death/bankruptcy) leads to catastrophic cascades when defection is present. After such cascades, only cooperators survive, regardless of network topology or game type. Defectors may gain higher payoffs temporarily but are ultimately eliminated. The model does not include explicit punishment or reward mechanisms. The main outcomes are the fraction of dead agents, the survival of cooperators, and the conditions for extinction or survival, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It does not manipulate or analyze punishment, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff. The model's 'sanction' is the endogenous removal of agents with low payoffs, not a punishment technology as in standard PGG experiments. Therefore, this paper should not be used as direct evidence for the downstream prediction task regarding punishment effects on efficiency. It may provide some adjacent theoretical context about the consequences of defection and the importance of cooperation for system survival, but not about the efficiency impact of punishment interventions.

J., Tjikundi, K., & Ali, W. (2025). Public Goods Games in Disease Evolution and Spread. *Dynamic Games and Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper synthesizes theoretical and modeling work on PGGs in disease and cancer, highlighting how cooperation can be sustained or undermined depending on game parameters, network structure, and the presence of mechanisms like social norms or sanctions. It reviews how public health interventions (e.g., vaccination, mask mandates) can be conceptualized as public goods dilemmas, and how individual decision-making, social structure, and perceived costs/benefits shape compliance. In cancer, it discusses how cooperation among cancer cells can be modeled as a PGG, with implications for treatment strategies. However, the paper does not provide quantitative findings on efficiency or group payoff, nor does it systematically analyze the effects of punishment or sanctions on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides conceptual background on how PGGs are used to model cooperation in health-related contexts and discusses mechanisms (including sanctions and incentives) that can promote cooperation. However, it does not provide direct evidence or quantitative results on the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in PGGs. For the downstream prediction task, this paper may inform the qualitative understanding of how design dimensions (e.g., network structure, cost-benefit ratios, threshold effects) can influence cooperation, but it does not supply data or direct evidence to calibrate predictions of efficiency with or without punishment. Its discussion of sanctions is high-level and not tied to specific game parameters or outcomes.

- source: Panchanathan, K., & Boyd, R. (2004). Indirect reciprocity can stabilize cooperation without the second-order free rider problem. *Nature*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that linking collective action (public goods contribution) to indirect reciprocity (mutual aid based on reputation) can stabilize cooperation through exclusion of non-contributors from future aid. This mechanism avoids the second-order free rider problem because punishers (Shunners) do not incur a direct cost; instead, they withhold aid from defectors, which is socially sanctioned. The model identifies conditions under which this strategy is evolutionarily stable and shows that both full cooperation (Shunner) and full defection (Defector) can be stable equilibria, depending on initial frequencies and assortment. The model also shows that strategies linking collective action and indirect reciprocity cannot easily invade populations practicing only indirect reciprocity. While the model discusses group payoff and welfare in the context of equilibrium selection, it does not report explicit efficiency or payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that indirect punishment via exclusion (rather than direct costly punishment) can stabilize cooperation in public goods games, especially when reputation is visible and linked across domains. It suggests that such mechanisms can lead to higher group payoff and welfare, and that equilibrium selection processes (e.g., group selection, random drift) may favor these strategies. However, the paper does not provide direct quantitative predictions about efficiency or the effect of enabling punishment in a standard PGG. Its relevance is strongest for settings where punishment is implemented as exclusion from future benefits rather than direct payoff reduction. The model's insights are most applicable to predicting when and why punishment-like mechanisms (especially exclusion) might be effective, but less so for precise efficiency prediction in standard experimental PGGs with direct costly punishment.

- source: Ding, R., Wang, X. J., Liu, Y., Zhao, J. H., & Gu, C. L. (2023). Evolutionary games with environmental feedbacks under an external incentive mechanism. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper finds that external incentives (subsidies/carbon credits) can promote cooperation and improve environmental quality in a spatial public goods game with environmental feedback, but the effect is not always positive. Higher subsidies increase the competitiveness of cooperators (both those who reinvest in the public good and those who use credits for personal gain), but can also create conditions where defectors survive by exploiting improved environments, leading to coexistence or even dominance of defectors. The use of incentives to promote cooperation is thus sensitive to the balance between environmental repair and degradation rates, the structure of incentives, and the spatial dynamics of strategy clusters. The model does not include punishment or sanctions in the standard sense, but the 'second-order social dilemma' (where some cooperators act as free-riders on others' altruism) is analogous to the problem of sustaining costly punishment. The main outcomes are cooperation rates and environmental quality, with payoffs determined by these variables.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect but informative evidence for predicting the effect of external incentives (akin to rewards) on cooperation and group welfare in spatial public goods games with environmental feedback. It suggests that the effect of such mechanisms on efficiency or payoff is highly context-dependent: increasing the incentive can improve cooperation and welfare in some parameter regions, but can also reduce them in others due to complex spatial and strategic dynamics. The findings caution against assuming monotonic positive effects of incentives and highlight the importance of considering environmental feedbacks and the possibility of second-order free-riding. However, the model does not include punishment or sanctions as defined in the prediction task, so its direct applicability to punishment effects is limited. The results are most relevant for reward-based or subsidy-based interventions in spatial, environmentally-coupled PGGs.

- source: Li, Y., Liu, X. S., Claussen, J. C., & Guo, W. L. (2015). Evolutionary dynamics for persistent cooperation in structured populations. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that persistent cooperators can invade and dominate structured populations (lattices) under more relaxed conditions than in well-mixed populations. The presence of persistent cooperators suppresses defectors and can lead to the dominance or coexistence of cooperative strategies, depending on the parameters (fraction of shared benefit, second cost, synergy factor). The evolutionary time to equilibrium is shorter in structured populations. The model shows that spatial structure facilitates the spread of persistent cooperation, and that the parameter region for cooperative dominance is larger than in well-mixed populations. However, the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, focusing instead on strategy frequencies and fixation probabilities.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that mechanisms analogous to costly punishment (here, persistent cooperation) can suppress defection and promote cooperation in structured populations, especially when the cost of retrieving one's own contribution is not too high and the fraction of shared benefit is not too large. However, because the model is not a standard punishment PGG and does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. The findings suggest that spatial structure and the ability to pay a cost to counteract defectors can expand the parameter space where cooperation is stable, but the lack of payoff-based outcomes limits its direct applicability to efficiency prediction tasks.

- source: Quan, J., Zheng, J. J., Wang, X. J., & Yang, X. K. (2019). Benefits of asynchronous exclusion for the evolution of cooperation in stochastic evolutionary optional public goods games. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that asynchronous exclusion (where excluders act sequentially and avoid redundant costs) is more effective at promoting cooperation than synchronous exclusion (where all excluders act simultaneously and pay redundant costs). This effect is strongest when the probability of exclusion success is high and the public goods multiplier is low (well-mixed) or intermediate (structured). The main reported outcomes are the probability of the system reaching a cooperative state and the frequency of different strategies at equilibrium. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, so its findings are only indirectly relevant to predicting efficiency effects of punishment-like mechanisms in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the effectiveness of exclusion (a sanctioning mechanism) in promoting cooperation in optional public goods games. While it does not report efficiency or group payoff, the increased probability of reaching cooperative states under asynchronous exclusion suggests that, under some parameter regimes, more effective or less costly sanctioning can increase cooperation rates. However, because efficiency is not measured, and because exclusion differs from standard punishment (it removes defectors from benefit sharing rather than imposing a direct cost), the findings should be used with caution for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in standard PGGs. The results are most relevant for settings where exclusion is the main sanction and where the cost structure of sanctioning can be varied.
