  findings: The study finds that allowing cooperators to rewire their links away from defectors (link rewiring) in a CPR game can prevent the collapse of cooperation and resource depletion, especially in sparsely connected networks and when resource inflow is high. The model uses a costless social ostracism mechanism (reputation-based exclusion) rather than costly punishment. The main reported outcomes are the fraction of cooperators and resource abundance, not group efficiency or total payoff. The results suggest that adaptive network structure (partner switching) can be a powerful mechanism for sustaining cooperation, but the effects depend on network density and ecological parameters.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of costly punishment on efficiency in public goods or CPR games. Instead, it analyzes a costless ostracism/reputation mechanism and the impact of adaptive network rewiring. While the findings are relevant for understanding how social structure and reputation can support cooperation, they do not inform predictions about the efficiency effects of enabling costly punishment. The model and results are adjacent to the prediction task, offering indirect insight into the role of network adaptation and costless sanctions, but not the direct impact of costly punishment on efficiency.

- source: Pedroso, M. (2021). Blind Cooperation: The Evolution of Redundancy via Ignorance. *British Journal for the Philosophy of Science*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that redundancy in group cooperation can evolve without targeted punishment or coercion if individuals are ignorant of others' strategies and/or face harsh environments. In a threshold public goods game, when players do not know what others will do, they cooperate with an intermediate probability, leading to more cooperators than strictly necessary (redundancy). Harsher environments (higher group-level cost of failure) increase the probability of cooperation and thus redundancy. In contrast, if individuals are well-informed about others' strategies, redundancy does not evolve without punishment. The paper does not provide empirical or simulated data on efficiency or group payoff, but discusses these concepts in relation to the model.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical context for how information availability and environmental harshness affect cooperation and redundancy in threshold public goods games. It suggests that punishment is not strictly necessary for redundancy or intermediate cooperation rates if players are ignorant of others' actions. However, it does not provide direct evidence or predictions about efficiency or group payoff with or without punishment, nor does it manipulate or analyze punishment parameters. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is most useful for understanding the role of information and environmental context as moderators, but does not provide quantitative or empirical evidence on the efficiency effects of punishment.

- source: Chen, Q., Chen, T., & Wang, Y. J. (2016). How the expanded crowd-funding mechanism of some southern rural areas in China affects cooperative behaviors in threshold public goods game. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that both publishing the list of cooperators and the presence of lobbyists can significantly increase the number of cooperators in a threshold public goods game, especially when the synergy factor is not too high. The effectiveness of these mechanisms depends on the population structure (proportion of unconditional cooperators vs. defectors), the synergy factor, and emotional influence parameters. When the synergy factor is high, publishing the list is always beneficial and the role of lobbyists becomes negligible. When the synergy factor is low, the decision to publish the list should consider the relative numbers of cooperators and defectors and the strength of emotional inhibition. The paper does not report on efficiency or group payoff, so its findings are only indirectly relevant to predicting efficiency outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about how social recognition (publishing the list) and persuasive agents (lobbyists) can increase cooperation rates in threshold public goods games, especially under certain parameter regimes. However, because it does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment or similar mechanisms on efficiency. The findings may be useful for understanding the behavioral pathways by which social mechanisms affect cooperation, but should be used with caution for efficiency prediction tasks.

- source: Du, J. M., Wu, B., & Wang, L. (2014). Climate collective risk dilemma with feedback of real-time temperatures. *Epl*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The model demonstrates that coupling real-time environmental feedback (temperature) to payoffs in a threshold public goods game can promote and stabilize cooperation. When the public good threshold is not met, temperature rises, which reduces all players' payoffs (environmental punishment). This feedback leads to cyclical dynamics where cooperation rebounds as payoffs fall, stabilizing temperature near a reference level. The model outperforms versions without feedback in both temperature control and cooperation rates. However, the model does not directly report efficiency or group payoff as an outcome, and the 'punishment' is environmental rather than peer-based.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is only indirectly relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. The 'punishment' mechanism is environmental (temperature rise reducing payoffs for all), not a peer or institutional punishment option. The model does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, but shows that environmental feedback can stabilize cooperation and temperature. The findings suggest that automatic, group-level sanctions (environmental feedback) can promote cooperation, but the results may not transfer directly to settings with explicit punishment options. Use this paper for contextual or mechanistic insight, not for direct quantitative prediction.

- source: Chen, X. J., Szolnoki, A., & Perc, M. (2012). Risk-driven migration and the collective-risk social dilemma. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The study shows that risk-driven migration, where players move in response to the risk of collective failure, can substantially increase the fraction of cooperators in a collective-risk social dilemma. Cooperation is promoted most effectively at intermediate population densities and when the collective target is high and the risk function is not too flat. The mechanism outperforms random migration, which can actually inhibit cooperation. The results highlight the importance of spatial structure and self-organized mobility for sustaining cooperation, but do not provide direct evidence on efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is relevant for understanding how migration and risk of collective loss affect cooperation in public-goods-like environments, but it does not directly address the effect of punishment or sanctions on efficiency or group payoff. The model does not include explicit costly punishment or reward mechanisms; instead, it uses a collective-risk loss as a sanction for group underperformance. Therefore, while the findings may inform predictions about the role of endogenous migration and risk in promoting cooperation, they do not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency as required by the downstream prediction task. Use this paper for contextual or mechanistic insight, not for quantitative prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment.

- source: Hauert, C. (2006). Spatial effects in social dilemmas. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that spatial structure can both promote and inhibit cooperation in social dilemmas, depending on the type of game (prisoner's dilemma, snowdrift, by-product mutualism, coordination) and the microscopic update rule. In strict prisoner's dilemma or public goods settings, spatial structure allows cooperators to survive by forming clusters, but the parameter range for this effect is limited. In snowdrift-type games, spatial structure can actually reduce cooperation. The effect of spatial structure is highly sensitive to the update rule: the death-birth process is most favorable to cooperation, while the replicator rule can lead to reduced cooperation in some parameter regions. The main outcome is the equilibrium fraction of cooperators; efficiency or group payoff is not directly analyzed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. However, it is informative about how spatial structure, group size, cost-benefit ratios, and update rules affect the equilibrium level of cooperation in group social dilemmas. If the prediction task involves spatial structure or local interactions, the findings suggest that such structure can have complex, sometimes counterintuitive effects on cooperation, which may indirectly affect efficiency. The absence of punishment as a mechanism means the paper cannot directly inform predictions about the efficiency impact of enabling punishment. The main value is in understanding baseline cooperation dynamics in spatially structured games.

  findings: The paper finds that cooperation in collective-risk social dilemma games is strongly affected by perceived risk, inequality, uncertainty about thresholds, intergenerational discounting, and the possibility of reputation-based rewards or punishments. High perceived risk, communication (pledges), intermediate targets, and reputation mechanisms can increase cooperation (i.e., the proportion of groups that avert catastrophe). Inequality and uncertainty about the threshold for catastrophe reduce cooperation. The paper discusses the need for credible enforcement mechanisms (punishment) in real-world climate agreements but does not review experiments that directly implement or vary punishment in the game. The main findings are about behavioral cooperation, not efficiency or group payoff.

It is useful for understanding moderators of cooperation and the conceptual role of punishment, but cannot be used to quantitatively predict the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency in a public goods game. For the prediction task, this paper suggests that credible punishment mechanisms are likely to be important, especially under uncertainty, but does not provide data or effect sizes.

  findings: The paper argues that cooperation in resource dilemmas (including public goods and commons dilemmas) is shaped by individual value orientation (pro-social vs egoistic), situational constraints (such as group identification, communication, and social norms), and the degree and type of uncertainty (social and environmental). It proposes that pro-social individuals are more likely to cooperate regardless of uncertainty, while egoistic individuals are more likely to defect, especially under uncertainty. Situational factors (including the possibility of sanctions or rewards) can constrain egoism and promote cooperation. The paper reviews prior findings that rewards increase cooperation and sanctions decrease defection, but notes that the costs of such interventions may outweigh collective gains and that effects may not persist when interventions are removed. The analysis is theoretical and hypothesis-generating, not empirical.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides conceptual background on the psychological mechanisms that may moderate the effect of punishment, reward, and uncertainty on cooperation in public-goods-like environments. It suggests that punishment and reward can increase cooperation, but may not always improve efficiency due to their costs, and that their effects may not persist. The paper does not provide quantitative or empirical evidence for predicting efficiency outcomes from game design dimensions. Its main value for prediction is in highlighting the importance of situational constraints (including punishment/reward), group identification, communication, and uncertainty, and in warning that interventions may have costs that offset their benefits. However, it does not provide direct evidence for the efficiency impact of punishment in public goods games.

- source: Chen, W., Zhu, Q. L., & Wu, T. (2023). Unfairness promotes the evolution of cooperation. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that allowing individuals to reject unfair resource allocations (interpreted as a form of punishment) leads to higher levels of cooperation, especially when moderate unfairness is present. Both extreme fairness and extreme unfairness are suboptimal for cooperation. The mechanism is explained by cyclic dominance among strategy types. In continuous strategy settings, cooperators tend to act fairer than defectors, and clusters of cooperators can resist exploitation. The model is theoretical and simulation-based, and does not report group efficiency or total payoff, but rather focuses on the fraction of cooperators and the evolution of fairness/unfairness strategies.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that the ability to reject unfair offers (a form of costly punishment) can promote cooperation in public-goods-like games, especially when resource allocation is neither perfectly fair nor extremely unfair. However, because the main outcomes are cooperation rates and not efficiency or group payoff, the findings are only adjacent to the prediction task of estimating efficiency changes due to punishment. The model is also theoretical and does not manipulate or report most of the game design dimensions used in the prediction task. Thus, while the paper supports the general idea that punishment for unfairness can promote cooperation, it does not provide direct quantitative guidance for predicting efficiency outcomes in empirical PGG experiments.

- source: 
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that cognitive mechanisms such as intention recognition, commitment, apology, and forgiveness can reinforce the emergence and stability of cooperation in populations, as modeled by evolutionary game theory. In PGG-like settings, strategies that restrict benefits to non-committers (RESTRICT) or avoid participation with non-committers (AVOID) can promote cooperation, with RESTRICT being more effective in larger groups or when the public good is highly beneficial. Arranging prior agreements (commitments) is more effective at promoting cooperation than costly punishment after the fact, and can reduce the need for excessive punishment. Apology and forgiveness mechanisms, especially when apology is sincere and costly, further support cooperation, particularly in repeated interactions. The chapter hypothesizes that guilt, as an internalized mechanism, would further enhance cooperation by increasing the likelihood and sincerity of apology, and by reducing defection.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and simulation-based insights into how various cognitive and social mechanisms (commitment, apology, forgiveness, restriction of benefits) can promote cooperation in PGG-like environments, sometimes more effectively than punishment. However, it does not provide direct evidence or quantitative predictions about efficiency or group payoff, nor does it report experimental or empirical data on the effect of punishment on efficiency. For the downstream prediction task, the paper suggests that mechanisms which pre-commit or restrict benefits to cooperators may outperform simple punishment in promoting cooperation, especially as group size or public good benefit increases. However, the lack of explicit efficiency or payoff outcomes limits its direct applicability for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment in PGGs.

It highlights that in repeated public goods games, cooperation can be stabilized by making contributions observable and by enabling accountability through punishment or gossip. The review notes that punishment can promote cooperation, but also that gossip may be as effective as punishment and more efficient due to lower costs. The paper discusses the challenges of scaling cooperation to large groups and complex real-world dilemmas, emphasizing the importance of observability, local interactions, and psychological proximity. However, it does not provide quantitative predictions or direct evidence on the efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This review is useful for understanding the mechanisms by which punishment, reputation, and observability can promote cooperation in public goods games and related social dilemmas. It provides conceptual support for the idea that punishment can increase cooperation, but also cautions that gossip and reputation mechanisms may be equally or more efficient due to lower costs. The review does not provide direct empirical evidence or quantitative estimates of efficiency effects, so it should be used for contextual and mechanistic understanding rather than for parameterizing predictive models of efficiency. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the inclusion of observability, reputation, and punishment as important moderators, but does not allow for direct estimation of their effects on efficiency.

- source: Salomonsson, M. (2010). Group selection: The quest for social preferences. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that group selection can, under certain conditions, support the evolution of cooperation and altruism in public-goods-like and Prisoner's Dilemma games. Three main approaches are discussed: fixation (requiring isolated pure groups), assortative group formation (e.g., via conformity or signalling), and reproductive externalities (where group-level payoffs affect evolutionary success). The paper notes that group selection is more likely to support cooperation when migration is low, groups are small, or positive externalities can be internalized. It also reviews how cultural mechanisms (like conformity) and group competition can foster social preferences. However, the paper does not provide direct evidence or predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff, and only discusses punishment as a possible mechanism in the context of conformity and social preferences.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical context for how group structure, migration, and mechanisms like conformity or signalling can affect the evolution of cooperation in public-goods-like games. It is useful for understanding the broader conditions under which cooperation and social preferences may arise, but it does not provide direct evidence or quantitative predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff. For the downstream prediction task, this paper suggests that group structure and the ability to internalize externalities are important moderators, but it does not inform the specific effect of punishment interventions in experimental public goods games.

- source: Aktipis, A. (2016). Principles of cooperation across systems: from human sharing to multicellularity and cancer. *Evolutionary Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count
  findings: The paper identifies three general principles that facilitate cooperation: (1) the ability to 'Walk Away' from uncooperative partners, (2) need-based resource transfers, and (3) the necessity of increasingly sophisticated cheater suppression mechanisms as cooperation scales up. It reviews agent-based models (from prior work) showing that conditional movement (Walk Away) can stabilize cooperation in both dyadic and group contexts, including public goods game-like environments. The paper discusses how positive assortment (cooperators interacting more with each other) is key to the evolution of cooperation. It also highlights the challenges of cheater suppression in large-scale systems and draws analogies between human societies, multicellular organisms, and social insects. However, it does not provide new empirical or experimental results on efficiency or group payoff, nor does it directly analyze the effects of punishment mechanisms in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides conceptual and theoretical background on mechanisms that support cooperation, such as partner choice (Walk Away) and cheater suppression, which are relevant to understanding the effects of punishment in public goods games. However, it does not provide direct empirical or quantitative evidence on how punishment affects efficiency or group payoff in public goods games or their variants. The discussion of cheater suppression includes punishment as one possible mechanism but is not specific or detailed enough to inform quantitative prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment. The most relevant insight is that as cooperative systems scale up, more sophisticated and costly cheater suppression mechanisms (including punishment) are required, but the paper does not specify the efficiency consequences of these mechanisms in controlled game environments.

- source: Centola, D. (2013). A Simple Model of Stability in Critical Mass Dynamics. *Journal of Statistical Physics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count
  findings: The paper finds that weakly self-reinforcing collective action systems (where incentives to participate peak before full participation, allowing for free riders) are more robust to perturbations and have greater long-term stability than strongly self-reinforcing systems (where incentives increase monotonically with participation and free riding is eliminated). Strongly self-reinforcing systems can achieve higher participation but are more vulnerable to collapse from stochastic shocks. The presence of free riders in weakly self-reinforcing systems provides a reserve of potential contributors that stabilizes participation when it drops. The analysis is theoretical and does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical insight into the trade-off between maximizing participation (and thus, potentially, efficiency) and ensuring long-term stability in collective action systems. While it does not directly address efficiency or payoff outcomes, it suggests that systems designed to eliminate free riding (e.g., via strong incentives or possibly punishment) may be less stable, and thus may not always achieve higher long-term group benefit. For prediction tasks, the paper implies that the relationship between punishment (or strong incentives) and efficiency may be moderated by stability concerns, but does not provide quantitative or empirical evidence for efficiency effects. The model is most relevant for understanding the dynamics of participation and the potential unintended consequences of interventions aimed at increasing cooperation.

  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The study finds that introducing heterogeneous social institutions (pool punishment, anti-social punishment, and social security/reward) in a spatial prisoner's dilemma framework can promote the evolution of pro-social groups and suppress second-order free-riding. The model shows that increasing the cost of institutional investment and the growth factor of the institutional pool generally promotes cooperation and higher average payoffs, but only up to an optimal point. The introduction of a non-wasteful model with an upper limit on individual punishment/reward further improves the efficiency of the system by reducing resource waste and allowing pro-social groups to survive under harsher social dilemmas. The network (spatial) structure enhances these effects compared to well-mixed populations. While the main outcomes are strategy proportions, the paper also analyzes average payoffs, showing that institutional punishment and reward mechanisms can increase group welfare compared to baseline scenarios without such institutions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and simulation-based evidence that the design of punishment and reward institutions—including their cost, effectiveness, and resource allocation rules—can significantly affect the efficiency (average payoff) of groups in social dilemma games. While the model is a spatial prisoner's dilemma rather than a standard public goods game, the mechanisms are closely related, especially for pool punishment and institutional reward. The findings suggest that enabling punishment (and reward) institutions, especially with non-wasteful resource allocation and optimal parameter settings, will generally increase group efficiency compared to control conditions without such institutions. The results also highlight the importance of network structure, the cost and effectiveness of punishment, and the presence of anti-social punishment as moderators. However, the lack of direct efficiency ratios and the use of a prisoner's dilemma rather than a PGG limit the direct quantitative transferability to PGG-like environments.

- source: Rubin, H. (2022). When it pays to punish in the evolution of honesty and cooperation. *Synthese*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper uses evolutionary game theory and simulations to analyze how different forms of costly punishment (punishing lying vs. punishing defection) affect the evolution of honesty and cooperation in a repeated signaling/donation game. It finds that: (1) Punishment of lying increases honesty but often reduces the prevalence of cooperation, because the costs of punishing and being punished can outweigh the benefits of increased honesty. (2) Punishment of defection (as in standard strong reciprocity models) increases cooperation and group success, consistent with prior findings in stag hunt and repeated PD games. (3) The effect of punishment on cooperation and efficiency depends on game parameters (number of rounds, benefit of donation, lie detection probability, punishment cost and effectiveness). (4) When both types of punishment are available, the results are intermediate. The paper highlights that the effect of punishment on efficiency is not universally positive and can be negative when punishment targets lying rather than defection.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides important cautionary evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in games with multi-stage cooperation (e.g., signaling plus donation). If punishment targets lying (dishonest signaling), it may increase honesty but can reduce overall cooperation and thus efficiency, especially when punishment is costly and lie detection is imperfect. If punishment targets defection (failure to cooperate), it is more likely to increase cooperation and efficiency, as in standard public goods or stag hunt models. For prediction tasks, the design dimension 'punishment target' (lying vs. defection) is critical. The paper also shows that the cost and effectiveness of punishment, the number of rounds, and the benefit of cooperation all moderate the effect. Control efficiency (without punishment) is not always a reliable predictor of treatment efficiency (with punishment), especially when punishment targets lying.

- source: Wei, X., Xu, P., Du, S. T., Yan, G. H., & Pei, H. Y. (2021). Reputational preference-based payoff punishment promotes cooperation in spatial social dilemmas. *European Physical Journal B*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that introducing a reputational preference-based payoff punishment mechanism in spatial social dilemmas (PDG and SDG) significantly increases the fraction of cooperators. At low punishment intensity, both cooperators and defectors may have low or negative average payoffs due to the cost of punishment, but as punishment intensity increases, cooperators' average payoff and fitness rise, and defectors' payoffs drop to zero. At high punishment intensity, the system can reach full cooperation with high average payoffs for cooperators. The effect is more pronounced in the snowdrift game, where coexistence of cooperators and defectors persists over a wider parameter range. The study does not directly report group-level efficiency, but the increase in average payoffs for cooperators at high punishment intensity suggests a positive effect on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical/simulation evidence that introducing a costly, reputation-based punishment mechanism in spatial social dilemmas increases cooperation rates and, at sufficient punishment intensity, increases average payoffs for cooperators, leading to higher group efficiency. The effect is robust across both prisoner's dilemma and snowdrift games, with stronger effects in the latter. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that enabling punishment (especially with high effectiveness and shared cost) will increase efficiency relative to the control (no-punishment) condition, particularly in spatially structured games. However, the results are based on simulation, not empirical data, and do not use the public goods game directly, so transfer to PGG-like environments should be cautious but is likely informative.

- source: Wubs, M., Bshary, R., & Lehmann, L. (2016). Coevolution between positive reciprocity, punishment, and partner switching in repeated interactions. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that in repeated Prisoner's Dilemma games, the evolutionary success of punishment, positive reciprocity, and partner switching depends on the number of rounds, group size, and the costs/effectiveness of punishment and switching. Punishment is favored in small and moderately sized groups, especially when punishment is efficient and its behavioral effect is long-lasting. Partner switching dominates in large groups or when the number of rounds is high, unless switching is costly. Positive reciprocity is rarely the dominant mechanism, except when punishment is less effective or more costly. The model does not directly report group efficiency or total payoff, but evolutionary success is determined by aggregate payoff, so the results indirectly inform about conditions under which punishment or other mechanisms would lead to higher average payoffs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect but informative evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in repeated Prisoner's Dilemma-like environments. It suggests that punishment is more likely to improve group outcomes (in terms of evolutionary success/payoff) in small or moderately sized groups, especially when punishment is efficient and its behavioral effect is long-lasting. In large groups or with many rounds, partner switching may be more effective. The findings are most relevant for adjacent settings to public goods games, especially repeated dyadic interactions, and should be interpreted with caution when generalizing to standard PGGs. The paper does not provide direct quantitative estimates of efficiency, but the evolutionary dominance of strategies is determined by payoff, so the results can be used to infer the likely direction of punishment's effect on efficiency under different design dimensions.

- source: Gao, S. P., & Liang, J. L. (2020). Cooperation under institutional incentives with perfect and imperfect observation. *Physics Letters A*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: Institutional punishment (and reward), funded by a tax on positive payoffs, can promote the evolutionary emergence of cooperation in a well-mixed prisoner's dilemma. The effectiveness of punishment increases with higher tax rates, greater benefit of cooperation, and stronger incentive effectiveness. There is a threshold tax rate above which cooperation is favored by natural selection. Imperfect monitoring (i.e., noise in identifying defectors) reduces the effectiveness of punishment and increases the required tax rate for cooperation to emerge. The model provides analytical results for average payoffs and fixation probabilities, but does not directly report group efficiency as a fraction of the fully cooperative outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that institutional punishment (with costs funded by a tax on payoffs) can increase the evolutionary success of cooperation, especially when monitoring is perfect, the tax rate is high, and incentives are strong. While it does not report group efficiency directly, the increase in fixation probability of cooperation and average payoffs for cooperators suggest that enabling punishment in similar game designs would increase efficiency relative to a control without punishment, particularly under high tax rates and effective monitoring. The model is adjacent to public goods games, as it uses a prisoner's dilemma structure, so predictions should be transferred with caution. The findings are most informative for settings with institutional, payoff-funded punishment and well-mixed populations.

- source: Rand, D. G., Ohtsuki, H., & Nowak, M. A. (2009). Direct reciprocity with costly punishment: Generous tit-for-tat prevails. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper shows that in repeated Prisoner's Dilemma games with costly punishment, the addition of a punishment option does not increase cooperation or group payoffs in evolutionary simulations. Even when Nash equilibrium analysis allows for cooperative equilibria using punishment, evolutionary selection in finite populations favors generous tit-for-tat strategies that do not use costly punishment. Costly punishment is rarely used, and when it is, it is associated with lower payoffs. The presence of costly punishment does not allow cooperation to evolve in parameter regions where it would not already be supported by direct reciprocity alone. These findings are robust to variations in game parameters and are supported by experimental data from Dreber et al. (2008).
  prediction_guidance: For the prediction task, this paper suggests that enabling costly punishment in a repeated two-player PD-like environment does not increase efficiency or group payoff, regardless of the punishment cost or effectiveness. Evolutionary dynamics select against strategies that use costly punishment, and the most successful strategies are those that reciprocate cooperation and defect against defectors (generous tit-for-tat), not those that punish. Thus, when predicting the effect of enabling punishment in similar repeated games, one should not expect an increase in efficiency or group payoff, and may even expect a negative association between punishment use and payoff. However, the results are specific to repeated two-player PDs and may not generalize to larger group public goods games.

- source: Hu, B., & Du, Y. X. (2023). Integrated analysis of employee cooperation and conflict behaviors in the context of digital technology. *Simulation-transactions of the Society for Modeling and Simulation International*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that the presence and magnitude of punishment (penalty for conflict) is a key determinant of stable cooperation in a group setting modeled as an evolutionary game. If the penalty for conflict exceeds half the cost of cooperation, cooperation becomes the evolutionary stable strategy, and the group achieves higher average perceived payoffs. If the penalty is lower, the group exhibits unstable, turbulent behavior with frequent reversals between cooperation and conflict, and the average perceived payoff from cooperation is often lower than from conflict. The model also shows that increasing the proportion of cooperators and the perceived benefit of cooperation stabilizes group behavior. The findings are based on theoretical analysis and simulation, not on real-world or laboratory data.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that increasing the penalty for non-cooperation (punishment) above a critical threshold (half the cost of cooperation) can shift group behavior from unstable, low-payoff states to stable, high-cooperation, higher-payoff states. For prediction tasks, the presence and magnitude of punishment cost and effectiveness are critical moderators. However, the outcomes are modeled as perceived payoffs and behavioral states, not as direct group efficiency ratios. The findings are most relevant for settings where punishment is a salient, well-understood, and enforceable mechanism, and less so for empirical prediction of actual group efficiency in experimental PGGs.

- source: Patrzyk, P. M., & Takác, M. (2017). Cooperation Via Intimidation: An Emergent System of Mutual Threats can Maintain Social Order. *Jasss-the Journal of Artificial Societies and Social Simulation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The study finds that in agent-based models of repeated Prisoner's Dilemma with post-game fighting (punishment), aggressive strategies (harassers) become evolutionarily stable and promote high rates of mutual cooperation (CC) in small groups with strong deterrence. The mechanism is that agents develop reputations for toughness, deterring defection and reducing the need for actual fighting. In large groups, anonymity undermines this effect, and cooperation rates fall. When third-party (moralized) punishment is allowed, coalitions can enforce cooperation, but only under certain conditions (small groups, justified defection rules). The models suggest that punishment can increase group-level cooperation and, by proxy, efficiency, but only under specific structural conditions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect but informative evidence that enabling punishment in repeated PD-like environments can increase cooperation rates (and thus efficiency) compared to control, especially in small groups with strong deterrence and information spread. The effect is weaker or absent in large, anonymous groups. The findings are most relevant for settings where punishment is reputation-based and where agents can observe and remember others' aggressive acts. The results are not from public goods games but from repeated PDs with post-game punishment, so transfer to PGGs should be cautious but is likely informative for adjacent settings. The paper does not report efficiency as a direct ratio of group payoff to maximum, but CC rates can be used as a proxy for high group welfare.

- source: Han, T. A., & Lenaerts, T. (2016). A synergy of costly punishment and commitment in cooperation dilemmas. *Adaptive Behavior*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that a strategy combining prior commitment and costly punishment (CPP) can achieve higher frequencies of cooperation than either mechanism alone, across a wide range of parameter values. The improvement is most significant when the cost of arranging commitments is high and the punishment impact is above a threshold. The model shows that commitment reduces the effect-to-cost ratio required for punishment to be effective, and punishment helps deal with free-riders who exploit commitment. The results are robust across different cost and punishment parameters, but are derived for the two-player, one-shot Prisoner's Dilemma, not for public goods games or group interactions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that combining commitment and punishment mechanisms can outperform either alone in promoting cooperation, especially when commitment is costly and punishment is sufficiently effective. However, the model is based on the two-player Prisoner's Dilemma, not a public goods game or group setting. The outcomes are reported as frequencies of strategies and risk-dominance, not as group efficiency or total payoff. Thus, while the findings suggest that punishment (especially when combined with commitment) can improve cooperation and, by implication, group outcomes, the results cannot be directly mapped to efficiency predictions in public goods games. The paper is most useful for understanding the interaction of punishment and commitment mechanisms and their parameter dependencies, but less directly informative for quantitative efficiency prediction in PGG-like environments.

- source: Zhu, Y. Y., Zhang, J. L., Sun, Q. L., & Chen, Z. Q. (2017). Evolutionary dynamics of strategies for threshold snowdrift games on complex networks. *Knowledge-based Systems*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that introducing punishment in threshold snowdrift games increases the evolutionary advantage of cooperation over defection, especially when punishment cost is low and the threshold is moderate. Larger thresholds generally promote cooperation, but too high or too low thresholds can be detrimental. Lower punishment cost (to the punisher) and higher punishment impact (on the defector) both facilitate cooperation. These effects are robust across well-mixed and networked populations, including single-layer and double-layer (interconnected) networks. However, the main reported outcomes are cooperation rates, not group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that punishment can increase cooperation rates in threshold public-goods-like games, especially when punishment is effective and not too costly. However, it does not report group efficiency or total payoff, so its direct value for predicting efficiency outcomes is limited. Theoretical payoff difference (P_c - P_d) is analyzed, but simulation results focus on cooperation rates. The findings suggest that punishment is more effective when the cost to punishers is low and the penalty to defectors is high, and that network structure and threshold size moderate these effects. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the idea that punishment can improve cooperation under certain parameter regimes, but does not provide direct quantitative evidence on efficiency.

- source: Cui, P. B., & Wu, Z. X. (2014). Selfish punishment with avoiding mechanism can alleviate both first-order and second-order social dilemma. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_punishment_id
  findings: The study finds that introducing selfish punishment with an avoiding mechanism into a spatial prisoner's dilemma game can alleviate both first-order and second-order social dilemmas, depending on the punishment cost, fine, and identification probability. The presence of selfish punishers allows for the stable coexistence of cooperators and punishers, and under certain parameter regimes, reduces the prevalence of defectors and second-order free riders. Theoretical predictions using extended pair approximation and interaction web analysis match well with simulation results. The main reported outcomes are the frequencies of each strategy type, not group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the idea that punishment (especially when including mechanisms for selfish punishment and avoidance) can promote cooperation and reduce free riding in spatially structured social dilemmas. However, because the model is a prisoner's dilemma (not a public goods game) and the main outcomes are strategy frequencies rather than group efficiency or total payoff, the findings are only adjacent to the prediction task. The results suggest that the effectiveness of punishment depends on the cost/fine ratio and the ability to identify defectors, but do not provide direct quantitative predictions for efficiency outcomes in public goods games. The paper is most useful for understanding mechanisms and qualitative moderators of punishment effects, rather than for direct efficiency prediction.

- source: Roos, P., Gelfand, M., Nau, D., & Carr, R. (2014). High strength-of-ties and low mobility enable the evolution of third-party punishment. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper finds that responsible third-party punishment (3PP) can evolve and sustain cooperation in structured populations when social-structural constraints are high (i.e., high strength-of-ties and low mobility). Under these conditions, clusters of responsible punishers can induce cooperation in their local neighborhoods, leading to higher local pay-offs and the spread of both cooperation and punishment. In contrast, in well-mixed or highly mobile populations, responsible 3PP does not evolve, and cooperation fails to emerge. The model shows that 3PP is pivotal for the emergence of cooperation and increased pay-off, as neither population structure alone nor direct punishment alone suffice. The findings are robust to variations in network structure and to the presence of antisocial punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the effectiveness of punishment in promoting cooperation and increasing pay-off (and thus efficiency) depends critically on the social structure of the population. Specifically, punishment is more likely to increase efficiency in games with high strength-of-ties (low average degree, i.e., players interact repeatedly with a small set of neighbors) and low mobility (players do not frequently change partners). In well-mixed or highly mobile settings, punishment is unlikely to improve efficiency. For prediction tasks, this suggests that game designs with fixed, repeated local interactions and low partner switching will see greater efficiency gains from punishment mechanisms, while designs with random matching or high mobility will not. The model is adjacent to public goods games (it uses a two-player cooperation game with third-party punishment), so the transfer to standard PGGs is not exact but likely informative.

- source: Takesue, H. (2018). Evolutionary prisoner's dilemma games on the network with punishment and opportunistic partner switching. *Epl*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that both punishment and partner switching (opportunistic rewiring of social ties) can support the evolution of cooperation in a networked prisoner's dilemma. When partner switching is infrequent, strong punishment is necessary to suppress defectors. When partner switching is frequent, cooperation dominates even with weak or no punishment. The mechanism is that defectors sever ties with punishers and preferentially connect to cooperators, which increases cooperators' opportunities to be imitated and thus proliferate. Average payoffs by strategy are reported, showing that cooperators and punishers can outperform defectors, but the main outcome is the frequency of each strategy. The results suggest that social fluidity (partner switching) can substitute for punishment in maintaining cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect but informative evidence for the prediction task. While it does not report group efficiency or total welfare directly, it shows that both punishment and partner switching can increase the prevalence of cooperation and reduce defection, and that average payoffs for cooperators and punishers can exceed those of defectors. The findings suggest that in networked games with opportunities for partner switching, the marginal benefit of adding punishment to increase efficiency may be reduced if partner switching is already frequent. The results also highlight the importance of the punishment cost, punishment effectiveness, and the fluidity of social relationships (analogous to partner switching) as moderators of punishment's effect. However, because the main outcomes are strategy frequencies and not group efficiency, the evidence is most useful for understanding mechanisms and moderators rather than for direct quantitative prediction of efficiency.

- source: Shen, C., Song, Z., Shi, L., Tanimoto, J., & Wang, Z. (2025). Exit options sustain altruistic punishment and decrease the second-order free-riders, but it is not a panacea. *Science China-information Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=unclear
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that adding an exit option to a prisoner's dilemma with altruistic punishment changes the evolutionary dynamics. In well-mixed populations, exiters replace defectors as the exit payoff increases, but altruistic punishers do not become dominant. In networked populations, exiters can support the coexistence of altruistic punishers, defectors, and exiters via cyclic dominance, but this effect is unstable and depends on the exit payoff. Sometimes, non-punishing cooperators replace altruistic punishers, and the system can exhibit bi-stability between these outcomes. The effect of exiters on cooperation and punishment is nuanced and context-dependent. The paper does not provide direct predictions or measurements of group efficiency or welfare, but the results suggest that exit options can sometimes support, but also sometimes undermine, the mechanisms that sustain cooperation and punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about how the presence and incentive level of an exit option (voluntary participation) can affect the evolutionary stability of punishment and cooperation in social dilemmas. While the model is not a standard public goods game and does not report efficiency as a ratio to the fully cooperative outcome, it suggests that exit options can destabilize defection and, under some conditions in networked populations, support the coexistence of altruistic punishment. However, the effect is highly sensitive to the exit payoff and population structure, and exiters can also undermine punishment by enabling non-punishing cooperators to dominate. For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that the presence of an exit option and its incentive level are important moderators of punishment's effect, especially in structured populations, but does not provide quantitative efficiency outcomes.

- source: Hernandez, I., Cohen, D., Gruschow, K., Nowak, A., Gelfand, M. J., & Borkowski, W. (2022). The Importance of Being Unearnest: Opportunists and the Making of Culture. *Journal of Personality and Social Psychology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that (1) more severe punishment for noncooperation reduces the amount of voluntary cooperation in a population, both in agent-based simulations and in a human experiment; (2) raising the standard for what counts as sufficient cooperation to avoid punishment also reduces voluntary cooperation; (3) the presence of opportunists is necessary for strong reciprocators to thrive, but when punishment is too severe, opportunists proliferate and strong reciprocators decline; (4) these dynamics help explain why tight or collectivist cultures with strong norms and sanctions may have lower social capital (voluntary cooperation) than expected. The main outcome is the amount of voluntary cooperation, not direct group efficiency or total payoff, but the results imply that harsher punishment can reduce overall welfare by crowding out voluntary, uncoerced cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in environments structurally similar to public goods games with punishment, increasing the severity of punishment or raising the bar for what counts as sufficient cooperation can reduce the amount of voluntary cooperation, and thus likely reduce group efficiency or welfare. The presence of punishment is necessary for sustaining cooperation, but making punishment too severe or standards too high backfires. For prediction tasks, this suggests a non-monotonic or negative effect of increasing punishment severity on efficiency, especially when punishment is harsh or standards are high. However, the paper does not report efficiency as a ratio of actual to maximum possible payoffs, so the mapping to efficiency is indirect. The findings are most relevant for predicting the effect of punishment severity and standards on voluntary cooperation and, by extension, on group efficiency in similar multi-agent, multi-round games.

- source: Nakamura, M. (2019). Rare Third-Party Punishment Promotes Cooperation in Risk-Averse Social Learning Dynamics. *Frontiers in Physics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that risk-averse individuals in evolutionary social dilemma games can achieve stable, high levels of cooperation with only rare third-party punishment. Both exogenous (authoritative) and endogenous (peer) punishment models are analyzed. Theoretical results show that the required frequency of punishment to maintain cooperation is much lower for risk-averse individuals than for risk-neutral or risk-prone individuals. Among punishment variants, punishing all defectors or punishing one defector (as a warning) are both effective in promoting cooperation, while punishing only one-sided defectors is less effective. The models predict that, under risk aversion, group payoff and cooperation rates are higher with punishment than without, and that the cost of maintaining cooperation via punishment can be minimized.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support that, in two-player social dilemma games with third-party punishment, enabling punishment (even rarely) can substantially increase cooperation and group payoff, especially when individuals are risk averse. The findings suggest that the efficiency gain from punishment is robust to low punishment frequency and that the structure of punishment (punish all vs. punish one) can moderate the effect. However, the results are derived from theoretical models, not empirical data, and the games are not public goods games but two-player dilemmas. The paper is most informative for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in environments where risk aversion is salient and punishment is rare.

- source: Matsuzawa, R., & Tanimoto, J. (2018). Sanctions triggered by jealousy help promote the cooperation in spatial prisoner's dilemma games. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=unclear
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that jealousy-motivated sanctions (where agents punish neighbors with higher payoffs, regardless of strategy) can promote cooperation in spatial prisoner's dilemma games. The effect depends on the network structure: in lattice networks, higher sanction efficiency (penalty per cost) promotes cooperation, while in scale-free networks, lower efficiency (higher cost per penalty) can be more effective. However, the paper notes that even when cooperation is achieved, the continued use of jealous sanctions (due to persistent payoff inequalities) can reduce total social payoff, potentially lowering efficiency. The main reported outcome is the cooperation fraction, not direct group efficiency or payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like environments, but the model is a spatial prisoner's dilemma, not a public goods game. The main outcome is cooperation rate, not efficiency. The findings suggest that punishment can increase cooperation, but may not always improve efficiency due to the cost of sanctions, especially when punishment is motivated by jealousy and can be directed at cooperators as well as defectors. The effect of punishment on efficiency is likely to depend on network structure and the cost-effectiveness of punishment. For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that punishment may increase cooperation but could reduce efficiency if sanctioning is costly and not well-targeted.

- source: Gao, S. P., Wu, T., Nie, S. L., & Wang, L. (2015). Promote or hinder? The role of punishment in the emergence of cooperation. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the effectiveness of punishment in promoting cooperation depends critically on the pattern of punishment and the presence of anti-social punishment. Centralized (pool) pro-social punishment is more effective than decentralized (peer) punishment at promoting cooperation. When anti-social punishment is possible and both pro- and anti-social punishments are in the same pattern, the positive effect of punishment is largely neutralized. However, if pro-social punishment is centralized and anti-social punishment is decentralized, cooperation can still be promoted. The results are derived from theoretical evolutionary game models and focus on the stationary abundance of strategies, not direct efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect but informative theoretical evidence about how punishment design (centralized vs decentralized, presence of anti-social punishment) can affect the evolutionary success of cooperation in social dilemmas. While it does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, it shows that centralized punishment mechanisms are more robust to anti-social punishment and more likely to sustain cooperation. For prediction tasks, this suggests that games with centralized punishment (pool) are more likely to see positive effects of punishment on efficiency, especially when anti-social punishment is possible. However, the results are based on evolutionary dynamics and strategy abundance, not direct efficiency measures, and the model is a prisoner's dilemma rather than a public goods game.

- source: Gao, S. P., Wu, T., & Wang, L. (2015). Emergence of parochial altruism in well-mixed populations. *Physics Letters A*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that parochial altruism (in-group cooperation, out-group punishment) is favored by selection under certain parameter regimes, especially when the benefit of cooperation is high relative to its cost, and when the effectiveness of punishment (fine imposed) exceeds its cost. Intergroup conflict (punishment) can promote the evolution of in-group cooperation, and vice versa. High mutation rates are detrimental to the evolution of parochial altruism. Larger populations and intermediate selection strengths can also favor parochial altruism. The model provides analytical conditions for when parochial altruism is favored, but does not directly report on group efficiency or total welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like environments, by showing that punishment (when effective and not too costly) can promote the evolutionary success of parochial altruism (in-group cooperation and out-group punishment). However, the model is not a standard public goods game, but rather a two-group model with prisoner's dilemma for in-group and a punishment game for out-group interactions. The main outcomes are evolutionary frequencies of strategies, not group efficiency or total payoff. The results suggest that punishment can promote cooperation under certain conditions, but do not provide direct quantitative predictions for efficiency changes in standard PGGs. The findings are most relevant for understanding the parameter regimes where punishment is likely to support cooperative strategies, which may inform priors about efficiency effects in related experimental designs.

- source: CLUTTONBROCK, T. H., & PARKER, G. A. (1995). PUNISHMENT IN ANIMAL SOCIETIES. *Nature*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: Theoretical models show that punishment can be stable and effective in animal societies, especially when dominants can punish subordinates at low cost and with high impact. Punishment can enforce cooperation, deter cheating, and maintain social order. The models identify conditions under which punishment is likely to evolve, such as strong asymmetries in power or fighting ability. Empirical examples illustrate that punishment is widespread in animal societies and can modify behavior, but the paper does not report quantitative effects on group efficiency or welfare. The findings suggest that punishment can increase the fitness of punishers by reducing the frequency of damaging actions by others, but do not directly address group-level efficiency as in PGGs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides conceptual and theoretical support for the idea that punishment can enforce cooperation and deter selfishness, which is relevant to predicting the effects of punishment in PGG-like games. However, the models are dyadic, focus on fitness rather than explicit group payoff, and do not provide quantitative estimates of efficiency changes. The paper suggests that punishment is more likely to be effective when the cost to the punisher is low and the impact on the punished is high, and when there are clear asymmetries in power. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the plausibility of positive effects of punishment on cooperation and (by analogy) efficiency, but does not provide direct empirical or quantitative evidence for use in predictive modeling.

- source: Rumble, A. C., Willcox, K., Imada, H., & Yansen, D. (2022). Beyond Reciprocity: Forgiveness, Generosity, and Punishment in Continuing Dyadic Interactions. *Journal of Theoretical Social Psychology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper synthesizes evidence from simulation and behavioral studies of dyadic repeated games (mainly Prisoner's Dilemma and its continuous variants) to argue that punishment, while sometimes increasing cooperation, generally reduces average payoffs and is less effective than generous or forgiving strategies for maintaining cooperation and maximizing outcomes. Costly punishment is found to lower group welfare and does not restore cooperation as effectively as non-punitive responses. The review highlights that punishment also carries social and emotional costs, making it less desirable in ongoing dyadic relationships. Generosity and forgiveness, by contrast, are more likely to sustain cooperation and higher payoffs, especially when noise or uncertainty is present.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and literature-based evidence that, in dyadic repeated games (especially noisy or continuous-choice environments), enabling punishment is likely to reduce efficiency or group payoff compared to control conditions without punishment. The negative effect is attributed to both the direct material cost of punishment and the escalation of retaliation cycles, as well as social/emotional fallout. The review suggests that, for prediction tasks, the presence of punishment (especially if costly and in dyads) should be expected to have a less positive or even negative effect on efficiency, unless the punishment is very cheap or the environment is highly structured to avoid retaliation spirals. The findings are most directly relevant to dyadic (two-player) repeated games, and less so to larger public goods games, but the mechanisms discussed may generalize to small groups.

- source: Zu, J. J., Xu, F. X., Jin, T., & Xiang, W. (2022). Reward and Punishment Mechanism with weighting enhances cooperation in evolutionary games. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The study finds that the introduction of a reward and punishment mechanism with weighting (RPMW) increases the frequency of cooperation in evolutionary games (PDG, SDG, SHG), both in well-mixed and lattice populations. The effect is stronger with higher intensity (alpha) and a higher proportion of supervisors (beta). The combination of reward and punishment is more effective than either alone in promoting cooperation. However, all results are reported in terms of cooperation frequency, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that punishment (and reward) mechanisms can increase cooperation rates in social dilemma games, and that the effect is stronger with higher punishment/reward intensity and more supervisors. However, because the outcomes are reported as cooperation frequency rather than efficiency or group payoff, the findings are only adjacent to the prediction task. The results suggest that, in similar game designs, enabling punishment is likely to increase cooperation, which may translate to higher efficiency, but this is not directly demonstrated. The specific game structure is not a public goods game but rather two-player matrix games (PDG, SDG, SHG), so transfer to PGG-like environments should be cautious.

- source: Yang, Q. X., & Yang, Y. L. (2025). Effects of an update mechanism based on combinatorial memory and high-reputation learning objects on the evolution of. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The introduction of a social monitoring mechanism (SMM) that rewards judges based on the observed cooperation rate among participants leads to higher levels of cooperation and lower corruption in the simulated two-layer network. The effect is robust across different parameter settings, including reward/fine amounts, the social reward criterion, the presence of zealots, and the strength of the reward factor. SMM is more effective than simply increasing rewards/fines in the absence of SMM. The model is applied to both Prisoner's Dilemma and Snowdrift Game variants, showing similar qualitative results. However, the paper does not report group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare outcomes, so the findings are limited to behavioral outcomes (cooperation/corruption rates).
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that mechanisms which reward third-party punishers based on group cooperation rates can increase cooperation and reduce corruption in environments structurally similar to the Prisoner's Dilemma and Snowdrift Game. However, because the outcomes are reported as cooperation rates rather than efficiency or group payoff, the results should be interpreted as informative about behavioral mechanisms rather than direct efficiency effects. The model is not a standard public goods game, but the two-layer structure and the focus on punishment/reward mechanisms are adjacent to PGG-like environments. The findings suggest that adding a social monitoring/reward mechanism for punishers may increase cooperation, but the effect on efficiency is not directly quantified.

- source: Schlaepfer, A. (2018). The emergence and selection of reputation systems that drive cooperative behaviour. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in a population of initially non-cooperative individuals, a reputation system based on punishment is likely to emerge first and drive the initial evolution of cooperation. However, once cooperation is established, it is mainly sustained by a reputation system based on cooperative actions (social image). The model shows that cooperation rates are highest when both punishment-based and cooperation-based reputation systems are available. The emergence and stability of these systems depend on the costs of information acquisition (gossip) and the relevance of active gossip channels. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, but focuses on cooperation rates and the use of reputation systems.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the idea that punishment-based reputation systems can help establish cooperation in repeated social dilemmas, especially when information about others' behavior is costly to acquire. However, it does not provide direct evidence about efficiency or group payoff, nor does it use a standard public goods game (it uses a dyadic Prisoner's Dilemma with a punishment stage). The findings suggest that punishment may be most important for the initial emergence of cooperation, but less so for its maintenance. For prediction tasks about efficiency in public goods games with punishment, this paper is only indirectly informative, mainly by highlighting the role of punishment-based reputation in overcoming initial defection.

- source: Sánchez, A., & Cuesta, J. A. (2005). Altruism may arise from individual selection. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that strong reciprocity (altruistic punishment) can arise and persist in agent-based evolutionary models of the Ultimatum Game under individual selection, without the need for group or cultural selection, repeated interactions, or reputation. The key mechanism is the role of fluctuations and finite selection intervals, which allow altruistic punishers to survive and proliferate. The model reproduces the empirical finding that offers and acceptance thresholds are much higher than the self-interested Nash equilibrium. However, the paper does not analyze or report on group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, focusing instead on the distribution and evolution of behavioral strategies.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the possibility that punishment (in the form of rejection of unfair offers) can evolve and persist under individual selection in one-shot, anonymous games. However, it does not provide evidence about the effect of punishment on group efficiency or payoff in public-goods-game-like environments. The findings are most relevant for understanding the evolutionary plausibility of punishment behaviors, not for predicting efficiency outcomes in PGGs with or without punishment. Thus, its direct value for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions is limited, though it may inform mechanistic expectations about the robustness of punishment behaviors.

- source: Wang, C. Y., & Cui, W. F. (2022). Supervision for the Public Health Services for Older Adults Under the Background of Government Purchasing: An Evolutionary Game Analysis Framework. *Frontiers in Public Health*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that in a principal-agent setting with information asymmetry, static punishment or reward mechanisms are insufficient to ensure full compliance (self-discipline) by service providers. Dynamic mechanisms, where the intensity of punishment or reward depends on the observed behavior, can stabilize higher levels of self-discipline. Specifically, higher punishment intensity increases the proportion of self-discipline, while higher reward intensity can decrease it due to moral hazard and adverse selection. The model is theoretical and does not report efficiency or group payoff, but the results suggest that dynamic sanctioning mechanisms are more effective at promoting compliance than static ones.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that the effectiveness of punishment in promoting cooperation (or self-discipline) depends on the design of the sanctioning mechanism, particularly whether it is static or dynamic. While the model is not a public goods game per se, it is structurally similar in that it models a social dilemma with potential for free-riding and the use of sanctions. The findings suggest that, in analogous PGG-like environments, dynamic punishment mechanisms are likely to be more effective at increasing cooperation than static ones, and that increasing punishment intensity generally increases compliance.
