However, the paper does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes, so its direct value for predicting efficiency is limited. It is most useful for understanding the qualitative effects of punishment design and intensity on compliance behavior.

- source: Larson, J. M. (2017). Networks and Interethnic Cooperation. *Journal of Politics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that the ability of groups to enforce cooperation through peer punishment depends critically on the structure of their internal communication networks. Specifically, the 'reach' or 'integration' of the network—how widely and quickly information about defections can spread—determines how easily cooperation can be sustained. Denser networks are not necessarily better; networks with high integration (no highly peripheral or bottleneck nodes) are optimal for supporting cooperation. The model also shows that there is an upper bound on the proportion of interethnic (cross-group) interactions that can be kept cooperative, determined by the network's reach. Improvements in communication speed, longer punishment phases, and more integrated networks all make cooperation easier to sustain. The paper does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes, but rather the theoretical conditions for sustaining cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical guidance on how network structure moderates the effectiveness of peer punishment in sustaining cooperation in repeated social dilemmas. For prediction tasks, it suggests that the effectiveness of punishment (and thus the likely efficiency gain from enabling punishment) depends not on network density but on integration/reach. However, the model is not a public goods game per se, and does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes, so it cannot be used directly for quantitative prediction of efficiency. It is most useful for qualitative reasoning about the importance of network structure (especially communication reach) as a moderator of punishment's effect on cooperation.

- source: Morris, A., MacGlashan, J., Littman, M. L., & Cushman, F. (2017). Evolution of flexibility and rigidity in retaliatory punishment. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that when punishment is costly, flexible (learning-based) victims are less likely to learn to punish, making them more vulnerable in repeated interactions. This leads to the evolution of rigid punishment strategies (i.e., always punish theft), even when such punishment is costly and not always effective. The learning dynamics of reinforcement learning agents show that thieves are more likely to adapt flexibly, while victims tend to persist in punishment. The evolutionary simulations confirm that the cost of punishment biases selection toward rigid punishment. The findings are primarily about the evolution and persistence of punishment strategies, not about the effect of punishment on group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and simulation-based evidence about the evolution of punishment strategies in repeated dyadic games, focusing on the interplay between learning dynamics and evolutionary selection. While it does not directly address efficiency or group payoff in public goods games, it is informative about when rigid punishment is likely to evolve and persist, especially when punishment is costly. For prediction tasks about efficiency in public goods games with punishment, this paper suggests that the cost of punishment and the learning dynamics of participants may influence the prevalence of rigid punishment, but it does not provide direct evidence about efficiency outcomes. Its relevance is mainly in understanding the conditions under which punishment is likely to be rigid or flexible, which may indirectly affect efficiency in related environments.

- source: Larson, J. M. (2017). WHY THE WEST BECAME WILD Informal Governance with Incomplete Networks. *World Politics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that the ability of peer punishment to sustain cooperation depends critically on the social network structure and the reach of gossip. Incomplete networks with peripheral members cannot sustain full cooperation: the most peripheral members are likely to be persistently cheated and ostracized, as their negative gossip does not reach enough punishers to deter defection. Shocks that increase the gains from defection or that create more peripheral positions (e.g., through rapid population growth or enclave settlement) lead to a ratcheting down of cooperation, with more members becoming persistent cheaters. The model generates hypotheses about when and where cooperation will break down, but does not provide quantitative predictions about group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical insight into how network structure and information transmission affect the limits of peer punishment in sustaining cooperation. It is relevant for understanding moderators of punishment effectiveness, especially the role of network incompleteness and peripheral positions. However, it does not provide direct evidence or quantitative predictions about efficiency or group payoff in public goods games or their variants. For the downstream prediction task, the paper suggests that punishment will be less effective at increasing efficiency in settings with incomplete networks, high population turnover, or enclave settlement, but does not provide parameterized estimates or control-treatment efficiency comparisons.

- source: Ye, W. X., & Fan, S. H. (2020). Evolutionary traveler's dilemma game based on particle swarm optimization. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that using a PSO-based learning mechanism in the traveler's dilemma game leads to higher levels of cooperation (higher average claims) compared to traditional Fermi dynamics, across different network types. The effect of the reward/punishment parameter (R) and the balance between self-cognition and social cognition (c1) are explored: social learning promotes cooperation when the reward/punishment is small (large strategy interval), while self-cognition becomes more important as the reward/punishment increases (small strategy interval). The main outcome is the normalized cooperation level, not efficiency or total group payoff. The results are robust across network types, but the study is simulation-based and does not report direct efficiency or payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about how learning mechanisms and the structure of reward/punishment in a traveler's dilemma game (an adjacent but not exact variant of the public goods game) affect the prevalence of cooperative behavior (high claims). It does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency or group payoff in public goods games with or without punishment. However, it suggests that the effectiveness of punishment/reward mechanisms in promoting cooperation depends on the size of the incentive and the learning dynamics. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is only weakly informative, as it does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes and studies a different game structure.

- source: Pan, Q. H., Wang, Y., & He, M. F. (2022). Impacts of special cooperation strategy with reward and punishment mechanism on cooperation evolution. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that introducing special cooperators who can both reward and punish leads to a variety of stable states, including coexistence of all three strategies or pairs of strategies. Punishment is necessary to suppress defectors and enable cooperation, but excessive punishment can reduce the prevalence of special cooperators. Rewards only promote ordinary cooperation when punishment is at least moderate. The effects of reward and punishment on cooperation are non-linear: moderate levels are most effective, while too much of either can be counterproductive. The model does not report efficiency or group payoff, so findings are limited to behavioral strategy frequencies.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, mechanistic evidence that punishment (especially when combined with reward) can promote the prevalence of cooperative strategies in spatial evolutionary games. However, because the outcomes are proportions of strategy types rather than efficiency or group payoff, the results are only adjacent to the prediction task. The findings suggest that moderate punishment is most effective for promoting cooperation, and that the effect of reward depends on the level of punishment. For prediction of efficiency, this paper suggests that the relationship between punishment and efficiency may be non-monotonic, with moderate punishment being optimal, but does not provide direct quantitative evidence for efficiency outcomes.

- source: Lee, H. W., Cleveland, C., & Szolnoki, A. (2024). Suppressing defection by increasing temptation: The impact of smart cooperators on a social dilemma situation. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The study finds that adding a 'smart cooperator' strategy (who both punishes defectors and rewards cooperators, at a cost) to a spatial prisoner's dilemma model leads to cyclic dominance among defectors, cooperators, and smart cooperators. This cyclic dominance produces counter-intuitive effects: for example, increasing the temptation to defect can reduce the prevalence of defectors by indirectly supporting smart cooperators. Similarly, increasing punishment does not always reduce defection, and lowering the cost of smart cooperation can benefit unconditional cooperators more than smart cooperators. The main reported outcomes are the stationary fractions of each strategy, not group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, mechanistic insight into how punishment and reward mechanisms interact in a spatial social dilemma with multiple strategies, but does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency or group payoff outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment. The findings suggest that the introduction of complex sanctioning strategies can lead to non-linear and sometimes counter-intuitive effects on cooperation and defection rates, but the lack of efficiency or payoff reporting limits its direct utility for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment in PGG-like environments. The main value is in highlighting the potential for cyclic dominance and unexpected behavioral dynamics when multiple sanctioning strategies are present.

- source: Zhao, S. S., Pan, Q. H., Zhu, W. Q., & He, M. F. (2023). How punishing evil and promoting good promotes cooperation in social dilemma. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that the introduction of a 'punishing evil and promoting good' strategy can promote the survival of cooperation in social dilemmas. Increasing punishment strength alone can induce the emergence of this strategy and ordinary cooperators, but is not always sufficient to eliminate defectors. Increasing reward strength can shift the system from full defection to full cooperation when the temptation to defect is low, but is insufficient when temptation is high. The most robust promotion of cooperation occurs when both punishment and reward are present, with certain combinations of minimum values able to eliminate defectors entirely. The results are presented as equilibrium proportions of each strategy, not as efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that both punishment and reward mechanisms can promote cooperation in social dilemma settings, and that their effects are complementary. However, because the outcomes are reported as equilibrium strategy frequencies rather than efficiency or group payoff, the results are only indirectly informative for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment. The model is adjacent to public goods games (it is a prisoner's dilemma variant), and the findings suggest that the presence and strength of punishment and reward mechanisms are important moderators of cooperation, but do not provide quantitative predictions about efficiency.

- source: Jiang, X. H., & Zheng, W. Q. (2024). Dynamic strategies for collaborative governance of rural environments: a simulation study on the most effective mechanisms. *Environment Development and Sustainability*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that under a static reward and punishment mechanism (SRPM), the system does not reach a stable equilibrium; strategies of all parties fluctuate periodically. Under a dynamic reward and punishment mechanism (DRPM), the system converges to a stable mixed-strategy Nash equilibrium (MSNE), interpreted as an evolutionarily stable strategy (ESS). Increasing the upper limit of punishments for illegal emissions by village enterprises significantly increases the probability of legal emissions and reduces the need for strict government regulation and active supervision by villagers. Increasing the upper limit of rewards for villagers' active supervision increases their supervision probability but may reduce legal compliance by enterprises and government regulatory effort. The DRPM is recommended for more effective and stable governance.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the prediction task by showing that dynamic, behavior-dependent punishment mechanisms (analogous to more flexible or responsive punishment rules in public goods games) can stabilize cooperative behavior (legal emissions) and reduce the need for costly monitoring and enforcement. However, the model is not a standard public goods game and does not report efficiency or group payoff. The main outcomes are strategy probabilities, not payoffs. The findings suggest that increasing punishment effectiveness (punishment_tech) and using dynamic punishment rules may improve stability and compliance, but the effect on efficiency is not directly quantified. The results are most relevant for understanding how punishment design (cost, tech, dynamic vs static) may affect behavioral outcomes in multi-agent governance games.

- source: Wang, R. Y., & Ng, C. N. (2015). Can Centralized Sanctioning Promote Trust in Social Dilemmas? A Two-Level Trust Game with Incomplete Information. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that introducing a centralized sanctioning authority into a repeated trust game with incomplete information can increase trust and reciprocity, especially when actors believe the authority is likely to punish defectors. The model shows that the threat of centralized punishment relaxes the conditions under which trust is placed and honored, compared to the baseline trust game without an authority. The effect is strongest when there is optimism about the authority's willingness to punish. The model predicts that centralized sanctioning can mitigate the end-game effect (decline in cooperation in final rounds). However, the analysis is theoretical and does not provide explicit efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that adding a centralized punishment mechanism to a trust-based social dilemma can increase trust and cooperation, especially under incomplete information. While the model is not a public goods game, it is adjacent and relevant for predicting the effect of centralized punishment in repeated social dilemmas. The findings suggest that, all else equal, enabling centralized punishment is likely to increase cooperation and, by implication, group payoffs or efficiency, particularly when the punishment is sufficiently strong and actors believe the authority is likely to punish. However, the paper does not provide quantitative predictions of efficiency or group payoff, so its value for direct efficiency prediction is limited to qualitative inference.

- source: Berenji, B., Chou, T., & D'Orsogna, M. R. (2014). Recidivism and Rehabilitation of Criminal Offenders: A Carrot and Stick Evolutionary Game. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The model demonstrates that neither punishment alone nor rehabilitation alone is optimal for reducing crime and recidivism. Instead, the best outcomes (highest ratio of reformed to unreformable individuals) are achieved by balancing punishment severity and rehabilitation resources, subject to a fixed total resource constraint. There is an optimal allocation of resources between punishment and rehabilitation, and both too little and too much of either can worsen outcomes. The model also shows that the duration and intensity of rehabilitation matter, with intermediate durations being most effective. These findings are robust across different initial conditions and parameter values. However, the model does not report efficiency or group payoff in the sense of public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, adjacent evidence for the prediction task. It supports the idea that the effect of punishment on group-level outcomes (here, the ratio of reformed to unreformable individuals) depends on the balance between punishment and rehabilitation, and that both are needed for optimal outcomes. However, because the model is not a public goods game and does not report efficiency or group payoff, its direct predictive value for efficiency in PGG-like environments is limited. The main implication is that punishment's effect is non-monotonic and context-dependent, especially when resources are constrained and when rehabilitation is possible. The findings suggest that in analogous PGG settings, enabling punishment without considering complementary positive interventions or resource tradeoffs may not maximize efficiency.

- source: Fang, X., & Chen, X. J. (2021). Evolutionary dynamics of trust in the N-player trust game with individual reward and punishment. *European Physical Journal B*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The introduction of individual reward and punishment in the N-player trust game can promote the stable coexistence of investors and trustworthy trustees, which is interpreted as a promotion of trust. The size of the attraction domain for this coexistence increases with both the overall incentive strength and the share of incentives devoted to reward (as opposed to punishment). Reward is found to be more effective than punishment in promoting trust. The analysis is theoretical, using replicator dynamics, and does not directly report efficiency or group payoff, but the results imply that mechanisms increasing trust and trustworthiness can move the system toward more socially optimal states.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect theoretical support for the idea that introducing punishment (and especially reward) mechanisms can promote cooperation-like behavior in trust-based social dilemmas. However, it does not provide direct evidence on efficiency or group payoff, nor does it report quantitative efficiency outcomes for control vs. punishment-enabled conditions. The model is adjacent to public goods games, so its findings may be cautiously extrapolated to similar environments, especially regarding the positive effect of incentive strength and the relative effectiveness of reward over punishment. For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that increasing the strength and effectiveness of incentives (especially reward) is likely to expand the basin of attraction for cooperative outcomes, but does not provide direct efficiency data.

- source: Su, F., Chang, J. B., & Li, X. (2021). Research on the Evolution Path and Influence Factors of Core Enterprise Oriented Entrepreneurship Ecosystem Under the Government Regulation. *Ieee Access*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper finds that the presence and magnitude of punishment (by core enterprises to start-ups, and by government to both) increases the likelihood and speed of positive strategy selection (cooperation, entrepreneurship, regulation) among all agents. Conversely, higher costs (supervision, regulation, entrepreneurship) and higher subsidies can reduce the tendency for positive strategies. The model identifies multiple possible stable equilibria depending on parameter values, and shows that a moderately high, flexible reward and punishment mechanism is most effective for promoting positive strategies. However, the paper does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare as outcomes, focusing instead on the behavioral evolution of strategies.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, theoretical support for the idea that punishment mechanisms (and their cost/benefit structure) can promote positive, cooperative, or pro-social strategies in multi-agent systems similar to public goods games. However, it does not provide direct evidence or quantitative predictions about efficiency or group payoff, nor does it report empirical results. For prediction tasks, the paper suggests that increasing punishment (and reducing its cost) should increase the prevalence of positive strategies, which may be associated with higher efficiency in analogous PGG settings, but this is not directly demonstrated. The model's complexity and focus on behavioral strategy selection, rather than payoff, limits its direct applicability to efficiency prediction.

- source: Wu, Y. E., Li, Z. Q., Liu, Z. Y., Cui, B. W., & Yin, A. R. (2024). Third party interventions promote cooperation on the interdependent networks: A perspective based on prospect theory. *Chaos*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The introduction of third-party intervention (punishment and/or reward) in a networked PD/SD game increases the fraction of cooperators in both the intervention and dispute layers. The effect is stronger when the intervention cost is lower and the intervention intensity (punishment/reward coefficients) is higher. The results are robust across different network topologies (square lattice and ER random networks) and game types (PD and SD). However, the paper does not report group payoff, efficiency, or welfare; all results are in terms of cooperation rates.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that third-party punishment and reward mechanisms can increase cooperation rates in networked social dilemmas, and that the effect is moderated by the cost and intensity of intervention. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be directly used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. The results are most useful for predicting changes in cooperation rates, not payoffs. The model is also based on the PD/SD rather than a standard PGG, and is simulation/theory-based rather than empirical.

- source: Shinada, M., Yamagishi, T., & Ohmura, Y. (2004). False friends are worse than bitter enemies: Altruistic punishment of in-group members. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The bidirectional supervision mechanism, where players can check and avoid corrupt umpires, is effective at suppressing both corruption and defection in a spatial donation game with third-party punishment. The effectiveness of this mechanism depends on the punishment amount (must be within a certain range), the bribe amount (must not be too high), and the ratio of tax to checking cost (higher tax or lower checking cost is better). The benefit-to-cost ratio of cooperation has little effect within reasonable ranges. The main outcomes are the fractions of different strategies (cooperation, defection, corruption, fairness) in the population. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, but argues that the mechanism is most beneficial to collective interests when parameters are tuned appropriately.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the prediction task by showing that the design of punishment, bribe, tax, and checking cost parameters can strongly affect the prevalence of cooperation and corruption in a donation game with third-party punishment. However, because the main outcomes are behavioral (strategy fractions) and not efficiency or group payoff, the findings should be used as qualitative or mechanistic guidance rather than quantitative prediction of efficiency. The results suggest that punishment is only effective at suppressing defection and corruption if its severity is within a certain range and if corruption incentives (bribes) are not too high. The presence of a mechanism for players to avoid corrupt punishers is critical. The findings are most relevant for adjacent game designs with third-party punishment and corruption, but less directly transferable to standard public goods games or to quantitative efficiency prediction.

- source: Huang, Y. K., & Inohara, T. (2015). Group-separations based on the repeated prisoners' dilemma games. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper provides a theoretical framework for understanding how the ability to sustain cooperation via punishment (trigger strategies) in repeated pairwise prisoners' dilemma games depends on the discount factor and the players' mixed strategies. It shows that as the discount factor increases, more pairs can sustain cooperation, leading to larger groups of mutually cooperating players. The main findings are the mathematical characterization of the group-separation structure as a function of the discount factor, and the identification of intervals where complete-group-separations (where all within-group pairs can sustain cooperation) are possible. However, the paper does not analyze or report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is of limited direct use for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their variants, as it does not analyze efficiency or payoff outcomes. Its main contribution is theoretical: it shows how the ability to sustain cooperation via punishment in repeated pairwise games depends on the discount factor and the heterogeneity of players' strategies. For prediction tasks, it suggests that the effectiveness of punishment in sustaining cooperation (and thus potentially efficiency) is sensitive to the discount factor and the similarity of players' strategies, but it does not provide quantitative or empirical evidence on efficiency effects. The model is also for pairwise repeated PDs, not for n-player public goods games.

- source: Goodman, J. R. (2023). The problem of opportunity. *Biology & Philosophy*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in agent-based models of the Prisoner's Dilemma with punishment (strong reciprocity), cooperation can be maintained at high levels when only overt defectors are present. However, when agents can mimic cooperators or defect covertly (evading detection and punishment), the population can be dominated by individuals who are not true cooperators, even though the observed rate of cooperation remains high. The models highlight that the opportunity to defect undetected undermines the effectiveness of punishment-based strategies in ensuring genuine prosociality. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, but raises concerns that high observed cooperation rates in experimental games may not indicate high group welfare or internalized prosocial preferences.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical caution for interpreting high cooperation rates in punishment-enabled games as evidence of increased efficiency or group welfare. It shows that punishment can maintain high cooperation in simple models, but that the introduction of undetectable defection strategies (mimicry, covert defection) can allow free-riding to persist without reducing the observed cooperation rate. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the presence of punishment may not always translate to higher efficiency, especially if the game design allows for undetected defection. However, the models are dyadic PDs, not public goods games, and do not report efficiency or group payoff, so direct quantitative prediction is not possible from this paper.

- source: Blonski, M., & Spagnolo, G. (2015). Prisoners' other Dilemma. *International Journal of Game Theory*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in the infinitely repeated Prisoner's Dilemma, the riskiness of cooperation equilibria can be precisely characterized by comparing the Nash-products of cooperation and defection equilibria. The least risky cooperation equilibrium is always the grim trigger strategy. There exists a critical discount factor (delta*) above which cooperation can be supported without strategic risk, and below which all cooperation equilibria are risky. The more patient the players (higher discount factor), the more forgiving the punishment strategies can be while still supporting cooperation. The paper also introduces the concept of 'risk perfection', requiring that not only the equilibrium path but also all subgames (punishment phases) are not risky. The results generalize to asymmetric games and to repeated Cournot duopoly (collusion) games, which are structurally similar to public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical guidance on the relationship between punishment harshness, forgiveness, and the viability of cooperation in repeated games. While it does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes, it shows that the ability of punishment to support cooperation depends on the discount factor and the riskiness of the equilibrium. In environments structurally similar to public goods games, the results suggest that harsher punishments (like grim trigger) are less risky and more likely to support cooperation, especially when players are less patient. As patience increases, more forgiving punishments become viable. For prediction tasks, the paper implies that the effectiveness of punishment in increasing efficiency depends on the strategic riskiness of the environment and the patience of players, but does not provide quantitative efficiency outcomes.

- source: Christoforou, E., Anta, A. F., Georgiou, C., Mosteiro, M. A., & Sánchez, A. (2013). Crowd Computing as a Cooperation Problem: An Evolutionary Approach. *Journal of Statistical Physics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that introducing punishment for cheating in a repeated master-worker (principal-agent) setting with reinforcement learning leads to faster convergence to a state where all workers cooperate (i.e., provide correct answers), compared to no punishment. However, punishment is not strictly necessary for eventual correctness if other conditions (such as sufficient reward relative to aspiration and cost) are met. The system is robust to high tolerance for defectors, but as group size increases, high tolerance can reduce the probability of achieving full cooperation. The main outcomes are the probability of cheating and the system's convergence to correctness, not direct measures of group efficiency or payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like environments. While it does not report efficiency or group payoff, it shows that punishment reduces the average cheating probability and accelerates convergence to a fully cooperative state. In settings where efficiency is closely tied to the rate of cooperation (e.g., if only correct answers generate group benefit), these results suggest that punishment would increase efficiency, especially in the short-to-medium term. However, the absence of explicit payoff or efficiency data limits the direct applicability of these findings to the prediction task. The effects of group size, tolerance, and aspiration are also informative: higher group size and higher tolerance to defectors can undermine the effectiveness of punishment in achieving full cooperation.

- source: Shen, C., He, Z. X., Shi, L., Wang, Z., & Tanimoto, J. (2024). Prosocial punishment bots breed social punishment in human players. *Journal of the Royal Society Interface*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that introducing a fraction of bots that always cooperate and punish defectors (CP) into a population of normal players in a two-stage Prisoner's Dilemma can significantly increase the prevalence of prosocial punishment among normal players. This effect is stronger when bots are placed on high-degree nodes in scale-free networks or when players have a conformist bias (copying the majority). The effect is robust to different network structures, learning rules, and parameter values. However, the main reported outcomes are behavioral (strategy frequencies), not efficiency or group payoff. The study does not report the effect of punishment on efficiency or total group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the conditions under which punishment (specifically, prosocial punishment implemented by bots) can increase the prevalence of prosocial punishment and cooperation in a two-stage Prisoner's Dilemma. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. The findings may be useful for understanding how network structure, imitation rules, and the presence of committed punishers affect the spread of prosocial punishment, but not for quantitative prediction of efficiency outcomes.

- source: Villatoro, D., Andrighetto, G., Conte, R., & Sabater-Mir, J. (2015). Self-Policing Through Norm Internalization: A Cognitive Solution to the Tragedy of the Digital Commons in Social Networks. *Jasss-the Journal of Artificial Societies and Social Simulation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper finds that agents capable of norm internalization (EMIL-I-A) achieve higher levels of cooperation (as measured by successful service transactions) than purely self-interested agents (IUMA) in a digital commons scenario. Internalizers flexibly adapt to environmental changes, internalizing and de-internalizing norms as conditions shift. The presence of punishment and sanction mechanisms supports norm salience and internalization, but the main driver of improved cooperation is internalization rather than external punishment alone. The model demonstrates that norm internalization can enable self-policing and robust cooperation in dynamic, decentralized environments. However, the outcomes are not reported in terms of group efficiency or total payoff as in standard public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that the presence of punishment and especially norm internalization mechanisms can increase cooperation and successful collective action in social dilemma-like environments. However, because the scenario is not a standard public goods game and does not report efficiency or group payoff, the findings are only adjacent for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGGs. The results suggest that when agents internalize norms (potentially supported by punishment/sanction), cooperation is more robust and adaptive, but the direct effect of punishment on efficiency is not isolated or quantified. The model's focus on norm salience and internalization may inform predictions about environments where norm-based motivations are strong, but it does not provide direct quantitative guidance for the efficiency impact of punishment in PGGs.

- source: Zhao, X., & Zou, H. (2025). Research on the Evolutionary Game of Multi-Body Co-Innovation in Green Innovation Ecosystems. *Polish Journal of Environmental Studies*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that government-imposed rewards (subsidies) and punishments (fines) both promote the stability of collaborative innovation among the three actors. There is an optimal benefit-sharing ratio for collaboration, and the effect of government intervention is strongest when both rewards and punishments are used together. The model shows threshold effects for punishment severity, and that the stability of cooperation is sensitive to the distribution of benefits and the magnitude of policy interventions. However, the main outcomes are the evolutionary stability of cooperation, not explicit efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the prediction task. It shows that introducing punishment (and/or reward) mechanisms increases the stability and likelihood of cooperation in a multi-actor game with public-goods-like features. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, so it cannot be used to quantitatively predict the change in efficiency from enabling punishment. The results suggest that, in similar multi-actor public-goods-like environments, punishment (especially when combined with reward) is likely to increase cooperation and system stability, which may be associated with higher efficiency, but this is not directly measured.

- source: Chang, Q., & Zhang, Y. (2021). Peer pressure in extortion game can resolve social dilemma. *European Physical Journal B*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that introducing peer pressure (punishment) into a spatial donation/extortion game can promote the prevalence of cooperation, especially at moderate punishment intensities. When punishment is too strong, extortioners dominate. The effect of punishment is non-monotonic: moderate punishment helps cooperation, but high punishment harms it. The main reported outcomes are the frequencies of each strategy, not group payoff or efficiency. The findings are based on simulation, not empirical data.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, mechanistic evidence that punishment can promote cooperation in spatial evolutionary games with extortion strategies, but only within a certain range of punishment intensity. However, because the outcomes are frequencies of strategies rather than group payoff or efficiency, and because the model is a spatial donation/extortion game (not a standard public goods game), the results are only adjacent to the prediction task. The paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment in public goods games, but it does suggest that the effect of punishment on cooperation (and by extension, possibly efficiency) is non-monotonic and context-dependent.

- source: Gao, D. D., & Guo, W. (2025). Evolutionary game and simulation analysis of tripartite subjects in public health emergencies under government reward and punishment mechanisms. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The study finds that government reward and punishment mechanisms (subsidies and fines) for pharmaceutical companies and the public promote compliant production and cooperation in prevention and control, respectively. Increasing the magnitude of rewards or punishments accelerates the convergence to full compliance and cooperation. The initial willingness of parties to participate also affects the speed of convergence. However, the model does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, and all results are in terms of the probability of adopting certain strategies and the speed of convergence to stable equilibria.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, theoretical support for the idea that increasing the strength of punishment and reward mechanisms can promote compliance and cooperation in a multi-party public-goods-like setting. However, it does not provide quantitative predictions about efficiency or group payoff, nor does it report empirical results. The findings may inform the qualitative direction of punishment effects in similar environments, but cannot be used for direct prediction of efficiency outcomes as required by the downstream task.

- source: Qu, X. L., Zhou, C. L., Cao, Z. G., & Yang, X. G. (2016). Conditional dissociation as a punishment mechanism in the evolution of cooperation. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: Conditional dissociation, modeled as a punishment mechanism where players can break up with defectors and must wait before re-entering the game, promotes the evolution of cooperation in an iterated Prisoner's Dilemma. The effect is stronger when the punishment is more severe (longer waiting time) and when the population's lifespan is longer. Allowing mixed strategies (as opposed to only pure strategies) reduces the probability of ending up with a fully cooperative population. The model is clean, excluding other cooperation-promoting mechanisms, and shows that both intolerant cooperators and tolerant defectors can be stable outcomes. The main outcomes are the frequency of cooperative vs. defective strategies, not group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence for the effect of punishment-like mechanisms (conditional dissociation) on cooperation in repeated pairwise social dilemmas. While not a public goods game, the findings suggest that punishment mechanisms that impose a cost on defectors (and sometimes on punishers) can promote cooperation, especially when the punishment is more severe and when the game is longer. The absence of direct efficiency or group payoff outcomes limits its direct applicability to efficiency prediction, but the results support the general idea that increasing punishment severity (e.g., higher punishment cost or effectiveness) can increase cooperation, though possibly at the expense of overall efficiency if punishers also incur costs. The negative effect of mixed strategies on cooperation may also be relevant for predicting outcomes in more flexible or noisy environments.

- source: Chan, N. W. H., Xu, C., Tey, S. M., Yap, Y. J., & Hui, P. M. (2013). Evolutionary snowdrift game incorporating costly punishment in structured populations. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that introducing a costly punishing strategy into the snowdrift game on a square lattice leads to richer population dynamics and phase structures than in well-mixed populations. For certain parameter ranges (low cost-to-benefit ratio r and sufficiently high punishment impact β), punishers can suppress defectors and coexist with cooperators, or even dominate. The spatial structure (lattice) allows for stable coexistence of all three strategies (C, D, P) in some regions, a phenomenon not observed in well-mixed populations. The pair approximation analytic method only qualitatively matches the simulation results and fails to capture some spatially-induced phases. The main reported outcomes are the steady-state frequencies of each strategy, not group efficiency or payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the effects of punishment in structured populations, specifically in the context of the snowdrift game (which is adjacent to public goods games). It shows that costly punishment can promote the prevalence of cooperative strategies and suppress defectors in spatially structured settings, but does not quantify the effect on group efficiency or total payoff. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, the paper suggests that spatial structure and the presence of punishment can qualitatively enhance cooperation, but does not provide direct quantitative evidence for efficiency outcomes. The findings are most relevant for understanding how punishment and spatial structure interact to shape population composition, rather than for predicting efficiency changes in public goods games.

- source: Yan, Z. Y., Li, L., Shang, J., & Zhao, H. (2024). Periodic update rule with Q-learning promotes evolution of cooperation in transition with mechanism. *Neurocomputing*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that introducing a punishment mechanism, where cooperators can choose to become punishers at a cost, increases the frequency of cooperation in a spatial prisoner's dilemma game. The effect is strongest when the punishment cost is low and the update period for strategy revision is short. The Q-learning-based periodic update rule (switching between Fermi and aspiration-driven rules) further enhances cooperation. However, the main reported outcome is the fraction of cooperators, not group efficiency or total payoff. The model is not a public goods game but a spatial pairwise prisoner's dilemma with punishment and learning dynamics.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that punishment mechanisms can increase cooperation rates in spatial social dilemmas, especially when punishment is not too costly and agents update strategies frequently. However, because the model is not a public goods game and does not report efficiency or group payoff, it should be used only as weak, adjacent evidence for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments. The findings suggest that low punishment cost and frequent update opportunities are likely to make punishment more effective at promoting cooperation, but the translation to efficiency is uncertain.

- source: Xie, Y. Y., Zhang, S. H., Zhang, Z. P., & Bu, H. Y. (2020). Impact of binary social status with hierarchical punishment on the evolution of cooperation in the spatial prisoner's dilemma game. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that allowing only a subset of agents (the 'powerful' group) to punish defectors can promote cooperation in a spatial prisoner's dilemma, especially when the punishment fine is sufficiently high. However, the relationship is non-linear: too many powerful agents or excessively strong punishment can reduce cooperation. The main reported outcome is the fraction of cooperators, not group efficiency or total payoff. The findings are based on agent-based simulations, not empirical or experimental data.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the effect of hierarchical punishment on cooperation rates in spatial prisoner's dilemma games, which are adjacent to public goods games. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot directly inform predictions about efficiency changes due to punishment. The results suggest that the structure and exclusivity of punishment rights, as well as the intensity of punishment, can have non-monotonic effects on cooperation, which may be relevant for interpreting similar mechanisms in public goods games. Use this paper for qualitative or mechanistic insight, not for quantitative prediction of efficiency.

- source: Wu, Y., Zhang, B., & Zhang, S. H. (2017). Probabilistic reward or punishment promotes cooperation in evolutionary games. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.

  findings: The introduction of a third-party agent who probabilistically rewards cooperators and punishes defectors increases the fraction of cooperators in both the prisoner's dilemma and snowdrift games across different network structures. The effect is stronger as the reward/punishment magnitude increases and as the probability of encountering the third-party agent increases. The main outcome is the fraction of cooperators, not group payoff or efficiency. The results are robust across square lattice, Erdös-Rényi, and scale-free networks.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the idea that probabilistic punishment (and/or reward) can increase cooperation rates in social dilemma games, but it does not report on efficiency or group payoff. The model is not a public goods game, but a two-player prisoner's dilemma or snowdrift game, so direct prediction of efficiency in PGGs is not possible. The findings may be useful for understanding the general effect of third-party sanctions on cooperation, but not for quantitative prediction of efficiency outcomes in PGG-like environments.

- source: Yang, H. X., & Chen, X. J. (2018). Promoting cooperation by punishing minority. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that in a multilayer network with inter-layer feedback and reinforcement learning, cooperation is promoted by lower punishment costs and higher punishment severity. The frequency of cooperation is sensitive to these parameters, with cooperation collapsing when punishment is too costly or too weak. The self-adapting Q-learning mechanism enables agents to learn strategies that support higher cooperation rates, especially when learning rates and discount factors are tuned appropriately. However, the main outcome is the frequency of cooperation, not group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about how punishment cost and severity affect cooperation rates in a spatially structured, multilayer environment with reinforcement learning. While it does not report efficiency or group payoff, the findings suggest that punishment can increase cooperation under certain cost/severity regimes, but excessive punishment cost can undermine cooperation. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper's results are only indirectly informative, as it does not quantify group payoff or efficiency. The model is also not a standard public goods game, but rather a spatial prisoner's dilemma with punishment administered by a management layer.

The presence and strength of punishment and reward (relative to benefit) are key moderators. - source: Li, J. Y., Wu, W. H., Li, Z. Z., Wang, W. X., & Zhang, B. Y. (2023). Data-driven evolutionary game models for the spread of fairness and cooperation in heterogeneous networks. *Frontiers in Psychiatry*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that costly punishment, when amplified by network heterogeneity and increased inequality aversion (e.g., via oxytocin), can spread through a network and promote higher levels of fairness and cooperation. This effect is observed in both the ultimatum game and the two-stage prisoner's dilemma with punishment, but not in the trust game, where the absence of punishment means that increased trust does not diffuse. The models fit experimental data well for behavioral outcomes (offers, cooperation rates, punishment rates), but do not directly analyze or report group efficiency or total payoff. The findings suggest that punishment mechanisms are more effective than reward/trust mechanisms for promoting prosocial behavior in networks.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the prediction that enabling costly punishment in networked social dilemmas (such as public goods or prisoner's dilemma variants) increases cooperation and fairness, especially when network heterogeneity allows central nodes to amplify the effect. However, because the paper is theoretical and focuses on behavioral outcomes (not efficiency or group payoff), it does not provide direct quantitative evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency. The models suggest that punishment is more effective than reward in promoting prosocial outcomes, but efficiency implications must be inferred from increased cooperation and fairness, not directly measured. The paper is most useful for understanding mechanisms and moderators (e.g., network structure, inequality aversion), but less so for direct efficiency prediction.

  findings: The study finds that without dynamic penalty-incentive (punishment/reward) mechanisms, the system does not reach a stable equilibrium and fluctuates. Introducing dynamic penalty-incentive measures leads to a stable equilibrium where platforms monitor and car owners comply with regulations, even with minimal government supervision. The speed of convergence to this stable state depends on the magnitude of penalties, rewards, and initial conditions. The model demonstrates that appropriate punishment and reward levels are necessary for stability and legal compliance, but does not quantify efficiency or group payoff.

However, it does not provide quantitative predictions of efficiency or group payoff, nor does it report empirical data. Its main value for prediction is in supporting the qualitative claim that punishment (and reward) can suppress fluctuations and promote stable compliance, but the lack of efficiency or payoff outcomes limits its direct applicability to predicting efficiency changes in PGG-like experiments.

- source: Cardoso, F. M., Gracia-Lázaro, C., & Moreno, Y. (2020). Dynamics of heuristics selection for cooperative behaviour. *New Journal of Physics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The model shows that cooperation can emerge and be sustained in iterated Prisoner's Dilemma games when agents have memory and low mutation rates, with punishment and reciprocity as key mechanisms. Increasing memory increases cooperation, while higher mutation rates reduce it. When agents can discriminate kin, cooperation is still sustained, but the underlying heuristics shift from reciprocity to kin-based discrimination. The main reported outcomes are the fraction of cooperative actions and the distribution of strategies; efficiency or group payoff is not directly analyzed.

However, because the model is based on the Prisoner's Dilemma (not a public goods game or direct variant), and because efficiency or group payoff is not directly reported, the findings are only adjacent to the prediction task. The results suggest that enabling punishment and memory can increase cooperation rates, but do not provide quantitative predictions about efficiency or payoff outcomes in public-goods-game-like environments.

- source: Shutters, S. T. (2009). Strong reciprocity, social structure, and the evolution of fair allocations in a simulated ultimatum game. *Computational and Mathematical Organization Theory*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that in a simulated ultimatum game, third-party punishment (altruistic punishment) does not generally lead to fair allocations, except in linearly structured populations where fair offers can evolve even without punishment. As the punishment multiplier (punishment impact per unit cost) increases, populations with spatial structure exhibit a sharp transition from low to very high offers, but these are not necessarily fair. The number of neighbors (social structure) strongly moderates both baseline offers and the threshold at which punishment induces high offers. The main outcome is the mean offer rate, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about how punishment cost-effectiveness and social structure (number of neighbors) affect behavioral outcomes (mean offer rates) in an ultimatum game, which is adjacent to public goods games. It does not report efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot be used directly to predict efficiency changes from punishment in PGG-like environments. However, it suggests that the effectiveness of punishment in changing behavior is highly sensitive to the punishment cost/impact ratio and the social network structure. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the importance of considering punishment cost-effectiveness and group structure, but does not provide quantitative efficiency data.

- source: Guo, T., Du, C. P., & Shi, L. (2024). Evolution of cooperation on interdependent networks: The impact of asymmetric punishment. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that asymmetric punishment, where only one group can punish another and only across network layers, can substantially increase the fraction of cooperators in spatial prisoner's dilemma games on interdependent networks. The effect is stronger when the punishment is more severe (higher fine per unit cost). When the temptation to defect is low, punishment is more effective than reward at promoting cooperation; when the temptation is high, reward becomes more effective. The main reported outcome is the fraction of cooperators, not group payoff or efficiency. The paper does not report efficiency or total group payoff, so its findings are most directly relevant to predicting cooperation rates rather than efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in agent-based models of spatial prisoner's dilemma games with asymmetric punishment, increasing punishment strength or effectiveness increases cooperation rates. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, it is not directly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency. The results suggest that, in similar environments, enabling or strengthening punishment will likely increase cooperation rates, which may be correlated with higher efficiency, but this is not directly tested. The paper also shows that the effect of punishment depends on the temptation to defect and the cost-to-fine ratio. For prediction tasks, this paper is most useful for understanding how punishment design (asymmetry, cost-to-fine ratio) affects cooperation rates, with only indirect implications for efficiency.

- source: Wen, Y. J., He, Z. X., Shen, C., & Tanimoto, J. (2025). Indirect punishment can outperform direct punishment in promoting cooperation in structured populations. *Plos Computational Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that, in structured populations modeled as a square lattice, indirect punishment (punishing defectors who harm one's neighbors) can outperform direct punishment (punishing one's own defectors) in promoting cooperation, but only when both the cost and fine of punishment are low. Outside this narrow parameter region, direct punishment may be more effective, both may be equally effective, or neither may improve cooperation. The mechanism is that indirect punishment can impose more total fines on defectors without greatly increasing the cost to punishers, but only when punishment is cheap. The main reported outcomes are cooperation rates and the distribution of strategies; group efficiency or total payoff is not directly measured.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about how the cost and scope of punishment affect cooperation rates in spatial social dilemmas. While it does not report efficiency or group payoff, it suggests that indirect punishment can be more effective at promoting cooperation than direct punishment under low-cost, low-fine conditions, but not otherwise. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, the results are only adjacent, as the main outcomes are behavioral. The findings may inform expectations about when punishment is likely to increase efficiency, but direct quantitative mapping is not possible from this paper alone.

- source: Vukov, J., Pinheiro, F. L., Santos, F. C., & Pacheco, J. M. (2013). Reward from Punishment Does Not Emerge at All Costs. *Plos Computational Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in structured (scale-free network) populations, the introduction of peer punishment does not always promote cooperation. When the cost-to-impact ratio (q/p) is high, punishment can actually reduce the prevalence of cooperation compared to the no-punishment baseline. Only when the cost of punishing is sufficiently low relative to its impact does punishment enhance cooperation. The effect is mapped across a range of game parameters (T, S) and punishment parameters (p, q), showing regions where punishment is beneficial or detrimental for cooperation. The main outcome is the fraction of cooperators, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence for the prediction task. It does not report efficiency or group payoff, but shows that the effect of punishment on cooperation (and by implication, on efficiency in adjacent games) is highly sensitive to the cost-effectiveness of punishment (q/p) and to the network structure. In structured populations, punishment can backfire unless costs are low. For prediction, this suggests that the efficiency impact of punishment in public-goods-like games may be negative or mixed if punishment is costly relative to its impact, especially in networked or heterogeneous settings. However, the lack of direct efficiency or payoff outcomes limits the direct applicability of these findings to the prediction task.

- source: Li, B. F., Zhang, X., Shi, Z. Y., & Wei, W. (2023). Justice strategy can promote cooperation with the joint of dynamic aspiration in social dilemma. *Epl*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that introducing a justice strategy (where players punish defectors at equal cost) alongside dynamic aspiration updating can suppress or eliminate defectors and promote cooperation in a spatial prisoner's dilemma. The effectiveness depends on the initial aspiration level: higher aspirations lead to fewer defectors and more justicers. The justice strategy can dominate even when initially rare, especially when defectors form clusters. The model is agent-based and simulation-driven, with outcomes reported as the stable fractions of each strategy. No aggregate payoff or efficiency outcomes are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the idea that punishment (even when costly to the punisher) can reduce defection and promote cooperation in spatial social dilemmas, especially when combined with dynamic aspirations. However, because the outcomes are reported as strategy fractions rather than group efficiency or payoff, the paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment. The model is also a spatial prisoner's dilemma, not a public goods game, though the mechanisms are related. The findings may be useful for understanding the behavioral pathways by which punishment can affect cooperation, but cannot be directly used to predict efficiency outcomes in PGG-like environments.

- source: Wang, W., He, Z. X., Li, X. G., & Shi, L. (2023). Adaptive exit facilitates the evolution of cooperation in the spatial prisoner's dilemma game with punishment. *Epl*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that allowing individuals to adaptively exit the game based on their payoff relative to aspiration can significantly increase the prevalence of cooperation, even when punishment is weak. There is an optimal aspiration level at which adaptive exit best promotes cooperation. Adaptive exit protects cooperators from exploitation by defectors and reduces the burden of punishment on punishers by increasing defectors' exit probability. The model shows that adaptive exit can eliminate second-order free-riding and that the interplay between exit and punishment is crucial for the evolution of cooperation. However, the paper does not report on group efficiency, welfare, or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that adaptive exit mechanisms and the interaction between exit and punishment can strongly affect the prevalence of cooperation in spatial social dilemmas. However, because the outcomes are reported in terms of strategy frequencies and not group efficiency or total payoff, the findings are only adjacent to the prediction task. The results suggest that enabling exit (or similar mechanisms) may increase cooperation and reduce the cost of punishment, but do not provide quantitative guidance for predicting efficiency outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment.

  findings: The paper finds that mutual punishment in the snowdrift game can either promote or inhibit cooperation depending on the temptation to defect and the punishment fine. For low temptation to defect, increasing punishment fine increases cooperation; for high temptation, it decreases cooperation; for intermediate temptation, there is a non-monotonic relationship. The main reported outcome is the steady-state fraction of cooperators, not efficiency or group payoff. The results are shown for both well-mixed and structured populations, with phase transitions in cooperation rates observed. The findings are relevant for understanding how mutual punishment (peer pressure) affects conformity and cooperation, but do not provide direct evidence on efficiency or payoff outcomes.

Its results are based on the snowdrift game, which is adjacent but not equivalent to the PGG, and the main outcome is cooperation rate, not efficiency. The mutual punishment mechanism is novel and may inform mechanism design, but the lack of payoff or efficiency analysis limits its direct applicability to the prediction task. The findings may be useful for understanding how punishment parameters affect cooperation rates, but not for quantitative prediction of efficiency changes.

- source: Patrzyk, P. M., & Takác, M. (2017). Cognitive adaptations to criminal justice lead to paranoid norm obedience. *Adaptive Behavior*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The model demonstrates that evolutionary pressures can lead to agents developing a cognitive bias that causes them to overestimate the probability of being observed and punished for norm violations. As a result, agents often cooperate (obey norms) even when they are not actually being observed, especially in environments with high uncertainty and/or strong punishment. The rate of cooperation increases with the magnitude of punishment, the probability of being observed, and the uncertainty in classifying situations as observed or not. However, much of this cooperation is 'irrational' in the sense that it is not based on accurate assessment of risk, but on evolved bias. The model does not directly analyze efficiency or group payoff, but suggests that institutional punishment and cognitive bias can sustain high levels of cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the idea that increasing punishment magnitude and the probability of observation can increase cooperation rates in social dilemma settings, even when actual monitoring is imperfect. However, because the model is based on the Prisoner's Dilemma (not a public goods game) and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, its direct applicability to predicting efficiency in PGG-like environments is limited. The findings suggest that punishment can be effective in promoting cooperation, especially under uncertainty, but do not quantify the impact on group efficiency or surplus. The model also highlights the importance of cognitive biases in sustaining cooperation, which may be relevant for interpreting experimental results in PGGs with punishment.

Its relevance to the prediction task is limited and indirect.

- source: Yang, H. X., Wu, Z. X., Rong, Z. H., & Lai, Y. C. (2015). Peer pressure: Enhancement of cooperation through mutual punishment. *Physical Review E*.

  findings: The paper finds that mutual (symmetric) punishment, where individuals punish neighbors with different strategies, can enhance cooperation in the prisoner's dilemma game. In well-mixed populations, cooperation can only be sustained if the initial density of cooperators is above a threshold, and if the punishment fine is above a critical value. In structured populations (square lattice), there is often an optimal, moderate punishment fine that maximizes cooperation, especially when the initial density of cooperators is not too low. Too weak or too harsh punishment suppresses cooperation. The main outcome is the steady-state fraction of cooperators, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not directly inform prediction of efficiency or group payoff in public goods games with punishment. However, it provides indirect evidence that symmetric, mutual punishment can increase cooperation rates under certain conditions, especially with moderate punishment severity and sufficient initial cooperation. The results are based on the prisoner's dilemma, not a public goods game, and do not report efficiency or payoff outcomes. Thus, the paper is only adjacent for the prediction task, and should be used with caution, mainly as qualitative support for the idea that punishment can promote cooperation, but not necessarily efficiency.

If the cost of punishment is high or the punishment intensity is low, introducing punishment can actually reduce cooperation below the baseline with no punishment. The system can exhibit both continuous and discontinuous transitions in cooperation as punishment parameters are varied. The main outcome is the fraction of cooperators; group efficiency or total payoff is not reported. The findings are based on agent-based simulations, not empirical data.

While the outcome is not efficiency or group payoff, the results suggest that punishment does not always improve group outcomes: if punishment is too costly or too weak, it can reduce cooperation. For prediction tasks, this implies that the effect of punishment on efficiency may be non-monotonic and sensitive to cost-effectiveness. However, because efficiency or group payoff is not reported, the findings should be used as qualitative, mechanistic guidance rather than quantitative prediction.

The effect is robust: even small punishment fines can substantially increase cooperation. The paper analyzes the spatial and network organization of strategies, showing that punishers can form protective clusters that shield cooperators from defectors. However, the paper does not report on group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, so the direct effect of punishment on efficiency cannot be determined.

For prediction tasks focused on efficiency in public goods games, the results are only indirectly informative: they suggest that punishment can increase cooperation, which may translate to higher efficiency, but the actual payoff consequences (including the cost of punishment) are not analyzed. The game structure is not a public goods game but a 2-person PD/SD, so transfer to PGG-like settings should be cautious.

- source: Sun, X. P., Han, L. F., Wang, M., Liu, S. X., & Shen, Y. (2023). Social exclusion with antisocial punishment in spatial public goods game. *Physics Letters A*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
