  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that introducing punishment by investors can induce a stable coexistence of punishing investors and trustworthy trustees in a hierarchical N-player trust game. The effectiveness of punishment in promoting trust depends on both the intensity of punishment and the fraction of investors in the population. For low punishment intensity, an intermediate fraction of investors is optimal for promoting trust, while for high punishment intensity, a higher fraction of investors is more effective. Increasing punishment intensity generally enlarges the attraction domain of the coexistence state, but only up to a point. The model does not directly report efficiency or group payoff, but interprets the stable coexistence of trust and trustworthiness as a positive social outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that punishment can promote trust and trustworthiness in a hierarchical trust game, with the effect moderated by punishment intensity and the fraction of investors. However, it does not directly report efficiency or group payoff, so its value for predicting efficiency outcomes in public goods games is indirect. The findings suggest that the presence and intensity of punishment, as well as the composition of the population, are important moderators of the effect of punishment on cooperative behavior. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper provides indirect support for the idea that punishment can improve group outcomes under certain conditions, but does not provide quantitative efficiency data.

There is an optimal punishment time window that maximizes the frequency of cooperation. Too short punishment fails to deter defection, while too long punishment can backfire and reduce cooperation. The intensity of punishment (how much payoffs are reduced for punished individuals) is the dominant factor in promoting cooperation. The model does not include a cost for punishment, and does not analyze group payoff, efficiency, or welfare. All results are based on the proportion of cooperators in the population.

The findings suggest that punishment can increase cooperation rates, especially with optimal duration and sufficient intensity, but the absence of punishment cost and lack of payoff analysis limit its direct applicability to predicting efficiency in public goods games. The model is adjacent to PGGs and may inform qualitative expectations about punishment's effect on cooperation, but not on efficiency or welfare. However, because the model does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used directly to predict efficiency outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment. The findings are most relevant for understanding the mechanisms by which migration and spatial structure interact with punishment, rather than for quantitative prediction of efficiency.

- source: Guan, K. X., Chen, Y. Y., Zheng, W. J., Zeng, L. L., & Ye, H. (2022). Costly signals can facilitate cooperation and punishment in the prisoner?s dilemma. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.

  findings: The study finds that in a prisoner's dilemma with costly signaling, punishment as a costly signal can become prevalent if the signal is strong and noise is low, but this does not necessarily increase cooperation. Without punishment, cooperation can dominate if the cost-benefit ratio is favorable and noise is low. The introduction of punishment (when its cost is converted into a strong costly signal) increases the frequency of punishment and reduces defection, but does not increase cooperation. The model does not report on efficiency or group payoff, so the effect of punishment on efficiency cannot be directly inferred.

The findings suggest that punishment can reduce defection and increase the prevalence of punishment strategies if the punishment signal is strong, but does not necessarily increase cooperation or efficiency. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency in public goods games, the relevance is limited and indirect, as the model is not a PGG and does not report efficiency outcomes.

- source: Deng, Z. H., Huang, Y. J., Gu, Z. Y., & Li-Gao (2018). Multigames with social punishment and the evolution of cooperation. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that introducing a punishment strategy (punishers who pay a cost to fine defectors) in a spatial multigame environment (mix of PD and SD) increases the fraction of cooperation (cooperators and punishers), especially when the sucker's payoff is large or when more players play the Snowdrift game. Punishers form compact clusters that help protect against defectors. The average payoff is shown to recover as cooperation increases, but efficiency (as a ratio to full cooperation) is not directly analyzed. The findings are based on agent-based simulations, not experiments or real human subjects.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the idea that punishment can increase cooperation and average payoff in spatial social dilemmas, especially when there is heterogeneity in game parameters. However, because the model is not a public goods game and does not report efficiency as defined for the prediction task, its value for predicting treatment efficiency in PGG-like environments is limited. The main outcomes are behavioral (cooperation rates), and the only payoff-related outcome is average payoff, which is not benchmarked to full cooperation. The punishment mechanism is explicit and parameterized, but the results are not directly transferable to PGG efficiency prediction.

- source: Xu, C., Ji, M., Yap, Y. J., Zheng, D. F., & Hui, P. M. (2011). Costly punishment and cooperation in the evolutionary snowdrift game. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that introducing costly punishment into the evolutionary snowdrift game can lead to two types of steady states: (1) a mixture of cooperators and punishers (with defectors eliminated), or (2) a mixture of cooperators and defectors (with punishers eliminated), depending on the cost-to-benefit ratio, punishment parameters, and initial conditions. The presence of punishers can enhance cooperation relative to the standard snowdrift game, but this is measured in terms of the frequency of cooperators, not group efficiency or total payoff. The model provides analytic and simulation results for the conditions under which defectors can or cannot invade a population with punishers.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the effect of punishment on cooperation in a two-player snowdrift game, which is adjacent to public goods games. The main outcomes are strategy frequencies, not efficiency or group payoff, so the results are not directly usable for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment in PGG-like environments. However, the finding that costly punishment can eliminate defectors and increase the frequency of cooperators under certain conditions suggests that punishment may increase efficiency in analogous settings, especially when the cost of punishment is low relative to its impact. The model's focus on initial conditions and parameter sensitivity may inform qualitative expectations about when punishment is more or less effective, but quantitative prediction of efficiency is not supported.

- source: Xu, C., Hui, P. M., Yu, Y. Y., & Gu, G. Q. (2009). Self-organized cooperative behavior and critical penalty in an evolving population. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in a population of agents playing a two-action game (cooperate or defect), increasing the penalty for defection increases the mean probability to cooperate. There is a critical penalty threshold, dependent on initial conditions, above which the population becomes fully cooperative. For populations with higher initial cooperation, less penalty is needed to achieve full cooperation, and in some cases, no penalty is required. The model also shows that after a population is trained to cooperate with penalty, the penalty can be relaxed without immediate loss of cooperation. The structure of the population (well-mixed vs. 2D lattice) affects the level of cooperation at intermediate penalties, with lattices supporting higher cooperation. However, the paper does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare as outcomes, so the findings are not directly about payoff-based efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that increasing the severity of punishment can increase cooperation rates in repeated social dilemma games, with a critical threshold for full cooperation. However, because the main outcome is the mean probability to cooperate and not group payoff or efficiency, the results cannot be directly used to predict efficiency outcomes in public goods games. The model is adjacent to PGGs and may inform qualitative expectations about the effect of punishment on cooperation, but not on efficiency. The findings suggest that punishment can be effective in increasing cooperation, especially when initial cooperation is low, and that population structure can moderate this effect. For the prediction task, this paper is best used as indirect, qualitative support for the idea that punishment can increase cooperation, but not as quantitative evidence for efficiency effects.

- source: Zhu, W. Q., Pan, Q. H., Song, S., & He, M. F. (2023). Effects of exposure-based reward and punishment on the evolution of cooperation in prisoner's dilemma game. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in an infinite well-mixed population, exposure-based punishment (and especially the combination of reward and punishment) can promote the evolution of cooperation, but only if the cost of exposure is less than the punishment for defection. Rewarding exposed cooperators alone is insufficient to promote cooperation. There are threshold and nonlinear effects: simultaneous reward and punishment is most effective, and there are optimal values for exposure cost and reward/punishment strength. The model identifies parameter regions where cooperation can be sustained, but all results are in terms of the fraction of cooperators, not group efficiency or payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that punishment (and especially combined with reward) can increase cooperation rates in a prisoner's dilemma setting with exposure-based mechanisms. However, it does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, so it cannot be directly used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. The model is adjacent to PGGs and focuses on cooperation rates, so its main value is as indirect support for the idea that punishment can promote cooperation under certain cost/benefit conditions. It does not provide quantitative predictions for efficiency or payoff outcomes.

- source: Niu, X. T., Xu, J. W., & Deng, Z. H. (2017). Conditional punishment resolves social dilemma in spatial network. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study demonstrates that introducing and increasing the probability of social punishment (punishers who pay a cost to penalize defectors) in a spatial prisoner's dilemma model leads to a higher steady-state density of cooperators. When the willingness to punish is high, defectors are eliminated and cooperation dominates. The mechanism is shown to be robust across a range of temptation-to-defect values. However, the paper does not report on group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, so its findings are limited to behavioral outcomes (cooperation rates and spatial patterns).
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that punishment increases cooperation rates in spatial prisoner's dilemma settings, but does not provide direct evidence about efficiency or payoff-based outcomes. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, the results are only indirectly informative: higher cooperation rates may imply higher efficiency, but the cost of punishment and its effect on total group payoff are not analyzed. The model is also not a public goods game, but an adjacent two-player game with spatial structure. Use this paper as indirect support for the behavioral effect of punishment, but not for quantitative efficiency predictions.

  findings: The paper finds that introducing a revenge-based costly punishment mechanism in the PDG on networks increases the frequency of cooperation, especially when the cost to the punisher is low and the cost to the punished is high. The effect is robust across network types (square lattice and small-world), cost-to-benefit ratios, and network sizes. However, because the main outcome is the frequency of cooperation and not efficiency or group payoff, the findings cannot be directly used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. The model structure and parameterization may inform mechanistic expectations, but quantitative prediction of efficiency outcomes is not possible from this paper alone.

- source: 
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that, contrary to standard game-theoretic equilibrium predictions, increasing penalties for law violations can induce temporary compliance (reduced violation rates) among drivers, even if the long-run equilibrium violation rate is unchanged. The system dynamics model shows that the path to equilibrium is slow and oscillatory, so transient effects of penalty increases are significant and may be policy-relevant. Automatic penalty management (adjusting penalties in response to observed violation rates) can further reduce the amplitude of behavioral fluctuations. Information delays between players can prolong or even prevent convergence to equilibrium, making penalty management more effective in stabilizing behavior. The findings are about behavioral compliance, not aggregate payoffs or efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not directly inform predictions about efficiency or group payoff in public-goods-game-like environments, as it models a two-player (police vs. driver) enforcement game with behavioral outcomes (violation and patrol rates) rather than group payoffs. However, it provides indirect evidence that punishment (penalty) increases can have significant transient effects on compliance, even if equilibrium analysis suggests no effect. For prediction tasks involving dynamic or transient effects of punishment, or where compliance rates are a proxy for efficiency, the findings suggest that punishment can be effective in the short run and that adaptive punishment management can stabilize behavior. The model is not a PGG or direct variant, so transfer to PGG efficiency predictions should be cautious and limited to qualitative insights about the dynamics of punishment and compliance.

- source: Zhang, M., Zhang, X., Qu, C. Q., Wang, G. H., & Lu, X. (2022). The combination of social reward and punishment is conducive to the cooperation and heterogeneity of social relations. *Chaos*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, reward_exists
  findings: The study finds that a combined reward-punishment mechanism (RPM) in a dynamic network prisoner's dilemma can significantly increase the average frequency of cooperation compared to either pure punishment or pure reward alone. Pure punishment only promotes cooperation under high temptation to defect, while pure reward can actually undermine cooperation. The combination of both, with appropriately tuned intensities, sustains high cooperation even under strong temptation to defect. The model also shows that the RPM leads to spontaneous emergence of network heterogeneity, with cooperators becoming influential hubs. However, the main outcome is cooperation frequency, not group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that the presence and calibration of both punishment and reward mechanisms can strongly affect cooperation rates in dynamic networked social dilemmas. However, because the main outcome is cooperation frequency rather than efficiency or group payoff, and the game is a dynamic network prisoner's dilemma rather than a standard public goods game, the findings should be transferred to efficiency prediction with caution. The results suggest that in environments where both punishment and reward are available and appropriately balanced, cooperation (and likely efficiency) can be sustained at higher levels than with either mechanism alone, especially under high temptation to defect. The paper does not provide direct quantitative predictions for efficiency or group payoff.

- source: 
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, reward_exists
  findings: The model demonstrates that increasing punishment for enterprises promotes their strict implementation of regulations, but with diminishing returns. Higher rewards to the public increase their willingness to supervise, but also slow the government's adoption of strict governance due to fiscal burden. Subsidies to enterprises incentivize strict implementation but may reduce the government's willingness to govern strictly if subsidies are too high. Lowering the cost of public supervision increases supervision rates. The ideal outcome (all parties cooperate) is achieved when punishment, rewards, and subsidies are high enough and supervision costs are low. However, the paper does not provide quantitative predictions of efficiency or group payoff, focusing instead on the evolution of behavioral strategies.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides qualitative theoretical support for the idea that punishment mechanisms can increase compliance (analogous to cooperation) in multi-actor public goods-like settings, especially when combined with rewards and low supervision costs. However, it does not provide direct evidence or quantitative predictions about efficiency or group payoff, nor does it use a standard PGG or report efficiency ratios. Its findings may inform the expected direction of punishment effects on compliance, but not the magnitude of efficiency changes. The model's structure and results are adjacent to the prediction task, offering indirect support for the positive effect of punishment on cooperative behavior, but not on efficiency per se.

- source: Chen, L., Sun, J. J., Li, K., & Li, Q. R. (2022). Research on the effectiveness of monitoring mechanism for yield to pedestrian based on system dynamics. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that: (1) Without external control, the system exhibits persistent oscillations in the probability of drivers yielding to pedestrians, and TMD monitoring disappears over time. (2) Introducing dynamic punishment (fines that increase with non-compliance) suppresses oscillations and increases compliance, but does not fully eliminate violations. (3) Combining dynamic punishment and reward (penalty-incentive control) leads to full compliance (all drivers yield) and eliminates the need for monitoring or pedestrian supervision. (4) Alternatively, a sufficiently high static monitoring rate by TMD (above a threshold) can also achieve full compliance. The results are framed in terms of behavioral probabilities and system stability, not aggregate efficiency or payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that dynamic punishment and/or reward mechanisms can stabilize and maximize compliance in a multi-agent system with monitoring and sanctioning, analogous to public goods games with punishment and reward. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot be used directly to predict efficiency outcomes. The findings are most useful for understanding the qualitative effect of punishment and reward on stabilizing cooperation and eliminating violations, especially in systems with endogenous monitoring and third-party enforcement. The model is adjacent to PGGs, but the outcome mapping to efficiency is indirect.

- source: Huo, H., & Liu, X. Y. (2024). Behavioral decision-making of government, agricultural product producers, and consumers on agricultural product quality and safety regulation in a digital environment. *Frontiers in Public Health*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that severe government penalties for non-compliance (punishment), sufficient incentives, and reduced costs for producers to build traceability systems are key to achieving stable cooperation among stakeholders. Depending on the relative size of these parameters, the system can stabilize in different equilibria: (Not regulate, Not build, Satisfied), (Regulate, Not build, Satisfied), or (Regulate, Build, Satisfied). The presence and strength of punishment (penalties) are critical in shifting the equilibrium toward higher compliance by producers. However, the outcomes are reported as stable strategy profiles, not as explicit efficiency or group payoff measures.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the prediction task by showing that the presence and severity of punishment (penalties) can shift the equilibrium from non-compliance to compliance in a multi-actor regulatory game. However, because the model does not report group efficiency or explicit payoff outcomes, it cannot be used to directly predict efficiency changes due to punishment in public-goods-like games. The findings suggest that increasing punishment can increase compliance, but the translation to efficiency or group payoff is not quantified. The model structure and parameter dependencies may inform qualitative expectations about the effect of punishment in similar multi-actor regulatory environments.

- source: Zhang, C. Y., Zhu, Y. Y., Chen, Z. Q., & Zhang, J. L. (2017). Punishment in the form of shared cost promotes altruism in the cooperative dilemma games. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in a multi-player Prisoner's Dilemma with punishment (where only cooperators punish and share the cost), the evolutionary outcome depends on the punishment parameters and group size. Higher punishment effectiveness (greater penalty to defectors) and lower cost to punishers promote the spread and fixation of cooperation. Larger group size and higher temptation to defect make cooperation harder to sustain. The model provides explicit conditions under which cooperation can dominate, coexist with defection, or be eliminated. The main outcome is the fraction of cooperators at equilibrium, not group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect but informative evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like games. While it does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, it shows that punishment with shared cost can promote the fixation of cooperation under certain parameter regimes, especially when punishment is effective and not too costly. The effect of group size (player_count) and temptation to defect (analogous to MPCR) are also analyzed. The results suggest that, all else equal, enabling punishment with shared cost is likely to increase cooperation rates (and thus efficiency) when punishment is strong and not too costly, but the effect is weaker or negative when punishment is weak or costly, or when group size is large. However, because the model outcome is the fraction of cooperators, not efficiency, the translation to efficiency must be inferred.

- source: Perc, M., Donnay, K., & Helbing, D. (2013). Understanding Recurrent Crime as System-Immanent Collective Behavior. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that increasing punishment or inspection does not always reduce crime; instead, the system exhibits complex, sometimes counterintuitive dynamics, including cyclic dominance and abrupt phase transitions between different equilibria (e.g., dominance of criminals, coexistence of criminals and inspectors, or cyclic coexistence of all three strategies). The effectiveness of punishment depends critically on the temptation to commit crime, the cost and incentive for inspection, and the structure of social interactions. The model demonstrates that crime can be recurrent and robust to increased punishment, especially when spatial structure and local interactions are considered. However, the paper does not report on efficiency or group payoff, so its findings are not directly translatable to predictions about group welfare or efficiency in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the effect of punishment on crime (and by analogy, on cooperation in social dilemmas) is highly sensitive to the interplay of temptation, punishment cost, and network structure, and that simple linear predictions (e.g., more punishment always increases efficiency) are likely to fail. However, because the paper does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. Its main value is in highlighting the potential for non-monotonic, phase-transition-like effects and the importance of considering spatial and network structure. For prediction tasks, this paper suggests caution in assuming monotonic or simple effects of punishment, especially in spatially structured or networked environments.

- source: Wang, G. C., Su, Q., & Wang, L. (2022). Evolution of cooperation with joint liability. *Journal of the Royal Society Interface*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that joint liability, when combined with costly punishment, strongly promotes the evolution of cooperation in a lending game. Punishment alone is insufficient to sustain cooperation, but joint liability provides incentives for individuals to punish defectors, leading to high levels of cooperation and low levels of defection. The condition for cooperation to evolve is relaxed from β−γ>4c (without joint liability) to β>γ (with joint liability and low tolerability for defection). The effect is robust to model extensions, including intragroup donation games (Prisoner's Dilemma). The abundance of cooperators and punishers is maximized at intermediate values of selection intensity, population size, and mutation rate. The model is adjacent to public goods games but differs in structure and outcome measures.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the combination of joint liability and costly punishment can greatly increase cooperation in group settings similar to, but not identical with, public goods games. The findings suggest that punishment is much more effective at promoting cooperation when joint liability is present, and that the threshold for punishment to be effective is lower. However, because the model is not a standard PGG and does not report efficiency as a ratio of realized to maximum group payoff, the results should be transferred to PGG efficiency predictions with caution. The paper is most informative for understanding the mechanisms by which punishment and group-level liability interact, and for predicting that punishment will be more effective in environments with joint liability or similar group-level consequences.

- source: Wang, J., Liu, Z., Xu, Y., & Li, X. P. (2025). Dynamic evolution in multi-player networked trust games with graded punishment. *Chaos*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that introducing a graded punishment mechanism in a networked trust game simulation can effectively reduce untrustworthy behavior and increase the density of investment and trust. The effect of punishment depends on both the cost to punishers and the severity of punishment. Graded punishment outperforms traditional punishment in eliminating untrustworthy agents and increasing simulated group trust and total wealth. However, if punishment costs are too high, investment and trust collapse. The results are sensitive to network topology, with regular lattices supporting higher total wealth than scale-free networks. All findings are based on agent-based simulations, not real-world or laboratory experiments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, theory-based evidence that graded punishment mechanisms can increase simulated group trust and total wealth in a networked trust game, especially when punishment is not too costly. However, because the study is simulation-based and uses a trust game variant (not a standard PGG), its findings should be interpreted as suggestive rather than directly predictive for real-world or experimental PGGs. The results highlight the importance of the cost and effectiveness of punishment, and suggest that graded (rather than uniform) punishment may be more effective at sustaining cooperation and group payoff in networked environments. The paper does not provide empirical data for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions, but its modeling results may inform hypotheses about the role of punishment cost, punishment effectiveness, and network structure.

  findings: The presence of empty sites in a lattice-structured population (spatial game) can promote the evolution of cooperation and does not reduce the effect of costly punishment, especially when using a score-dependent viability updating rule. In well-mixed populations, empty sites do not promote cooperation by punishment. The evolutionary dynamics with empty sites resemble those of a fertility (death-birth) model. The effect of punishment depends on both the cost of being punished and the benefit from cooperation in the presence of empty sites. The paper does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes, but rather which strategies dominate under different conditions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for how spatial structure, population density (empty sites), and updating rules may moderate the effect of punishment on cooperation. However, it does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes, so it cannot be used directly to predict efficiency changes due to punishment. It is most useful for understanding mechanisms and for contextualizing the role of spatial structure and population density in evolutionary games with punishment. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper is only indirectly informative.

- source: Sekiguchi, T., & Nakamaru, M. (2009). Effect of the presence of empty sites on the evolution of cooperation by costly punishment in spatial games. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The presence of empty sites in spatially structured populations (lattices) promotes the evolution of cooperation and does not reduce the effect of costly punishment. In contrast, in completely mixing populations, empty sites reduce the effectiveness of punishment in promoting cooperation. The evolutionary success of strategies depends on the benefit of cooperation, the cost and effectiveness of punishment, and the density of empty sites. The model does not report group efficiency or total payoff, but rather which strategies dominate under different parameter regimes. The findings suggest that spatial structure and population density (via empty sites) are important moderators of the evolutionary dynamics of punishment and cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence for the prediction task. It shows that spatial structure (analogous to networked or local interaction in PGGs) and the presence of empty sites (analogous to non-participation or dropout) can strongly moderate the effect of punishment on cooperation. In spatial settings, punishment remains effective even with empty sites, while in well-mixed settings, empty sites undermine punishment's effectiveness. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, its direct value for predicting efficiency outcomes is limited. The results are most useful for understanding when punishment is likely to promote cooperation (and by extension, possibly efficiency) depending on spatial structure and participation rates, but do not provide quantitative efficiency effects.

The model's focus on network structure, punishment, and cooperation may inform qualitative expectations about the direction of efficiency effects when punishment is enabled, but does not provide direct predictive data for the downstream task.

  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: reward_exists, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper finds that TPP is used as a signal of trustworthiness when it is the only available signal, leading to greater trust and reciprocation in a subsequent trust game. When helping is also available as a signal, the use and signaling value of TPP are reduced, while helping remains a strong signal. The results support a reputational account of TPP, showing that people may punish to signal their character rather than solely to deter selfishness. However, the study does not report on group efficiency, welfare, or total payoffs in public goods or similar games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the signaling value and frequency of punishment depend on the availability and informativeness of alternative prosocial signals (like helping). However, it does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on group efficiency or total payoffs in public-goods-game-like environments. Its main relevance is in highlighting that the context and available signals can moderate the behavioral use of punishment, which may indirectly affect efficiency in other designs. For direct prediction of efficiency outcomes, this paper is only indirectly informative.

- source: Wang, Q. E., Lai, W., Ding, M. M., & Qiu, Q. (2022). Research on Cooperative Behavior of Green Technology Innovation in Construction Enterprises Based on Evolutionary Game. *Buildings*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that stronger punishment mechanisms (higher penalty and indirect punishment) increase the likelihood that both parties will adopt positive cooperation strategies, thus reducing opportunistic behavior. Similarly, higher willingness to cooperate, greater positive spillover (sharing), and more favorable income distribution also promote positive cooperation. However, the outcomes are reported in terms of the probability or stability of cooperation strategies, not in terms of group efficiency or total payoff. The model is adjacent to public goods games, as it involves cooperation with spillovers and punishment, but is tailored to the context of construction enterprise innovation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the prediction task: it shows that increasing punishment intensity (both direct and indirect) makes positive cooperation more likely in a repeated game with spillovers, which is structurally similar to public goods games. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, so its value is in supporting the mechanism that punishment can deter defection and promote cooperation under certain parameter regimes. The findings are most useful for understanding how punishment, sharing, and income distribution interact to affect cooperation, but less directly useful for quantitative prediction of efficiency outcomes.

- source: Jones, M. A. (1999). The effect of punishment duration of trigger strategies and quasifinite continuation probabilities for Prisoners' Dilemmas. *International Journal of Game Theory*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that the ability to sustain cooperation in indefinitely repeated Prisoners' Dilemma games depends critically on the duration of punishment (in trigger strategies) and the properties of the continuation probability sequence. Longer punishment durations (including the grim trigger) make it easier to support cooperation in equilibrium. If the continuation probability is 'quasifinite' (i.e., its geometric average tends to zero), then no amount of punishment can sustain cooperation, and only the noncooperative equilibrium exists. The paper provides explicit mathematical conditions for when cooperation is possible, and shows how to compute the minimum punishment duration needed for cooperation given the discount factor and continuation probability. These results are framed in terms of equilibrium existence and structure, not direct efficiency or payoff outcomes, but imply that punishment can enable higher group payoffs when the conditions are met.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical guidance on how punishment duration and the probability of future interaction (continuation probability) affect the possibility of sustaining cooperation in repeated social dilemmas. For the downstream prediction task, the results suggest that increasing punishment duration (severity) generally increases the likelihood that punishment will improve efficiency, provided the continuation probability is not too low. If the effective game horizon is short (quasifinite), punishment will not improve efficiency. While the analysis is for Prisoners' Dilemma rather than public goods games, the logic and mathematical structure are closely related and can inform predictions about when punishment will be effective in increasing efficiency. However, the paper does not provide quantitative predictions of efficiency changes, only conditions for the existence of cooperative equilibria.

- source: Hu, K. P., Tao, Y. W., Ma, Y. J., & Shi, L. (2021). Peer pressure induced punishment resolves social dilemma on interdependent networks. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that introducing a peer-pressure-like punishment mechanism—where players with inconsistent strategies across interdependent network layers have their payoffs reduced—can promote cooperation in a spatial prisoner's dilemma. The effect of punishment is non-monotonic: moderate punishment maximizes cooperation, while too little or too much punishment is less effective. The effect is robust to changes in network structure (lattice, small-world, random) and the degree of payoff interdependence between layers. However, the paper does not report on group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare; the main focus is on cooperation rates and the spatial distribution of strategies.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that punishment mechanisms can increase cooperation rates in social dilemma settings, especially when the punishment is moderate. However, because the model is a spatial prisoner's dilemma (not a public goods game or direct variant), and because the main outcome is cooperation rate rather than efficiency or group payoff, the findings should be transferred to efficiency prediction with caution. The results suggest that moderate punishment is likely to be most effective for promoting cooperation, but do not provide direct evidence about the effect on group efficiency or total payoff. The design dimensions most relevant here are the punishment cost (modeled as a reduction in payoff for inconsistent pairs) and the degree of interdependence between players' payoffs. The absence of direct efficiency or payoff outcomes limits the paper's direct utility for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments.

- source: Bruner, J. P. (2013). POLICING EPISTEMIC COMMUNITIES. *Episteme-a Journal of Individual and Social Epistemology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that peer-policing can sustain high levels of honest reporting in a decentralized community if and only if punishment is opportunistic—i.e., if those who police and catch cheaters are rewarded. When the benefit of catching cheaters and the punishment for being caught are both high (with the benefit exceeding the punishment), the population stabilizes with a majority of honest reporters and a minority of policers and cheaters. If punishment is purely altruistic (costly to the punisher), honesty cannot be sustained and the population converges to all cheating. The model also shows that reducing the cost of policing (e.g., by rewarding replication) or increasing the punishment for cheating can further increase the prevalence of honesty. However, the model does not directly report on group efficiency or total welfare, focusing instead on the behavioral dynamics of honesty, policing, and cheating.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that punishment can sustain prosocial behavior (honest reporting) in social dilemmas, but only if the incentive structure rewards punishers. While the model is not a direct public goods game, it is structurally similar and thus informative for predicting when punishment will be effective in PGG-like environments. The key implication is that punishment will only improve group outcomes (e.g., honesty, cooperation) if the cost-benefit structure makes policing individually rational—i.e., if the reward for punishing exceeds the cost and the punishment for being caught cheating exceeds the benefit of cheating. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used to directly predict efficiency outcomes. Its main value is in highlighting the importance of the incentive structure for the effectiveness of punishment.

- source: Steimanis, I., Hofmann, R., Mbidzo, M., & Vollan, B. (2020). When female leaders believe that men make better leaders: Empowerment in community-based water management in rural Namibia. *Journal of Rural Studies*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The introduction of conditional neutral punishment—where agents punish neighbors with the opposite strategy if their own payoff is below the neighborhood average—substantially increases the fraction of cooperators in a spatial prisoner's dilemma game. The effect is robust to variations in the temptation to defect and the cost and effectiveness of punishment. Cooperative punishers form protective clusters that shield cooperators, while antisocial punishment (defectors punishing cooperators) is largely ineffective. The main outcome is the fraction of cooperation, not group payoff or efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that conditional neutral punishment can increase cooperation rates in spatial social dilemmas, but it does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, the results are only indirectly informative: higher cooperation rates typically—but not always—imply higher efficiency, especially in prisoner's dilemma-like settings. However, without explicit efficiency or payoff data, the magnitude and reliability of efficiency gains from punishment in this design cannot be directly inferred. The spatial structure, conditionality of punishment, and the absence of communication or reward mechanisms are important contextual features.

- source: Li, X., & Cao, L. (2009). Largest Laplacian eigenvalue predicts the emergence of costly punishment in the evolutionary ultimatum game on networks. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in the evolutionary ultimatum game on networks, there is a critical value of the population's selfishness degree (the share of the resource claimed by the proposer) above which a fraction of players become pure costly punishers (always reject offers). This critical value is analytically predicted by the inverse of the largest Laplacian eigenvalue of the network. The emergence of costly punishment is thus determined by network topology. The study also shows that pure punishers can have a higher average payoff ratio than others, especially as selfishness increases, but group-level efficiency or welfare is not directly analyzed. The findings are based on theoretical analysis and numerical simulations on various network types (scale-free, small-world, real-world collaboration networks).
  prediction_guidance: This paper is not directly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their close variants, as it studies the ultimatum game (an adjacent but distinct paradigm) and focuses on the emergence of costly punishment rather than on group efficiency or total payoff. The main outcome is the fraction of pure punishers as a function of network structure and selfishness degree. While the paper provides insight into how network topology affects the threshold for costly punishment, it does not provide data or analysis on how punishment affects group efficiency or welfare in a public-goods-like environment. Thus, its value for the downstream prediction task is limited to theoretical context and mechanism discussion.

- source: Gao, S. P., Wu, T., Nie, S. L., & Wang, L. (2015). Emergence of parochial altruism in well-mixed populations of multiple groups. *Physics Letters A*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that parochial altruism is more likely to emerge and be favored by selection when intergroup punishment is more effective (higher fine-to-cost ratio), when there are more groups (higher assortment), and when mutation rates are low. Affiliation (belonging to more groups) makes parochial altruism less likely for low mutation, but more likely for high mutation. The model provides explicit conditions (in terms of benefit, cost, punishment fine, and punishment cost) for when parochial altruism is favored. However, the main outcomes are about the evolutionary abundance of strategies, not about group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the idea that punishment can promote the emergence of cooperation-like strategies in group-structured populations, especially when punishment is effective and group assortment is high. However, because the model is theoretical and focuses on evolutionary stability of strategies rather than direct efficiency or group payoff, it does not provide quantitative predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. The results suggest that punishment is more likely to be beneficial (in terms of promoting cooperation-like behavior) when punishment is effective and group structure is strong, but the translation to efficiency outcomes in experimental PGGs is indirect.

- source: Liu, J. Q., & Zhang, Q. W. (2025). Evolutionary dynamics of a probabilistic punishment mechanism with environmental feedback in regular networked Prisoner's Dilemma. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that a probabilistic punishment mechanism with environmental feedback can lead to three types of system states: (1) stable full defection with low probability of effective punishment, (2) stable full cooperation with high probability of effective punishment, and (3) bi-stability where both outcomes are possible depending on initial conditions. Increasing the speed of environmental feedback (i.e., how quickly the probability of effective punishment updates in response to behavior) can shift the system from defection to cooperation in the bi-stable regime. The model highlights the importance of punishment effectiveness, punishment cost, network degree (number of neighbors), and feedback speed in shaping cooperation dynamics. However, the paper does not report group efficiency or payoff outcomes directly.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical insights into how probabilistic punishment and environmental feedback affect cooperation in a networked Prisoner's Dilemma, which is adjacent to public goods games. It suggests that punishment mechanisms with high effectiveness and fast feedback can promote cooperation, especially in structured populations. However, because the outcomes are reported in terms of cooperation frequency and not efficiency or group payoff, the paper offers only indirect support for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in public-goods-like games. The findings may inform the expected direction of efficiency changes (via increased cooperation), but quantitative predictions for efficiency require caution.

- source: Wang, Y. F., & Meng, X. Z. (2023). Evolutionary game dynamics of cooperation in prisoner?s dilemma with time delay. *Mathematical Biosciences and Engineering*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in a 2-player prisoner's dilemma, adding a penalty for mutual defection (punishment) leads to stable coexistence of cooperators and defectors when the penalty exceeds the cost of cooperation (a > c). The equilibrium proportion of cooperators increases with the penalty size. When time delays are introduced, the system can undergo a Hopf bifurcation: for small delays, the equilibrium is stable, but for delays above a critical threshold (which decreases as penalty increases), the system oscillates between cooperation and defection. Adding mutation (random switching between strategies) slightly reduces the equilibrium proportion of cooperators, but does not fundamentally change the qualitative dynamics. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, focusing instead on the frequency of cooperation and the stability of these frequencies.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that increasing the severity of punishment for defection in a prisoner's dilemma increases the equilibrium proportion of cooperators, which is typically associated with higher group payoff in such games. However, the paper does not directly report efficiency or group payoff, so the findings must be interpreted as indirect evidence. The results suggest that, in similar 2-player games, enabling punishment (with sufficient severity) will increase cooperation rates, and thus likely improve efficiency relative to the no-punishment baseline. However, if time delays are present and large, the system may become unstable and oscillate, which could reduce average efficiency. The findings are most relevant for settings with 2 players, no communication, continuous contribution/strategy choice, and deterministic evolutionary dynamics. The results are less directly transferable to standard public goods games with more players, explicit payoff reporting, or experimental data.

- source: Zhang, Y. L., & Fu, F. (2018). Strategy intervention for the evolution of fairness. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the evolution of fairness in the Ultimatum Game is promoted by the presence of spiteful strategies (which reject low or unequal offers, i.e., costly punishment) and inhibited by altruistic strategies (which accept any offer). The introduction of non-monotonic rejection (rejecting both low and high offers) further helps fairness overcome selfishness. In group-structured populations, larger population size increases the frequency of altruism and decreases the frequency of selfishness and spitefulness, but does not change the dominance of fairness. Migration inhibits the evolution of selfishness and fairness, but promotes altruism and, with sufficient migration, spitefulness. Mutation has non-monotonic effects, with intermediate mutation maximizing fairness and selfishness, and minimizing spitefulness. The main dependent variable is the frequency of each strategy, not group payoff or efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the role of costly punishment (rejection) in promoting fairness in strategic environments similar to the Ultimatum Game, which is adjacent to public goods games. It does not report efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. However, it supports the idea that the availability of costly punishment can increase the prevalence of fairness-oriented behavior, especially when non-monotonic punishment is possible. The findings are most relevant for understanding how punishment mechanisms affect the evolutionary stability of fairness, not for predicting group efficiency outcomes.

- source: Pothos, E. M., Perry, G., Corr, P. J., Matthew, M. R., & Busemeyer, J. R. (2011). Understanding cooperation in the Prisoner's Dilemma game. *Personality and Individual Differences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that higher punishment severity, higher disguise cost, lower cost-to-benefit ratio, and larger group size all promote the extinction of defectors and the prevalence of cooperation (cooperators and punishers). When these parameters are less favorable, the system remains in a mixed state with both cooperators and defectors. The model shows that the system typically evolves to a state with only two strategies (either cooperators and punishers, or cooperators and defectors), and the boundaries between these regimes are mapped as functions of the key parameters. The results are robust to both deterministic replicator dynamics and stochastic mutation-selection dynamics in finite populations.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect but informative evidence about how punishment parameters (cost, effectiveness), group size, and cost-to-benefit ratio affect the prevalence of cooperation in an N-person social dilemma with punishment. While the model is for the snowdrift game (not a standard PGG), and the main outcomes are behavioral frequencies rather than efficiency, the results suggest that increasing punishment severity, increasing group size, and increasing the cost for defectors to avoid punishment all make punishment more effective at promoting cooperation. However, the paper does not report group efficiency or total payoff ratios, so direct prediction of efficiency outcomes is not possible. The findings are most useful for understanding qualitative moderators of punishment effectiveness in related social dilemmas.

- source: Liu, W. W., & Yang, J. N. (2018). The Evolutionary Game Theoretic Analysis for Sustainable Cooperation Relationship of Collaborative Innovation Network in Strategic Emerging Industries. *Sustainability*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that government intervention through incentives and punishments can significantly influence the frequency of positive cooperation between enterprises and URIs in collaborative innovation networks. When the sum of incentives and punishments exceeds the total additional cost (TAC) of cooperation, positive cooperation becomes stable. If incentives and punishments are too low, negative cooperation prevails. The model does not report group payoffs, efficiency, or welfare, but focuses on the conditions for stable cooperation strategies.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the prediction task by showing that punishment (and incentives) can increase the frequency of cooperative behavior in a multi-agent innovation network, but it does not quantify effects on efficiency or group payoff. The model is adjacent to public goods games, as it involves free-riding and cooperation, but the outcomes are behavioral (strategy frequencies) rather than payoff-based. The findings suggest that, in similar environments, increasing punishment and incentives above a threshold can shift the system toward more cooperation, which may imply higher efficiency, but this is not directly modeled or measured.

- source: Woodcock, S., & Heath, J. (2002). The robustness of altruism as an evolutionary strategy. *Biology & Philosophy*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that punishment mechanisms, such as enforcement and ostracism, dramatically increase the robustness of altruism in evolutionary simulations of the prisoner's dilemma. While group selection and imitation can help sustain altruism under certain conditions (e.g., small group size, high imitation rates), punishment allows altruism to persist in much larger groups and under a wider range of conditions. The key mechanism is that punishment increases correlation among altruists, allowing them to interact more with each other and less with defectors. However, the paper does not report group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare as outcomes; it focuses on the evolutionary persistence of altruism.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that punishment mechanisms can greatly increase the persistence of altruism (cooperation) in evolutionary environments similar to the prisoner's dilemma. However, because the main outcome is the proportion of altruists rather than group efficiency or payoff, the findings are only indirectly relevant to predicting efficiency in public goods games. The results suggest that, all else equal, adding punishment mechanisms is likely to increase cooperation and thus could plausibly increase efficiency, but the paper does not quantify this effect in payoff terms. The design dimensions most relevant here are the presence and effectiveness of punishment, group size, and the structure of interaction (e.g., group partitioning, imitation).

- source: Traxler, C., & Winter, J. (2012). Survey evidence on conditional norm enforcement. *European Journal of Political Economy*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that both punishment and reputation can sustain norm compliance, but their roles differ depending on the distribution of social motivation in the population. When reputation effects are weak, higher levels of punishment are needed to enforce the norm. When reputation effects are strong, norms can be sustained with little or no punishment. The model shows two stable equilibria: one with high compliance (if initial compliance is above a critical threshold) and one with low compliance (if initial compliance is below the threshold). The probability of punishment increases as the benefit-cost ratio for punishers decreases (i.e., as the psychological cost of punishing decreases). The interaction of punishment and reputation provides a robust mechanism for norm stability.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that punishment can increase norm compliance, especially when reputation effects are weak. However, it does not provide quantitative predictions about efficiency or group payoff, nor does it specify the effects of game design dimensions such as MPCR, player count, or number of rounds. The model is adjacent to public goods games but is not directly parameterized in terms of efficiency. For prediction tasks, the main insight is that punishment is more effective at sustaining compliance when reputation incentives are weak, and that the two mechanisms can substitute for each other to some extent. The paper does not inform how punishment affects efficiency or payoff in public goods games with specific design features.

- source: Wang, X. Y., Zhang, L., Du, X. R., & Sun, Y. L. (2017). Evolving cooperation in spatial population with punishment by using PSO algorithm. *Natural Computing*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds
  findings: The study finds that introducing punishment in spatial evolutionary games (n-player IPD and ISD) increases the proportion of cooperators and punishers, and decreases the proportion of defectors and loners, especially at lower cost-to-benefit ratios. The effect of punishment is stronger in the ISD game, where defection would otherwise dominate. Larger group sizes and higher cost-to-benefit ratios reduce cooperation. The network structure (regular, Moore, Von Neumann, small-world, complete) and neighborhood size also affect cooperation rates. The length of history record (memory) has a non-monotonic effect on cooperation. However, the study does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, so the direct effect of punishment on efficiency cannot be determined.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that punishment can increase cooperation rates in spatial evolutionary games, and that the effect is moderated by group size, cost-to-benefit ratio, and network structure. However, because the outcomes are not efficiency or payoff-based, the findings should be used with caution for predicting efficiency. The results suggest that, in similar spatial games, enabling punishment is likely to increase cooperation, especially in smaller groups and at lower cost-to-benefit ratios, but the effect on efficiency is not directly established.

- source: Perc, M., & Szolnoki, A. (2015). A double-edged sword: Benefits and pitfalls of heterogeneous punishment in evolutionary inspection games. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that introducing heterogeneous punishment (punishers who invest different amounts in sanctioning) can reduce the prevalence of second-order free-riders (ordinary people) compared to uniform punishment, but this does not always translate into lower crime rates. In some parameter regions, uniform punishment is as effective or more effective at reducing crime. The system exhibits complex phase transitions and cyclic dominance, which maintain the coexistence of criminals, punishers, and ordinary people. The main obstacle to eliminating crime is the ability of ordinary people to free-ride on the efforts of punishers. The results are presented as fractions of strategies in the population, not as efficiency or total group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence relevant to the prediction of punishment effects in public-goods-like environments, especially regarding the role of punishment heterogeneity and second-order free-riding. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, so it cannot be used directly to predict changes in efficiency when punishment is enabled. The findings suggest that the presence of second-order free-riders and the structure of punishment (uniform vs. heterogeneous) can have complex, non-monotonic effects on cooperation and norm enforcement, which may inform qualitative expectations about efficiency in related games. The results highlight the importance of considering strategy diversity and cyclic dominance in predicting the effects of punishment, but quantitative predictions about efficiency require additional evidence.

- source: Bausch, A. W. (2014). Evolving intergroup cooperation. *Computational and Mathematical Organization Theory*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper finds that in-group policing is highly effective at promoting inter-group cooperation in an evolutionary Prisoner's Dilemma model, with high levels of CC outcomes (over 80% in many runs). In contrast, collective sanctions alone do not increase cooperation above baseline and can even reduce it when combined with in-group policing, especially when the sanction period is long. The model does not report efficiency or group payoff, but the behavioral results suggest that agent-level punishment is more effective than group-level punishment for sustaining cooperation. The findings are robust across a range of parameterizations, but are limited to behavioral outcomes (cooperation rates) rather than direct payoff or efficiency measures.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in environments structurally similar to a repeated Prisoner's Dilemma with group structure, agent-level punishment (in-group policing) is much more effective at sustaining cooperation than group-level punishment (collective sanctions). However, because the model does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, and is not a public goods game, its direct predictive value for efficiency outcomes in PGG-like environments is limited. The results are most useful for understanding the behavioral mechanisms by which punishment can promote or hinder cooperation, and for anticipating that group-level punishment may not translate into higher efficiency or payoff. For the downstream prediction task, this paper suggests that the presence of in-group policing (individualized punishment) is likely to have a more positive effect on cooperation than collective sanctions, but does not provide quantitative efficiency data.

- source: Shi, Y. T., Liu, B., Kou, X. L., & Han, X. (2014). Punishment and Feedback Mechanism for the Evolution Game on Small-World Network Based on Varying Topology. *Mathematical Problems in Engineering*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds
  findings: The study finds that a punishment and feedback mechanism—whereby neighbors of frequent defectors break links with them and form new links with each other—can help maintain a stable fraction of cooperators in a dynamic small-world network playing an n-round Prisoner's Dilemma. The mechanism is robust to the number of rounds played, but its effectiveness diminishes as the average degree (connectivity) and network size increase. The main reported outcome is the fraction of cooperators, not efficiency or group payoff. The model is not a public goods game but is related in its focus on cooperation and punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their close variants. Its main relevance is in showing that punishment mechanisms based on breaking social ties can help sustain cooperation in networked Prisoner's Dilemma settings, but the outcome is measured as the fraction of cooperators, not group efficiency or payoff. The model is adjacent to PGGs, and the findings may be indirectly informative for understanding how network structure and punishment interact to support cooperation, but they do not provide quantitative or directly transferable evidence for efficiency outcomes in PGG-like environments.

- source: Bshary, R., & Bronstein, J. L. (2011). A General Scheme to Predict Partner Control Mechanisms in Pairwise Cooperative Interactions Between Unrelated Individuals. *Ethology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  findings: The paper provides a decision-tree framework linking specific game structures (defined by investment, payoff structure, symmetry, repetition, and partner choice) to the partner control mechanisms that can stabilize cooperation. Punishment is predicted to be a viable control mechanism in repeated interactions without partner choice, especially when both players can invest and defect. The framework also highlights the roles of reward, sanctions, partner switching, and indirect reciprocity in other game structures. The review does not provide empirical or quantitative evidence on efficiency or payoff effects of punishment, but it does clarify the conditions under which punishment is theoretically expected to promote cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is useful for identifying which game structures are theoretically compatible with punishment as a mechanism for stabilizing cooperation, but it does not provide quantitative or empirical evidence on the efficiency or payoff effects of punishment in public goods or similar games. For the downstream prediction task, the framework can help map which design dimensions (e.g., repeated interactions, partner choice, symmetry) are necessary for punishment to be a relevant mechanism, but it cannot inform the magnitude or direction of efficiency changes. The paper is most useful for contextualizing when punishment is likely to be effective, not for predicting efficiency outcomes.

- source: Bingham, P. M. (1999). Human uniqueness: A general theory. *Quarterly Review of Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=N/R | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The central finding is that coalitional enforcement—cooperative, low-cost punishment of defectors—was necessary and sufficient for the evolution of large-scale, stable cooperation among unrelated individuals in humans. The theory predicts that as the efficiency and scale of coalitional enforcement increased (via technological and social monitoring advances), so did the extent and complexity of human cooperation. The paper discusses how this mechanism underlies the emergence of language, culture, large political units, and rapid technological progress. However, the paper does not provide direct evidence or quantitative findings about efficiency or payoffs in public goods game-like environments; rather, it offers a theoretical framework for understanding the evolutionary roots of cooperation and punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that punishment (specifically, coalitional or group-based punishment) can enable and stabilize high levels of cooperation among non-kin, by reducing the individual cost of enforcement and making defection unprofitable. For the downstream prediction task, the paper suggests that the presence and efficiency of punishment mechanisms are critical moderators of group cooperation and, by extension, group efficiency. However, because the paper does not report empirical or experimental data on efficiency or payoffs in public goods games, it cannot be used to calibrate quantitative predictions. Its main value is in supporting the general principle that more efficient, lower-cost, and more widely distributed punishment mechanisms should be associated with higher group cooperation and efficiency, all else equal.

- source: Chowdhury, S. N., Kundu, S., Perc, M., & Ghosh, D. (2021). Complex evolutionary dynamics due to punishment and free space in ecological multi-games. *Proceedings of the Royal Society A-mathematical Physical and Engineering Sciences*.

  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the inclusion of punishment as a strategy can promote the coexistence of cooperation and punishment, and can reduce the prevalence of defectors under certain parameter regimes. The cost of punishment, the temptation to defect, and the probability of playing the Prisoner's Dilemma versus the Snowdrift game all modulate the long-term fractions of cooperators, punishers, and defectors. The model exhibits multi-stability, oscillatory, and chaotic dynamics, with the possibility of cyclic dominance among strategies. However, the paper does not report on efficiency or group payoff, focusing instead on the dynamical and stationary properties of the system.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical insights into how punishment can affect the prevalence of cooperation and defection in evolutionary games, but does not provide direct evidence on efficiency or group payoff. Its results may inform qualitative expectations about the conditions under which punishment can sustain cooperation or lead to complex dynamics, but cannot be used to quantitatively predict efficiency outcomes in public goods games. The model is adjacent to PGGs, as it is based on two-person games (PD and SD) rather than multi-person public goods games, and the outcomes are reported as fractions of strategies rather than payoffs or efficiency.

  findings: The study finds that the inclusion of punishment as a strategy can, depending on parameter values (notably the cost of punishment and the temptation to defect), lead to the coexistence of punishers and defectors, dominance of punishers, or extinction of all strategies. Time delay can induce oscillatory or chaotic dynamics, and the ecological 'free space' variable can support the survival of cooperation-related strategies. The fate of cooperation, punishment, and defection is highly sensitive to parameter values and initial conditions. However, the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, focusing instead on the population dynamics of strategies.
