However, it does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency or group payoff outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment. The results may inform qualitative expectations about the conditions under which punishment can suppress defection or support cooperation, but cannot be used for quantitative prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment in PGG-like environments.

- source: Chowdhury, S. N., Kundu, S., Banerjee, J., Perc, M., & Ghosh, D. (2021). Eco-evolutionary dynamics of cooperation in the presence of policing. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The model shows that the introduction of punishment (policing) as a third strategy in a repeated PD game can lead to stable coexistence or oscillatory dominance cycles among cooperators, defectors, and punishers, depending on ecological and game parameters. The strength of punishment, temptation to defect, and ecological benefits (from 'free space') all modulate which strategies persist. Moderate punishment can suppress defectors and promote cooperation, but high temptation to defect can still undermine cooperation. The model also demonstrates that initial conditions and parameter choices can lead to multistability, with different attractors (e.g., dominance by one strategy, coexistence, or extinction). The focus is on the eco-evolutionary dynamics of strategy frequencies, not on group efficiency or total welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that punishment can promote the persistence of cooperation in social dilemma settings, but the results are framed in terms of population dynamics (strategy frequencies and coexistence) rather than efficiency or group payoff. The model is based on a repeated PD game, not a public goods game, and does not report efficiency as defined by total group payoff relative to full cooperation. Therefore, while the findings support the idea that punishment can help sustain cooperation under certain conditions, they do not provide direct quantitative guidance for predicting efficiency changes in PGG-like environments with or without punishment. The results may be useful for understanding qualitative moderators (e.g., the importance of punishment strength, temptation, ecological context), but are not directly transferable to the efficiency prediction task.

- source: Li, H. Y. (2017). Evolutionary Game Analysis of Emergency Management of the Middle Route of South-to-North Water Diversion Project. *Water Resources Management*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost
  findings: Theoretical and numerical analysis show that: (1) Local governments are more likely to show strong willingness to implement emergency policies if the profit from doing so exceeds the increased cost; (2) To avoid all local governments selecting weak cooperation, the central authority (MWR) should increase penalties for weak willingness, making the penalty greater than the cost saved by weak cooperation. The model demonstrates how the strength of punishment and the cost/benefit structure affect the evolution of cooperation, but does not directly quantify efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the idea that increasing punishment (penalties) for non-cooperation can increase the willingness to cooperate in multi-agent settings similar to public goods games. However, it does not provide direct evidence on efficiency or group payoff, nor does it report empirical results. The findings are most useful for understanding the mechanism by which punishment can shift equilibrium behavior toward greater cooperation, which may be associated with higher efficiency in analogous settings. For prediction tasks, the paper suggests that higher punishment effectiveness and costliness of non-cooperation can promote cooperative equilibria, but does not provide quantitative estimates of efficiency change.

- source: Gavrilets, S. (2020). The dynamics of injunctive social norms. *Evolutionary Human Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent

  dimensions: punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that the presence of punishment (active disapproval) increases the stability and prevalence of norm compliance, can create multiple stable equilibria, and increases the complexity of group behavioral dynamics. Heterogeneity in individual values and costs leads to diverse group outcomes. Punishment stabilizes cooperative equilibria and is mainly administered by those with high norm internalization. The models are general and can be applied to a range of social norm contexts, including public-goods-like settings, but do not directly model or report efficiency or group payoff as an outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that punishment mechanisms can stabilize norm compliance and cooperative behavior, especially in heterogeneous populations. However, it does not provide quantitative predictions about efficiency or group payoff, nor does it report empirical data. For the downstream prediction task, the paper suggests that the presence and cost of punishment, as well as heterogeneity in values and beliefs, are important moderators of the effect of punishment on group behavior. The models are adjacent to public goods games but do not map directly onto the efficiency prediction task.

- source: Jaffe, K. (2008). Evolution of Shame as an Adaptation to Social Punishment and its Contribution to Social Cohesiveness. *Complexity*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that shame can evolve as an adaptation to social punishment, increasing pro-social behavior (generosity) after punishment. When punishment is present (especially if costless to the punisher) or when shame is present, populations maintain higher levels of generosity and a balance between punishers and non-punishers. However, the model does not directly report on group efficiency or total payoff, focusing instead on the evolutionary dynamics of behavioral traits. The results suggest that shame and punishment can stabilize cooperation for long periods, but with fluctuations between cooperative and selfish phases.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the idea that punishment (and shame) can increase pro-social behavior in resource-sharing environments, which may translate to higher efficiency in public goods games. However, because the model is not a direct PGG and does not report efficiency or group payoff, its value for predicting efficiency outcomes is limited. The main relevance is in showing that punishment and shame can stabilize cooperation, especially when punishment is not costly to the punisher. The findings are more about evolutionary stability and trait frequencies than about efficiency per se.

- source: Li, K., Szolnoki, A., Cong, R., & Wang, L. (2016). The coevolution of overconfidence and bluffing in the resource competition game. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_tech
  findings: The model shows that in the absence of punishment, both overconfidence and bluffing evolve to high levels, with bluffing generally higher than overconfidence. Introducing punishment (with probability p) for revealed deception reduces both overconfidence and bluffing, with overconfidence being more sensitive to punishment. The structure of the interaction network (degree, heterogeneity) modulates these effects: higher degree or more heterogeneous networks promote bluffing and can make punishment less effective. System bias (delta) that increases conflict frequency also enhances the effectiveness of punishment. Players with higher real ability tend to evolve higher overconfidence and bluffing. The paper provides some analysis of average payoff for punished vs. non-punished players, showing that those with lower ability and lower payoff are punished more often, but does not report group-level efficiency or total welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the presence and probability of punishment can reduce the prevalence of overconfidence and bluffing in resource competition games, especially overconfidence. However, because the main outcomes are behavioral (deception levels) and not group efficiency or total payoff, the direct predictive value for efficiency outcomes in public goods or similar games is limited. The model is adjacent to PGGs: it is a resource competition game with pairwise contests, not a standard PGG. The punishment mechanism is modeled as a reduction in deception traits rather than direct payoff reduction. The paper does provide some subgroup payoff analysis, suggesting that punishment targets and reduces the payoff of lower-ability, higher-deception individuals, but does not quantify overall group efficiency. Thus, the main decision support is qualitative: punishment reduces harmful behavioral biases, and its effectiveness depends on network structure and conflict frequency, but the effect on group efficiency is not directly established.

- source: Huang, F. (2007). Building social trust: A human-capital approach. *Journal of Institutional and Theoretical Economics-zeitschrift Fur Die Gesamte Staatswissenschaft*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that when punishment institutions are more effective at detecting and punishing defection, more individuals choose to invest in cooperative tendencies, leading to higher social trust (i.e., a higher proportion of cooperative players). However, the average level of intrinsic cooperative tendency among cooperators is lower, since extrinsic incentives substitute for intrinsic motivation. The model predicts multiple equilibria depending on the distribution of investment costs, and highlights the potential for both high- and low-trust equilibria. The relationship between institutional punishment and cooperation is complex: stronger institutions can crowd in more cooperators but may reduce intrinsic motivation. The paper does not provide direct predictions or analysis of group efficiency or total payoff, but the results are relevant for understanding how punishment institutions affect the prevalence of cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that increasing the effectiveness of punishment institutions (e.g., higher probability of detection or stronger sanctions) increases the proportion of cooperative players in a prisoners' dilemma-like environment. While it does not directly model public goods games or report efficiency outcomes, it suggests that punishment mechanisms can increase cooperation rates, which may translate into higher efficiency in related environments. However, the paper also warns that reliance on extrinsic punishment may reduce intrinsic motivation over time. For prediction tasks, the main implication is that enabling or strengthening punishment is likely to increase cooperation (and thus potentially efficiency), but the effect on efficiency is not directly quantified. The model is most relevant for settings where the main outcome is the prevalence of cooperation rather than group payoff.

- source: Bosworth, W. (2022). Social Power and Non-cooperative Game Theory. *Journal of Theoretical Politics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  outcomes: primary=N/R | overall_effect=N/A

  findings: The paper argues that the credibility of threats and offers (punishments and rewards) is central to understanding social power and governance in non-cooperative games. It shows that resources, reputation, and coordination problems determine the ability to credibly punish or reward, and thus to exercise power. The analysis highlights the importance of commitment mechanisms and tipping points in reputation for sustaining credible threats. However, the paper does not provide empirical findings or quantitative predictions about efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods or similar games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides conceptual and theoretical insights into how punishment and reward mechanisms may affect cooperation and social power in collective action settings, emphasizing the importance of credibility, resources, and coordination. However, it does not provide empirical or quantitative evidence that can be directly used to predict efficiency outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment. Its value for prediction is limited to high-level mechanism discussion and may inform the interpretation of empirical results from other sources.

- source: Li, B. Q., Wu, Z. X., & Guan, J. Y. (2022). Critical thresholds of benefit distribution in an extended snowdrift game model. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_tech
  findings: The main findings are: (1) In both infinite and finite structured populations, the critical proportion of benefits that defectors can exploit (while still maintaining stable cooperation) increases with the total resource available, but saturates at a limit. (2) Introducing incentives for cooperators and punishments for defectors increases this critical threshold, meaning that inequality (the share defectors can take) can become worse with such mechanisms. (3) In more highly connected populations, the critical threshold is lower, so inequality is less severe. (4) There is a critical value of incentive/punishment magnitude above which the relationship between resource size and critical threshold reverses. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, but rather the conditions for stable coexistence of cooperation and defection.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the effect of punishment (and reward) on the sustainability of cooperation under unequal benefit distributions in a snowdrift game, which is adjacent to public goods games. It suggests that introducing punishment (and reward) can increase the threshold of tolerated inequality, potentially making outcomes less fair for cooperators. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. Its main value is in highlighting that punishment can have counterintuitive effects on the distribution of benefits and the persistence of cooperation, especially under varying network structures and resource levels.

- source: Boehm, C. (2008). Purposive Social Selection and the Evolution of Human Altruism. *Cross-cultural Research*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, chat, reward_exists, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper finds that in small-scale, mobile hunter-gatherer societies, both punitive and positive social sanctioning are pervasive and target behaviors relevant to group cooperation, such as sharing, generosity, and suppression of dominance or cheating. Punishment (including ostracism and even capital punishment) is used to control antisocial behaviors, while positive sanctioning (reputation, social approval, preferential partnerships) rewards prosocial behaviors. The author theorizes that these mechanisms have had significant evolutionary effects, shaping both the prevalence of altruism and the development of conscience in humans. However, the findings are based on ethnographic coding and theoretical reasoning, not on experimental or quantitative analysis of efficiency or payoffs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and ethnographic support for the idea that punishment and reward mechanisms are central to the maintenance of cooperation in small-scale societies, and that they have long-term evolutionary consequences. However, it does not provide quantitative or experimental evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games or similar environments. For the downstream prediction task, this paper supports the plausibility of punishment as a mechanism for sustaining cooperation, but does not provide data or effect sizes relevant to predicting efficiency outcomes as a function of game design dimensions.

  findings: The paper finds that in mobile hunter-gatherer societies, group punishment—ranging from shaming and ostracism to capital punishment—has been a central mechanism for enforcing cooperation and curbing antisocial behaviors such as bullying, theft, and cheating. These sanctions are argued to have shaped the evolution of human conscience and moral norms, favoring individuals who are generous, honest, and humble. The author suggests that such punitive social selection has been critical for the emergence of egalitarian, cooperative societies, and for the evolutionary success of altruistic traits. However, the paper does not provide direct evidence or quantitative analysis of efficiency or group payoffs in public goods game-like settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and ethnographic context for the role of punishment in promoting cooperation and suppressing free-riding in small-scale societies. It supports the idea that punishment can be a powerful tool for maintaining cooperation, but does not provide quantitative or experimental evidence on efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods games or their variants. For prediction tasks involving the effect of punishment on efficiency, this paper offers indirect support for the positive role of punishment in sustaining cooperation, especially in small, face-to-face groups, but does not allow for parameter-specific predictions based on game design dimensions.

- source: Horne, C., & Mollborn, S. (2020). Norms: An Integrated Framework. *Annual Review of Sociology, Vol 46*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper reviews and synthesizes sociological theories of norms, focusing on why and how people enforce norms (including through punishment and reward), and the implications for social order and inequality. It distinguishes between consequentialist (cooperation-focused), relational (coordination-focused), and agentic (contextual/individual-focused) approaches. The review highlights that sanctions (punishment and reward) can increase norm compliance, but also that enforcement can reinforce inequalities and sometimes undermine collective welfare, depending on the structure of interests and relationships. The paper does not report new empirical findings or analyze efficiency or group payoff in experimental games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides high-level theoretical context for understanding the role of punishment and reward in norm enforcement and social order, but does not provide empirical evidence or quantitative findings that can be directly used to predict efficiency outcomes in public goods games or similar experimental settings. It may inform the interpretation of why punishment can sometimes promote or undermine cooperation, but does not offer parameter-level guidance for the prediction task.

- source: Molho, C., & Wu, J. H. (2021). Direct punishment and indirect reputation-based tactics to intervene against offences. *Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: chat, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper argues that both direct punishment and indirect, reputation-based tactics (gossip, ostracism) are important for maintaining cooperation and enforcing norms. Direct punishment is more effective at recalibrating offenders' behavior but is costly and risky, while indirect tactics are less costly and reduce retaliation risk but are less effective at changing behavior. The choice of tactic depends on factors such as the self-relevance of the offense, relationship to the offender, power dynamics, and emotional responses (anger vs. disgust). The paper highlights that indirect tactics are more prevalent in real-world settings than direct punishment as operationalized in laboratory public goods games. It also notes that punishment can sometimes reduce efficiency, and that the ecological validity of lab findings is limited.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment. However, it offers theoretical context suggesting that the effectiveness and efficiency of punishment depend on its cost, the risk of retaliation, and the availability of alternative (indirect) sanctioning mechanisms. It cautions that lab-based punishment games may overestimate the prevalence and impact of direct punishment compared to real-world settings, where indirect tactics are more common. For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that the presence of communication, relationship context, and the type of punishment available may moderate the effect of punishment on cooperation and efficiency, but it does not provide quantitative or directly predictive evidence.

- source: Cofnas, N. (2018). POWER IN CULTURAL EVOLUTION AND THE SPREAD OF PROSOCIAL NORMS. *Quarterly Review of Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: chat, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper argues that mainstream cultural evolutionary models, which treat punishment as an individual-level, uncoordinated action, fail to capture the collective, power-based enforcement of prosocial norms observed in real-world societies, especially among hunter-gatherers. It contends that collective punishment and coordinated norm enforcement are central to the evolution of morality and prosociality, and that these processes are not well-modeled by standard public goods game frameworks. The paper reviews anthropological evidence for collective punishment and discusses how this shapes moral psychology, but does not report or synthesize efficiency or payoff outcomes from experimental games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides important theoretical context for interpreting the role of punishment in public-goods-game-like environments, emphasizing that real-world punishment is often collective and coordinated, not merely the sum of individual actions. However, it does not provide quantitative or empirical evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff, nor does it analyze how game design dimensions (such as player count, rounds, or punishment cost) moderate these effects. For the downstream prediction task, this paper suggests that models which ignore collective decision-making and power dynamics may misrepresent the real impact of punishment, but it does not provide data or direct evidence to calibrate predictions of efficiency outcomes.

- source: Toelstede, B. (2020). The Absence of Policing and the Political Economy of Contribution and Defection. *Democracy & Security*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper finds that in large groups, the disappearance of institutional punishment leads to a delayed decline in public goods contribution and a rapid increase in defection, while the reappearance of punishment leads to a delayed recovery of contribution and a rapid decline in defection. The length of these delays depends on factors such as trust in the punisher, transparency, media behavior, and regime support. The paper highlights the asymmetry between small and large groups, and between peer and institutional punishment, in terms of the speed and nature of behavioral responses. It also discusses the role of blame assignment and political manipulation in shaping these dynamics.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide quantitative or directly predictive evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public-goods-game-like environments. However, it offers theoretical and qualitative insights into how the effect of punishment on contribution (and by extension, efficiency) may be moderated by group size, institutional structure, trust, and information transparency. For prediction tasks, the main implication is that in large, real-world groups, the positive effect of punishment on contribution (and thus efficiency) may be delayed or attenuated compared to small, transparent, lab-like settings. The paper is not suitable for direct quantitative prediction but may inform qualitative expectations about moderators.

- source: Kritikos, A., & Bolle, F. (2004). Punishment as a public good. When should monopolists care about a consumer boycott?. *Journal of Economic Psychology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the ability of consumers to punish a monopolist (e.g., via boycott) and thereby reduce prices depends on the number of consumers and the degree of solidarity among them. With few consumers, individual punishment can be effective and lead to lower prices. As the number of consumers increases, the incentive to individually punish diminishes (public good problem), and the monopolist can more easily charge higher (Cournot) prices unless solidarity is high. The model predicts that successful collective punishment (boycott) is more likely in small groups or when solidarity is strong, and less likely in large, uncoordinated groups.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical insight into how group size and solidarity affect the effectiveness of punishment in public-good-like environments, specifically in the context of consumer boycotts against monopolists. While it does not provide quantitative predictions of efficiency, it suggests that increasing group size without mechanisms for solidarity or coordination will reduce the effectiveness of punishment in improving group outcomes. For prediction tasks, this implies that punishment is less likely to improve efficiency in large, uncoordinated groups, but may be effective in small groups or when solidarity is high. However, the lack of direct efficiency or payoff data limits its quantitative predictive value.

- source: López-Pérez, R. (2008). Aversion to norm-breaking:: A model. *Games and Economic Behavior*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper develops a formal model in which agents internalize social norms (specifically, an 'E-norm' combining efficiency and equality) and experience emotional costs (shame, anger) when violating or observing violations of these norms. The model predicts that people will cooperate and punish norm violators even at a cost to themselves, and that punishment is targeted at those responsible for norm violations. The model is shown to qualitatively account for a range of experimental findings in games such as dictator, ultimatum, trust, centipede, Cournot, and public goods games. In the context of public goods games, the model predicts that the introduction of punishment opportunities increases cooperation by deterring selfish behavior, as selfish agents comply to avoid being punished by principled agents. However, the paper does not provide direct quantitative predictions or empirical estimates of efficiency or group payoff with and without punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides a theoretical rationale for why punishment increases cooperation and norm compliance in public goods and related games, suggesting that the presence of punishment opportunities should increase efficiency relative to control conditions without punishment, especially when a sufficient proportion of players are 'principled' and willing to punish norm violations. However, the model is qualitative and does not specify the magnitude of efficiency gains or how they depend on specific game design dimensions (e.g., player count, punishment cost, MPCR).   findings: The paper finds that conditional defector strategies—cheaters who sometimes cooperate (e.g., to share dispersal costs or pay to escape punishment)—can successfully invade and dominate populations even when mechanisms like punishment, kin selection, and spatial structure are present. In the models, when cheaters can share dispersal costs, they can migrate and outcompete cooperators, leading to cooperator extinction. Similarly, when cheaters reduce their own payoff to avoid detection and punishment, they can dominate despite the presence of punishment mechanisms. The main reported outcomes are the frequencies of agent types and extinction events, not direct efficiency or group payoff measures.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides a cautionary theoretical perspective: the presence of punishment mechanisms does not guarantee the maintenance of cooperation or high efficiency if conditional defector strategies are possible. The models are adjacent to public goods games and do not report efficiency as a ratio to the fully cooperative outcome, so direct quantitative prediction is not possible. However, the findings suggest that in environments where agents can adaptively evade punishment (e.g., by reducing their own payoff to avoid detection), the expected efficiency gain from enabling punishment may be much lower or even negative, as cheaters can still dominate. For prediction tasks, this paper highlights the importance of considering the sophistication of defector strategies and the limitations of punishment as a stabilizing mechanism.

The author reviews theoretical models (e.g., Boyd and Richerson 1992) and ethnographic/experimental evidence to support the claim that punishment, including direct, exclusion-based, and reputation-based forms, is universal in human groups. The paper also discusses the problem of stabilizing costly punishment and the implications for understanding moral diversity. However, it does not provide quantitative or experimental results on efficiency or group payoff.

The arguments are useful for understanding why punishment may be effective, but the paper does not supply empirical data or parameterized results that can be directly used for predicting efficiency changes in experimental public goods games.   findings: The paper claims that strong reciprocity and altruistic punishment are key to human cooperation, but that their effectiveness is limited by cognitive constraints, especially as group size increases. As groups grow, the costs and logistical difficulties of monitoring and punishing free riders rise, reducing the effectiveness of informal punishment and thus limiting group size. The emergence of large-scale societies required the development of institutions that formalize and distribute the responsibility for sanctioning (the 'social division of sanction'), which in turn enabled larger, more complex societies. The paper references experimental evidence (e.g., Isaac et al. 1994; Carpenter 2007; Keser and van Winden 2000) to support claims about group size, punishment, and cooperation, but does not itself report new data or analyze efficiency directly.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical context for how punishment and cooperation interact with group size and institutional structure, but does not provide direct empirical evidence for predicting efficiency outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment. It suggests that as group size increases, the effectiveness of punishment in sustaining cooperation may decrease due to cognitive and monitoring constraints, unless institutional mechanisms are introduced. For prediction tasks, the paper supports the idea that group size and the presence or absence of formal sanctioning institutions are important moderators of punishment's effect, but does not provide quantitative or directly predictive evidence for efficiency outcomes.

- source: Singh, M., & Boomsma, J. J. (2015). Policing and punishment across the domains of social evolution. *Oikos*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper proposes that punishment and policing behaviors are maintained when the inclusive fitness benefits to the punisher outweigh the costs, and when these benefits are greater than those from bystanding. The framework is applied to a range of biological and social systems, showing that power, relatedness, and reproductive options drive variation in who punishes. In systems with strong power asymmetries, dominants are more likely to punish; in systems with high relatedness and low individual reproductive prospects, policing is more likely to be corrective and group-beneficial. The paper highlights that the same inclusive fitness logic can explain punishment across diverse domains, but does not provide empirical data or direct predictions about efficiency or group payoff in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides a conceptual framework for understanding the conditions under which punishment is likely to emerge and be maintained in social groups, emphasizing the roles of power, relatedness, and reproductive options. However, it does not provide empirical evidence or quantitative predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public-goods-game-like environments. For the downstream prediction task, the paper may inform mechanistic expectations about when punishment is likely to be effective or who will implement it, but it does not supply data or direct evidence to calibrate efficiency outcomes as a function of game design dimensions.

- source: Phillips, T. (2018). The concepts of asymmetric and symmetric power can help resolve the puzzle of altruistic and cooperative behaviour. *Biological Reviews*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the effectiveness and function of punishment in promoting cooperation depend critically on the symmetry or asymmetry of power among individuals. In asymmetric power settings (e.g., dominance hierarchies), punishment is typically used by dominants to coerce subordinates, not to enforce reciprocity or group-beneficial cooperation. In symmetric power settings, punishment can be counteracted by retaliation, and cooperation may break down unless specific conditions are met (e.g., territoriality, exit options). The paper reviews evidence that punishment often reduces group payoffs in experimental public goods games, and that the cost-to-impact ratio of punishment is crucial for its effectiveness. However, it does not provide new quantitative findings on efficiency or payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and conceptual guidance for predicting the effects of punishment on efficiency in public goods game-like environments, especially regarding the role of power asymmetries. It suggests that punishment is unlikely to improve efficiency in settings with strong power asymmetries, as it is used for coercion rather than cooperation enforcement. In symmetric power settings, punishment may also fail to improve efficiency due to retaliation and the tragedy of the commons. The paper highlights the importance of considering power relations, cost-to-impact ratios, and the possibility of retaliation when predicting the efficiency effects of punishment. However, it does not provide direct empirical estimates or quantitative predictions for efficiency outcomes, so its value is mainly in informing the interpretation of empirical results and the design of experiments.

- source: Smith, D. (2020). Cultural group selection and human cooperation: a conceptual and empirical review. *Evolutionary Human Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=N/R | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the mechanisms often posited by CGS models to stabilize cooperation—such as conformist social learning and a norm psychology that motivates indiscriminate punishment—are not strongly supported by empirical evidence. Instead, individuals tend to use adaptive, context-sensitive strategies, and punishment is typically self-interested, aimed at increasing future cooperation or personal payoff rather than enforcing norms for their own sake. The review also notes that institutions facilitating cooperation often arise endogenously through rational design rather than through stochastic group-level processes. While punishment can stabilize cooperation, its effectiveness and the resulting group efficiency depend on the alignment of individual and group interests, the structure of institutions, and the presence of incentives. The review does not provide new quantitative evidence on efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods games or their variants, but it synthesizes theoretical and empirical arguments relevant to the design and interpretation of such experiments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides important conceptual guidance for predicting the effects of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. It suggests that the impact of punishment on efficiency is highly contingent on whether punishment is self-interested (aligned with individual and group payoffs) or norm-based (potentially maladaptive). The review argues that, in practice, punishment is usually self-interested and embedded in institutional contexts that align individual incentives with group benefit, which tends to support higher efficiency. However, it cautions that models assuming indiscriminate or maladaptive punishment may not reflect real human behavior. For prediction tasks, this implies that game designs with punishment mechanisms are more likely to increase efficiency when they include features that align individual and group interests (e.g., rewards for punishers, reputation systems, endogenous institution formation), and less likely to do so when punishment is costly, norm-based, and not incentivized. The paper does not provide direct empirical estimates but offers a framework for interpreting when and why punishment may or may not improve efficiency.

- source: Nakao, H., & Machery, E. (2012). The evolution of punishment. *Biology & Philosophy*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that punishment is widespread in nature and often evolves for reasons other than inducing behavioral change in the punishee. In many cases (especially among plants and insects), punishment is a loss-cutting or cost-imposing strategy, not a behavior-modification strategy. Even among vertebrates, where behavioral modification is more common, the evolutionary benefits of punishment often do not depend on the punishee changing its behavior. The paper challenges the view that punishment primarily evolved to promote cooperation or efficiency, and instead highlights alternative evolutionary pathways.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence or quantitative data relevant to predicting the efficiency effects of punishment in public-goods-game-like environments. It does, however, offer theoretical context suggesting that the evolutionary function of punishment may not always be to increase cooperation or group efficiency, but could instead be to reduce harm to the punisher or its kin. For the prediction task, this paper suggests caution in assuming that punishment mechanisms will always improve efficiency or cooperation, especially in settings where behavioral modification is unlikely or where punishment serves other evolutionary functions. However, it does not provide empirical or experimental data to calibrate such predictions.

  findings: The paper finds that, although theoretical models (including those based on the Prisoner's Dilemma) predict that punishment can stabilize cooperation, actual evidence for punishment as a mechanism for sustaining cooperation in nonhuman animals is rare. Most observed 'punishment' is better characterized as harassment, which yields immediate rather than delayed benefits. The rarity of punishment and reciprocity in animals is attributed to cognitive constraints, particularly the need for individual recognition, low temporal discounting, and sufficient memory to track interactions. In contrast, humans possess cognitive adaptations that enable widespread use of punishment and reciprocity to sustain cooperation. The paper does not provide quantitative or experimental evidence on efficiency or payoff effects of punishment in public goods games or their variants.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and comparative context for why punishment may or may not be effective in sustaining cooperation, especially in nonhuman animals. It highlights the importance of cognitive prerequisites (recognition, memory, discounting) for punishment to function as a stabilizer of cooperation. However, it does not provide direct evidence or data that can be used to predict the efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games or similar experimental designs. For the downstream prediction task, this paper suggests that the effectiveness of punishment may depend on whether the game environment allows for individual recognition, repeated interactions, and low discounting, but it does not quantify these effects or relate them to efficiency outcomes.

  findings: The paper argues that sanctions can have an expressive function, conveying moral norms and motivating compliance for moral reasons rather than purely instrumental ones. It reviews evidence that (1) retributive sanctions are more likely to induce moral norms than compensatory ones, (2) punishments are more effective than rewards at conveying obligatory moral rules, and (3) severe (but not unreasonably severe) sanctions are more likely to induce moral disapproval and compliance, especially when implemented by trustworthy and fair authorities. However, the paper also notes that sanctions can backfire, especially among those already engaging in the undesired behavior, and that excessive severity or unfairness can undermine moral norms.

For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper is only indirectly informative, as it does not report or synthesize efficiency or group payoff data. As punishment increases, confrontation rates decrease, and organisms become more cooperative. The model also finds that memory (even a simple one-step memory) has a limited but significant effect on confrontation rates, especially as punishment increases. The study provides evidence that risk-taking and confrontation are shaped by both environmental parameters and genetic/cognitive factors, but does not directly measure efficiency or group payoff as in public goods games.

However, the lack of direct efficiency or group payoff measures limits the ability to quantitatively predict efficiency outcomes from design dimensions. - source: Marco, J., & Goetz, R. (2024). Public policy design and common property resources: A social network approach. *American Journal of Agricultural Economics*.

  findings: The paper finds that social network structure (cohesiveness) and the severity of the resource dilemma jointly determine the effectiveness of informal (social pressure) enforcement in promoting cooperation. Network-oriented policies can reduce the minimum share of compliers needed for full cooperation, but only up to 26% in the calibrated case. Legal enforcement (fines/subsidies) can complement informal enforcement, but subsidizing the costs of social pressure may eliminate stable partial cooperation. The number of agents has only minor effects unless changed drastically. The model suggests that policy effectiveness depends on initial conditions (stock, share of compliers, network structure) and that mixed enforcement strategies should be applied cautiously, as they may interact in non-additive ways.

The model suggests that punishment can increase the basin of attraction for cooperation and reduce the critical mass of compliers needed, but the effect is bounded. For prediction tasks, the paper supports the idea that enabling punishment will generally increase efficiency (move the system toward the socially optimal steady state), but the magnitude of the effect is limited by network structure and agents' perceptions. The findings are most relevant for settings with costly, decentralized punishment and binary contribution choices, and less so for standard lab PGGs with explicit monetary punishment. The results are qualitative and parameter-dependent, not direct empirical estimates.

  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper develops a coupled socioeconomic-bioeconomic model of a fishing community, where individuals choose to cooperate with management or not, and non-cooperators experience psychological punishment ('social pressure') that depends on both internal (community-based) and external (outside community) sources. The model shows that when both internal and external social pressures are sufficiently strong, the system can reach full cooperation, resulting in sustainable fish stocks and maximum sustainable yield (MSY)—a proxy for group efficiency. If only one type of social pressure is strong, or both are weak, the system may stabilize at lower cooperation and lower stock levels. The model highlights the importance of both community relationships and external societal pressure in promoting cooperation for resource management.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect but informative evidence that psychological punishment mechanisms (social pressure) can increase group efficiency in common-pool resource settings analogous to public goods games, especially when both internal and external sources are present. While the model is not a standard PGG and does not report efficiency as a ratio of actual to maximum possible group payoff, it shows that the presence and strength of punishment-like mechanisms (social pressure) are critical moderators of group outcomes. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that enabling both internal and external punishment/sanction mechanisms is likely to increase efficiency, while the absence or weakness of either reduces the effect. The model also suggests that the effect of punishment is moderated by group size (player count), as social pressure per individual decreases with larger groups.

- source: Pin, P., & Rogers, B. W. (2015). Cooperation, punishment and immigration. *Journal of Economic Theory*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that increasing the punishment (expulsion) rate for immigrant defectors increases the overall cooperation rate in society, but decreases the cooperation rate among citizens. This is due to an equilibrium substitution effect: as immigrants are more strongly incentivized to cooperate, the overall level of cooperation rises above its 'natural' level, making defection more attractive for citizens. The model shows that the net effect of punishment is smaller than would be predicted if equilibrium effects were ignored. The paper does not directly analyze efficiency or group payoff, but expected payoffs for different agent types are derived. The results are robust to heterogeneity in agent preferences.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that punishment targeted at a subset of players (immigrants) can increase overall cooperation rates, but may also induce more defection among the untargeted group (citizens), reducing the net effect on cooperation. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff may be less positive than expected if substitution effects are present. However, the model does not directly report efficiency or group payoff, so its quantitative value for predicting efficiency is limited. The model is adjacent to public goods games, as it is based on a networked prisoner's dilemma with binary (all-or-nothing) choices and dynamic linking, rather than a standard PGG. The findings are most relevant for understanding equilibrium behavioral responses to targeted punishment policies.

- source: Armstrong, K., Zhong, Y. M., Bhide, S., Grant, S. B., Birkland, T., & Berglund, E. Z. (2024). Simulating the emergence of institutions that reverse freshwater salinization: An agent-based modeling approach. *Journal of Hydrology X*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The agent-based model demonstrates that self-organized institutions with sanctions (fines for defectors, monitoring) can emerge and stabilize resource use, keeping salt concentrations below harmful thresholds when defection rates are low and innovation rates are moderate to high. High defection rates undermine institution effectiveness, leading to resource degradation. The model highlights the importance of cooperation, monitoring, and sanctions in sustaining common-pool resources, but does not provide direct evidence on efficiency or group payoff as in public goods games. The findings are most relevant for understanding the emergence and stability of sanctioning institutions in resource dilemmas, rather than for predicting efficiency outcomes in PGG-like experiments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, conceptual support for the idea that punishment and monitoring can improve resource outcomes in social dilemmas, especially when defection is not too prevalent and agents can innovate or imitate successful strategies. However, it does not provide quantitative or empirical data on efficiency or group payoff, nor does it manipulate standard PGG design dimensions. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency in PGG-like games, this paper offers contextual and mechanistic insights but not direct predictive evidence. Its main value is in illustrating how sanctions and institutional emergence can stabilize cooperation and resource quality in agent-based models of common-pool resource dilemmas.

The model provides explicit conditions under which full cooperation, partial cooperation, or defection equilibria are stable. Increasing the effectiveness or magnitude of regulatory punishment/reward expands the parameter space in which cooperation is stable. However, the main reported outcomes are the equilibrium strategy fractions, not group efficiency or total payoff. - source: He, J. J., Liu, J. S., Qiu, Z. P., Nanzayi, N. E., & Zhang, M. (2021). How Does the R&D Cooperation Path of Supply and Demand Network Enterprises Evolve?. *Ieee Access*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that the evolution of cooperation in R&D networks depends on the balance of knowledge spillover, government reward, government penalty (punishment), cooperation cost, and cooperation risk. Increasing government penalty (punishment) and reward, and decreasing cost and risk, promote the stability of cooperation. The model shows that under certain parameter regimes, cooperation becomes evolutionarily stable, while in others, selfishness dominates. The effect of punishment (penalty) is to accelerate and stabilize cooperation, but the paper does not quantify efficiency or group payoff. The findings are based on theoretical analysis and numerical simulation of the evolutionary game model.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, theory-based evidence that introducing or increasing punishment (government penalty) in a social dilemma-like environment can promote cooperation, especially when combined with rewards and low costs/risks. However, the model does not directly report efficiency or group payoff, so its value for predicting efficiency outcomes is limited. The results suggest that, in similar networked cooperation settings, punishment is likely to increase cooperation rates, which may translate to higher efficiency if payoffs are aligned with cooperation. The paper is most useful for understanding the qualitative direction of punishment effects and the importance of cost, risk, and reward as moderators.

- source: Verma, P., Nandi, A. K., & Sengupta, S. (2017). Bribery games on inter-dependent regular networks. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper finds that in structured populations, the spread of honest officers (and thus reduction in bribery) is favored over a larger region of parameter space compared to mixed populations. However, the effect of network connectivity is non-monotonic: increasing the degree of the citizen network initially helps honest strategies, but beyond an optimal point, further increases hinder the spread of honesty. Similarly, the degree of the interaction network (citizen-officer links) has an optimal value for maximizing the prevalence of honest officers. Asymmetry in population sizes (more citizens per officer) makes it harder to eradicate bribery. The cost of complaining and the bribe amount are key parameters: lower cost of complaining and lower bribe amounts favor honest strategies. The main dependent variables are the fixation probability of honest officers and the phase space area where honest strategies dominate, not direct measures of group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect but informative evidence for the prediction task. While it does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, it analyzes how network structure, punishment asymmetry, and behavioral parameters affect the prevalence of honest (non-corrupt) strategies. In public-goods-like environments with asymmetric punishment, structured populations can reduce corruption, but only up to an optimal level of network connectivity. The findings suggest that, for similar games, enabling punishment (asymmetric, targeting only one side) in a structured population can increase the prevalence of socially beneficial behavior, but the effect depends on network connectivity and behavioral costs. For prediction, the paper supports the idea that punishment can improve outcomes (in terms of honest behavior) in structured settings, but warns that too much connectivity can undermine this effect. However, because efficiency or group payoff is not directly measured, the findings should be used as indirect evidence.

- source: Pan, Q. H., Wang, Y., Chen, Q., Gao, L. Y., & He, M. F. (2022). Effects of quasi-defection strategy on cooperation evolution in social dilemma. *Physics Letters A*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the introduction of quasi-defectors—who can both reward cooperators and punish defectors—enables the stable coexistence of cooperation and quasi-defection, and can suppress defection under certain parameter regimes. The effect of punishment on cooperation is positive but limited: increasing punishment initially increases cooperation, but further increases have diminishing returns. Reward can compensate for the limited effect of punishment and further promote cooperation. The cost of rewarding/punishing must be low for quasi-defectors to persist and support cooperation. The model is theoretical and does not report efficiency or group payoff, but the results suggest that mechanisms combining reward and punishment can sustain higher levels of cooperation than punishment alone.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect theoretical support for the idea that combining punishment and reward mechanisms can promote cooperation in social dilemmas, and that the effect of punishment on cooperation is positive but limited by cost and diminishing returns. However, because the model is based on a prisoner's dilemma with a quasi-defection strategy and does not report efficiency or group payoff, its direct applicability to predicting efficiency in public goods games is limited. The findings suggest that, in analogous settings, moderate punishment and reward can increase cooperation, but the effect on efficiency would depend on the cost structure and the mapping from strategy proportions to group payoff.

- source: Dong, D. K., & Li, S. (2023). How a Smart regulatory platform can improve the property fee adjustment dilemma. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that under traditional regulation, the game between homeowners and property management tends to a 'prisoner's dilemma' with mutual defection (betrayal). Introducing a smart regulatory platform with government oversight, information transparency, and punishment for non-cooperation shifts the evolutionary stable strategy toward mutual cooperation. Stronger regulation and higher punishment increase the speed and likelihood of cooperation, while higher property fee increases make cooperation less likely. The findings are based on theoretical analysis and numerical simulation of the evolutionary game, not on empirical data. The outcomes are expressed in terms of cooperation rates, not explicit efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, theory-based evidence that introducing punishment and regulatory oversight in a repeated social dilemma can increase cooperation rates, especially when punishment is strong and information is transparent. However, it does not provide direct evidence on efficiency or group payoff, nor does it use a standard public goods game. The results suggest that, in similar environments, enabling punishment and increasing regulatory strength would likely increase cooperation and, by implication, could improve efficiency, but this is not directly measured. The model's structure and findings may inform predictions about the effect of punishment in PGG-like environments, especially regarding the importance of punishment strength and transparency, but the lack of direct efficiency outcomes limits its predictive value for the target task.

- source: Pei, H. Y., & Yan, G. H. (2022). Punishment and reputation based partners-switching promotes cooperation in social networks. *Epl*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that a mechanism combining behavioral punishment (severing ties with defectors) and reputation-based partner selection leads to high levels of cooperation in a dynamic networked prisoner's dilemma. The frequency of partner switching (time scale ratio W) is a key moderator: higher W leads to higher cooperation, and there is a critical W above which cooperation dominates. The effect is robust to temptation to defect and network degree, though higher values of these parameters require higher W for cooperation to prevail. The model does not report efficiency or group payoff, but the results suggest that cooperation can be sustained or reach fixation under the right conditions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that punishment-like mechanisms (here, behavioral punishment via partner switching) can promote cooperation in dynamic networked social dilemmas. However, the model is a prisoner's dilemma, not a public goods game, and does not report efficiency or group payoff. The findings suggest that in environments where individuals can sever ties with defectors and form new ties based on reputation, cooperation is likely to be higher, especially when partner switching is frequent. For the prediction task, this paper supports the idea that punishment and partner selection mechanisms can increase cooperation, but does not provide direct evidence about efficiency or payoff outcomes in PGG-like settings.

- source: Geng, Y. N., Shen, C., Hu, K. P., & Shi, L. (2018). Impact of punishment on the evolution of cooperation in spatial prisoner's dilemma game. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The introduction of a punishment mechanism—where players with below-average payoffs are punished by a reduction in their fitness coefficient—substantially increases the stationary frequency of cooperators in a spatial prisoner's dilemma model. Increasing the punishment rate and memory length both further promote cooperation, leading to full dominance of cooperation under many parameter settings. The mechanism's effect is attributed to increased heterogeneity in individual fitness, which supports network reciprocity. However, the paper does not report on group payoff, efficiency, or welfare, so its findings are limited to behavioral outcomes (cooperation rates) rather than payoff-based outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that punishment mechanisms can increase cooperation rates in spatial prisoner's dilemma environments, especially when punishment is more intense and lasts longer. However, because the model does not report efficiency or group payoff, and because the game is a prisoner's dilemma rather than a public goods game, the findings should be transferred to PGG-like efficiency prediction with caution. The results suggest that, in similar spatial or networked environments, enabling punishment may increase cooperation, which could plausibly increase efficiency, but this is not directly demonstrated. The specific punishment mechanism (punishing those with below-average payoffs) is also nonstandard compared to typical PGG punishment designs.

- source: Gao, L. Y., Pan, Q. H., & He, M. F. (2022). Advanced defensive cooperators promote cooperation in the prisoner's dilemma game. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  findings: The paper finds that introducing advanced defensive cooperators, who can identify and punish anti-defensive defectors at a cost, leads to higher equilibrium fractions of cooperators compared to the standard prisoner's dilemma. The presence of advanced defensive cooperators is especially important when the temptation to defect is high. The model shows that the effectiveness of advanced defensive cooperation depends on parameters such as the temptation value, defensive benefit, defensive loss, fine (punishment), and screening cost. While the model tracks payoffs in the replicator equations, the main reported results are the fractions of each strategy at equilibrium, not group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the idea that punishment-like mechanisms (here, advanced defensive cooperation with screening and fines) can increase the prevalence of cooperation in social dilemma settings. However, because the main outcomes are strategy fractions rather than group efficiency or total payoff, the results are only adjacent to the prediction task of forecasting efficiency. The model is a prisoner's dilemma variant, not a public goods game, and does not report efficiency as a ratio of actual to maximum possible group payoff. Thus, while the findings suggest that enabling punishment can increase cooperation, they do not provide direct quantitative evidence for predicting efficiency changes in public-goods-game-like environments.

Under some parameter regimes, mutual cooperation (input, input) is the stable outcome; in others, only one party inputs knowledge. The effect of government penalty (punishment) is to increase the likelihood of cooperation, but the paper does not quantify efficiency or group payoff. The findings are based on theoretical analysis and numerical simulation of the evolutionary dynamics, not on empirical or experimental data.

However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, so it cannot be used directly to predict efficiency changes from punishment in public goods games. - source: Safarzynska, K. (2013). The coevolution of culture and environment. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The model shows that costly resource conservation (including punishment of defectors) can evolve in structured populations facing renewable resource constraints and environmental shocks. Groups with many defectors are more likely to overharvest and collapse, while groups with more cooperators and punishers are more resilient. Punishment reduces the within-group advantage of defection, but is itself costly. Environmental shocks (weather) accelerate the elimination of non-cooperative groups. Between-group interactions (resource-conflict and harvest-sharing) modulate the evolution of cooperation: resource-conflict increases the frequency of resource conservation by increasing collapse of defector-heavy groups, while harvest-sharing reduces group collapse and allows more defection. Larger group size has a non-monotonic effect: above a threshold, larger groups are more likely to collapse, which can favor the evolution of conservation. Policies that increase the cost of extraction or penalize defectors promote conservation. However, the model does not report group efficiency or total payoff as a primary outcome, focusing instead on behavioral strategy frequencies and group survival.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, theoretical support for the idea that punishment and resource conservation can evolve and persist in group-structured resource dilemmas, especially under environmental variability and when group collapse is possible. However, it does not provide direct evidence or quantitative predictions about the effect of punishment on group efficiency or total payoff in public goods games or their close variants. The model's outcomes are mainly about the frequency of cooperative/punishing strategies and group survival, not efficiency as defined for the prediction task. Thus, while the mechanisms discussed (e.g., punishment reducing defection, group collapse selecting for cooperation) are relevant for understanding when punishment might improve group outcomes, the paper does not provide data or results that can be directly mapped to efficiency predictions for specific game design dimensions.

- source: Jin, Q., Wang, Z., Wang, Z., & Wang, Y. L. (2012). Strategy changing penalty promotes cooperation in spatial prisoner's dilemma game. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_tech
  findings: The introduction of a strategy changing penalty (a form of endogenous punishment for switching strategies) in a spatial prisoner's dilemma game increases the fraction of cooperators. Both higher penalty rates and longer memory (the period after which the penalty is lifted if the player does not change strategy again) monotonically promote cooperation, with the effect saturating at high memory. The mechanism works by inducing heterogeneity in fitness coefficients, which helps cooperator clusters survive and expand. The paper does not report group payoff, efficiency, or welfare, so the findings are about cooperation rates, not efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that endogenous penalty mechanisms can increase cooperation in spatial social dilemmas, but it does not report efficiency or group payoff. The game is a spatial prisoner's dilemma, not a public goods game, and the punishment is not peer-to-peer but is an automatic penalty for changing strategy. Thus, while the findings suggest that certain forms of punishment can promote cooperation, they do not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games. The main value is as adjacent, mechanistic support for the idea that punishment-like mechanisms can increase cooperation, especially when combined with memory or forgiveness.

  findings: The introduction of a reputation-based warning/punishment mechanism—where players are less likely to imitate neighbors who have persistently defected—substantially increases the fraction of cooperators in a spatial PDG simulation. Stronger punishment (higher discount factor) and more timely punishment (lower threshold for triggering the discount) both further increase cooperation rates. The effect is robust across a range of temptation-to-defect parameters. However, the paper does not report on group payoff, efficiency, or welfare, so the findings are limited to behavioral outcomes (cooperation rates) rather than payoff-based outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that punishment-like mechanisms targeting persistent defectors can increase cooperation rates in spatial PDG environments. However, because it does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. The findings may be useful for understanding mechanisms that increase cooperation, but should be used with caution for efficiency prediction, as higher cooperation does not always translate to higher group payoff depending on the cost structure of punishment and the payoff matrix.

- source: Goodman, J. R., Caines, A., & Foley, R. A. (2023). Shibboleth: An agent-based model of signalling mimicry. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost
  findings: The models show that increasing the sensitivity to mimicry (the ability to detect outsiders) is a much more effective way to maintain group-defining traits than increasing the cost imposed on detected mimics. Punishment mechanisms (altruistic punishment of in-group members who are insufficiently sensitive) can help maintain group traits, but their effect is less dramatic than that of detection sensitivity. The cost of punishment, even when high, does not strongly predict which trait will go to fixation. The models do not report group efficiency or payoff, but focus on trait fixation as a proxy for group integrity.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments, as it does not report efficiency, group payoff, or surplus. However, it provides indirect theoretical support for the idea that the effectiveness of punishment in maintaining cooperation (or group integrity) depends more on the likelihood of detection than on the severity of punishment. For prediction tasks, this suggests that punishment mechanisms that increase detection or monitoring may be more effective than those that simply increase punishment cost. The findings are most relevant to environments where group identity or norm compliance is the main outcome, rather than efficiency or payoff.

- source: Vukov, J., Santos, F. C., & Pacheco, J. M. (2012). Cognitive strategies take advantage of the cooperative potential of heterogeneous networks. *New Journal of Physics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
