  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that allowing players to use reactive strategies (punishing defectors and rewarding cooperators based on memory of past actions) leads to high and robust levels of cooperation in scale-free networks, even when participation costs are present. The effect is much stronger than for unconditional strategies. The main outcome is the fraction of cooperative acts, not group efficiency or total payoff. The model is based on the prisoner's dilemma, not a public goods game, but the mechanisms of punishment/retaliation and reward are closely related. The findings suggest that cognitive strategies can overcome the negative effects of participation costs and defector invasion on cooperation, but do not provide direct evidence about efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that punishment/retaliation mechanisms (as part of cognitive/reactive strategies) can sustain high cooperation rates in networked social dilemmas, even with participation costs and frequent defector invasion. However, because the model is a prisoner's dilemma (not a public goods game) and does not report efficiency or group payoff, its direct value for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments is limited. The main signal is that punishment/retaliation can be highly effective in maintaining cooperation under adverse conditions, especially when players can condition their behavior on others' past actions. The findings are most relevant for settings with network structure, memory, and the possibility of both punishment and reward.

- source: Helbing, D., & Johansson, A. (2010). Cooperation, Norms, and Revolutions: A Unified Game-Theoretical Approach. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the dynamics and outcomes of cooperation and norm establishment in multi-population games depend critically on the payoff structure (game type), the relative power of populations, and initial conditions. In multi-population prisoner's dilemma games, defection dominates regardless of power. In stag hunt games, shared norms can emerge, often set by the more powerful or initially larger group, but sometimes by the weaker group depending on initial conditions. In snowdrift games, polarization and abrupt 'revolutionary' transitions can occur. Costly punishment can transform a prisoner's dilemma into a stag hunt, supporting norm establishment. However, the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, focusing instead on behavioral equilibria and norm dynamics.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical insight into how punishment, payoff structure, and group power shape the emergence of cooperation and norms in environments related to, but not identical with, public goods games. It suggests that punishment can promote norm establishment by changing the effective game structure, but does not provide quantitative predictions about efficiency or group payoff. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper is only indirectly informative: it supports the idea that punishment can shift equilibria toward more cooperative outcomes in some settings, but does not quantify efficiency gains or losses. Its main value is in highlighting the importance of game structure and group asymmetries as moderators of punishment effects.

- source: 
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The main finding is that the coevolution of owner aggressiveness (defense) and intruder cautiousness (sensitivity to defense) can stabilize ownership of resources, even when defense is not directly effective at preventing theft. If intruders can observe or learn about owner aggressiveness and adjust their behavior accordingly, a deterrence effect emerges: owners become more aggressive, intruders become more cautious, and theft is reduced. This effect can occur even if defense is functionally equivalent to costly punishment with no direct preventive effect. The model demonstrates that variation in aggressiveness and the ability to respond to it are sufficient to trigger this feedback, leading to stable defense and low theft. However, the outcomes are primarily at the level of individual behavior and evolutionary stability, not group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that punishment-like mechanisms (costly defense) can deter antisocial behavior (theft) and stabilize cooperation (ownership) even when direct preventive effects are weak or absent, as long as individuals can observe and respond to others' willingness to punish. However, the model is not a public goods game and does not report group efficiency or payoff outcomes. Its relevance to predicting efficiency in PGG-like environments is indirect: it suggests that the deterrence effect of punishment may be underestimated if only direct preventive effects are considered, and that variation in punishment propensity and observability can be important moderators. The findings are most useful for mechanism reasoning and for understanding the conditions under which punishment can stabilize cooperation, but do not provide quantitative predictions for efficiency outcomes in PGGs.

  findings: The paper synthesizes theoretical advances in indirect reciprocity, showing that cooperation can be maintained in social dilemma settings when reputation mechanisms and justified punishment are present. It details how the refinement of assessment functions (e.g., leading eight norms) resolves issues like the scoring and punishment dilemmas, enabling stable cooperation under certain conditions. However, it notes that these models are not directly applicable to public goods games, as selective cooperation (the core of indirect reciprocity) is not possible in standard PGGs. The review also highlights the importance of the benefit-cost ratio, error rates, and the distinction between public and private reputation systems in determining the stability of cooperation. While punishment and reward mechanisms are discussed, the focus is on their role in norm stability and cooperation, not on efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical context for how punishment and reputation mechanisms can stabilize cooperation in social dilemmas, but it does not offer direct evidence for predicting efficiency outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment. Its models are adjacent to PGGs but differ in key structural ways (e.g., selective cooperation vs. public goods provision). The review is useful for understanding the mechanisms by which punishment and norm refinement can support cooperation, but it does not provide quantitative or comparative data on efficiency or group payoff. For the downstream prediction task, this paper suggests that the effectiveness of punishment depends on norm structure, reputation information, and error rates, but it does not allow for direct mapping from game design dimensions to efficiency outcomes in PGGs.

  findings: The paper finds that while the strict assumptions of the IPD are rarely met in nature, the pay-off structure (temptation to cheat, mutual investment) is relevant to many real-world cooperative interactions. Punishment (negative reciprocity) is identified as a mechanism that can sustain cooperation in IPD-like games, as illustrated by the cleaner fish example, where dominant individuals punish defectors to promote future cooperation. However, the paper does not report or synthesize efficiency or group payoff outcomes, focusing instead on behavioral strategies and the conditions under which cooperation is sustained.

However, it does not provide quantitative or experimental evidence on efficiency or payoff outcomes, nor does it systematically vary game design dimensions. As such, it is not directly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments, but it does support the plausibility of punishment as a mechanism for sustaining cooperation in IPD-like settings.

  findings: The paper finds that indirect reciprocity, supported by reputation mechanisms and sometimes by costly punishment (altruistic punishment), can stabilize cooperation in social dilemmas. Theoretical models show that discriminating strategies (which punish defectors by withholding help) can be evolutionarily stable under certain conditions, especially when information about others' behavior is available. The paper reviews experimental evidence that reputation and information about others' past actions increase cooperation, and that costly punishment is observed in public goods and related games. However, the paper does not report direct effects of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games; instead, it focuses on the conditions for the evolution and maintenance of cooperation.

It suggests that punishment (including costly punishment) can help stabilize cooperation, which may lead to higher efficiency, but does not provide direct quantitative evidence on efficiency outcomes. For prediction tasks, the paper supports the importance of information, group size, and the cost/benefit structure, but does not allow direct mapping from control efficiency to treatment efficiency with punishment enabled. Its value is mainly in mechanism and moderator identification rather than direct effect estimation.

- source: Wang, J. W., Xu, W. S., Zhang, X. J., Zhao, N. X., & Yu, F. Y. (2023). Redistribution based on willingness to cooperate promotes cooperation while intensifying equality in heterogeneous populations. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.

  findings: The paper finds that cooperation can persist in the presence of corrupt central sanctioning institutions, provided the effectiveness of sanctions (exclusion probability) is high and the corruption rate (embezzlement) is not too large. The model shows that players are more willing to tolerate exploitation by corrupt third parties than by free-riders, and that the evolutionary stability of cooperation depends on the balance between the benefits of cooperation, the costs of joining/leaving the institution, the intensity of exclusion, and the corruption rate. However, the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff as outcomes, focusing instead on the prevalence of cooperation and trust strategies.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, contextual evidence for the prediction task. It suggests that the effectiveness of punishment (exclusion) and the cost/benefit structure are critical for sustaining cooperation, even when institutions are corrupt. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used directly to predict efficiency outcomes. The results imply that punishment can support cooperation under a range of conditions, but the translation to efficiency is not explicit. The model is adjacent to public goods games but differs in structure (prisoner's dilemma with exclusion and embezzlement).

- source: Mameli, M. (2013). Meat made us moral: a hypothesis on the nature and evolution of moral judgment. *Biology & Philosophy*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  outcomes: primary=N/R | overall_effect=N/A

  findings: The paper argues that the evolution of moral judgment is rooted in emotional dispositions that support the enforcement of group-beneficial norms through punishment and meta-punishment. It draws on ethnographic and evolutionary accounts of hunter-gatherer societies, where collective punishment of cheaters and bullies (those who disrupt cooperation and resource sharing) is common and is enforced through both direct and reputational means (e.g., gossip, ostracism, disapproval). The author suggests that these mechanisms enabled stable cooperation in small groups, but the analysis is conceptual and evolutionary rather than empirical or quantitative. The paper does not provide direct evidence or predictions about efficiency or payoff changes due to punishment in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that punishment and meta-punishment mechanisms can stabilize cooperation in small, egalitarian groups, especially in contexts analogous to public goods dilemmas (e.g., meat sharing after cooperative hunting). However, it does not provide empirical estimates or direct predictions about efficiency or group payoff outcomes with or without punishment. Its relevance is mainly in supporting the plausibility of punishment as a stabilizer of cooperation, especially when punishment is collective, reputational, and embedded in group norms. For prediction tasks, this paper may inform the expected direction of punishment effects in small, highly interactive, reputation-sensitive groups, but it does not provide quantitative or design-specific guidance.

  findings: The paper finds that under both market and administrative supervision mechanisms, certain policy levers (subsidies, liquidated damages, tax rates, penalties) can increase the probability and stability of cooperation among heterogeneous agents in intellectual property activities. However, the effect is non-monotonic: excessive penalties or taxes can reduce cooperation. The main findings are about the conditions under which the system converges to full cooperation, noncooperation, or mixed states, rather than about efficiency or total group payoff. The results are based on theoretical modeling and numerical simulation, not empirical data.

While the setting is not a standard public goods game, and efficiency is not directly measured, the results suggest that moderate punishment and incentives can stabilize cooperation, but excessive punishment or taxation can backfire. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the idea that the design and magnitude of punishment/reward mechanisms are important moderators of cooperation, but does not provide quantitative efficiency outcomes.

- source: Boehm, C. (1997). Impact of the human egalitarian syndrome on Darwinian selection mechanics. *American Naturalist*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper proposes that in prehistoric human forager societies, egalitarian social norms—enforced by moralistic punishment and social sanctions—dampened within-group competition and amplified between-group variation. This, in turn, empowered group selection and enabled the evolution and maintenance of altruistic and cooperative behaviors, despite the presence of free riders. The author argues that active punishment and social isolation of noncooperators reduced the free-rider problem, making group-beneficial traits more likely to persist. However, these arguments are theoretical and based on ethnographic and evolutionary reasoning, not on experimental or quantitative data.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that punishment and social sanctions can promote cooperation and suppress free riding in group contexts, which is relevant to public goods game environments. However, it does not provide quantitative or experimental evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff, nor does it specify how game design dimensions (such as punishment cost, player count, or MPCR) would moderate these effects. Its value for prediction is limited to supporting the plausibility of punishment as a mechanism for sustaining cooperation, not for estimating efficiency outcomes in specific game designs.

The findings may be useful for understanding the behavioral mechanisms by which punishment can promote cooperation, but should be used with caution for payoff-based prediction tasks.

- source: Schlapfer, A. (2024). Cross-cultural differences in retaliation: Evidence from the soccer field. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent

  findings: The paper finds, at a theoretical level, that polycentric governance structures are more likely to support the emergence and stability of group-beneficial norms, including costly punishment, by satisfying four key conditions: (1) minimizing within-group variance, (2) reducing within-group selective pressures against prosocial traits (e.g., via effective punishment), (3) increasing between-group selective pressures, and (4) maximizing between-group variance. It argues that punishment can transform altruistic traits into individually rational behaviors when punishment is widespread and coordinated, and that polycentricity supports the self-sorting and coordination needed for this. However, the paper does not provide empirical data or direct predictions about efficiency or payoff changes due to punishment in public goods games or similar environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides conceptual and mechanistic support for the idea that punishment, when embedded in polycentric or self-sorting group structures, can enhance cooperation and group-beneficial outcomes. It suggests that the effectiveness of punishment in improving efficiency or welfare depends on the ability of groups to self-sort, coordinate, and monitor, and on the cost structure of punishment. However, it does not provide quantitative or empirical evidence that can be directly used to predict efficiency changes in public goods games with or without punishment. Its value for prediction is in highlighting the importance of group structure, self-sorting, and the scaling of punishment costs, rather than in providing parameterized estimates.

It reviews evidence that allowing punishment or graduated sanctions in lab and field settings increases cooperation and joint outcomes, but does not provide direct quantitative estimates of efficiency or group payoff. The chapter also notes that the effect of design dimensions (e.g., group size, repetition, communication, sanctioning) on cooperation is variable and context-specific.

- source: Rand, D. G., Tarnita, C. E., Ohtsuki, H., & Nowak, M. A. (2013). Evolution of fairness in the one-shot anonymous Ultimatum Game. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: Theoretical and simulation results show that, in finite populations with stochastic evolutionary dynamics, fairness (both higher offers and nonzero rejection thresholds) can be favored by natural selection in the Ultimatum Game, even without reputation or repeated interaction. The favored strategies depend on mutation rate and selection strength. The behavioral experiment supports the prediction that greater uncertainty about others' success and less behavioral consistency are associated with higher offers and/or demands. However, the paper does not report group efficiency, total welfare, or surplus, and the Ultimatum Game structure is only adjacent to public goods games with punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the evolution of costly punishment and fairness in strategic interactions, but its findings are not directly transferable to predicting efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games. The Ultimatum Game is a two-player bargaining game with a binary accept/reject punishment mechanism, not a multi-player public goods game with explicit punishment options. The main outcomes are behavioral (offer and demand), not group efficiency. Thus, while the paper supports the idea that costly punishment (rejection) can be favored and lead to fairer outcomes, it does not provide quantitative evidence for the efficiency impact of punishment in PGG-like environments. Use this paper for mechanism insight, not for direct prediction of efficiency outcomes.

- source: Martinez-Vaquero, L. A., Han, T. A., Pereira, L. M., & Lenaerts, T. (2015). Apology and forgiveness evolve to resolve failures in cooperative agreements. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated social dilemmas modeled as noisy iterated Prisoner's Dilemma, the ability to make commitments and to retaliate (revenge) after commitment breakdown increases the prevalence of cooperative strategies. When apology and forgiveness are added, cooperation is further boosted, but only if apology is sufficiently costly (sincere). If apology is too cheap, fake committers exploit the system, reducing cooperation. The model shows that the optimal apology cost is above the cost of cooperation but below the penalty for breaking commitment. The results are robust to noise and repeated interaction length. While the model is not a public goods game, it is adjacent, and the mechanisms studied (commitment, retaliation, apology, forgiveness) are relevant to understanding how punishment-like mechanisms affect cooperation and group payoff in repeated social dilemmas.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect but informative evidence for the prediction task. It shows that in repeated, noisy social dilemmas, mechanisms analogous to punishment (revenge/retaliation) and costly apology/forgiveness can increase cooperation and, by implication, group payoff/efficiency, especially when the cost and effectiveness of these mechanisms are well-calibrated. The findings suggest that enabling punishment-like responses after norm violations (commitment breakdown) and allowing for costly apology/forgiveness can improve efficiency, but only if the costs are set appropriately. The results are most transferable to repeated games with commitment and noise, and less so to one-shot or standard PGGs. The paper does not provide direct quantitative predictions for PGG efficiency, but supports the idea that punishment and compensation mechanisms can be beneficial for group outcomes under certain conditions.

- source: Martinez-Vaquero, L. A., Han, T. A., Pereira, L. M., & Lenaerts, T. (2017). When agreement-accepting free-riders are a necessary evil for the evolution of cooperation. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in one-shot games, the coexistence of proposers and acceptors can be necessary to keep defectors out of the population, especially when the cost of commitment must be paid before knowing if the other will accept (prior commitments). In repeated games, proposers alone can sustain cooperation, and the presence of acceptors can actually reduce cooperation by making it easier for defectors to invade. The introduction of an apology-forgiveness mechanism (where apologies after defection can restore cooperation) can be exploited by acceptors if the apology cost is too low, leading to lower cooperation. The results are framed in terms of evolutionary stability and the stationary distribution of strategies, with average payoff as the fitness criterion, but do not directly report group efficiency or welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, but it does offer adjacent theoretical insights about how commitment mechanisms (which can be seen as a form of pre-emptive contract or compensation for defection) affect the prevalence of cooperation and the exclusion of defectors. The findings suggest that the design of commitment mechanisms (timing of costs, ability to recognize intentions, presence of apology-forgiveness) can strongly moderate the success of cooperation-promoting strategies and thus the average payoff in the population. However, because the model is based on the prisoner's dilemma and not a public goods game, and does not include explicit punishment or sanctioning as in PGGs, its direct predictive value for the downstream task is limited. The results are most relevant for understanding how alternative mechanisms to punishment (commitments, compensation, apology) might affect cooperation and payoff in related social dilemmas.

- source: Graser, C., Fujiwara-Greve, T., García, J., & van Veelen, M. (2025). Repeated games with partner choice. *Plos Computational Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that introducing the option to leave one's partner in repeated prisoner's dilemma games (i.e., partner choice) leads to a substantial increase in average cooperation rates across a wide range of benefit-to-cost ratios and continuation probabilities. This is because partner choice enables endogenous assortment: cooperators can avoid defectors by leaving, which stabilizes cooperation. Theoretical analysis and agent-based simulations both show that, with partner choice, the population spends more time in cooperative states and escapes from universal defection more quickly. The paper compares strategies that punish by defecting (grim trigger) to those that punish by leaving, and finds that punishing by leaving is more robust to invasion and more likely to support cooperation. Although the main outcome is cooperation rate, higher cooperation implies higher average payoffs, so efficiency is indirectly improved by partner choice.
  prediction_guidance: For the downstream prediction task, this paper provides strong theoretical and simulation evidence that enabling partner choice (the ability to leave and rematch) in repeated prisoner's dilemma games increases cooperation and, by implication, group efficiency/payoff. While the setting is not a public goods game per se, the mechanism is closely related, and the results are likely to transfer to PGG-like environments where partner choice is possible. The paper does not study costly punishment or explicit sanctions, but rather 'punishment' by leaving. The findings suggest that, all else equal, enabling partner choice should be expected to increase efficiency relative to a control with no partner choice, especially in repeated dyadic games. The results are most directly relevant when the game is dyadic, repeated, and partner choice is the main intervention.

- source: Fulker, Z., Forber, P., Smead, R., & Riedl, C. (2021). Spite is contagious in dynamic networks. *Nature Communications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in dynamic networks where agents can choose interaction partners and update their strategies by imitating higher-payoff individuals, spiteful (costly harmful) behavior can reliably invade and fixate in the population. This occurs even when the overall network does not exhibit strong anti-correlation, due to local and transient anti-correlated interactions. The spread of spite is facilitated by reinforcement learning and imitation based on average payoff per interaction. When spiteful behavior dominates, average payoffs are lower than in populations of only 'Social' agents, indicating a reduction in group efficiency. The findings challenge classic conditions for the evolution of costly harm and suggest that dynamic partner choice is a powerful mechanism for the emergence of antisocial behavior.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is primarily theoretical and does not directly study public goods games or experimental punishment interventions. However, it provides indirect evidence that dynamic partner choice and imitation based on payoffs can enable the spread of costly harmful behaviors, which reduce group efficiency. In environments where agents can choose partners and update strategies based on payoffs, the introduction of punishment-like costly harm may decrease efficiency, especially if such behaviors can spread by imitation. The findings suggest caution in predicting positive efficiency effects from punishment in dynamic or endogenous network settings, as costly harm can become self-reinforcing and reduce welfare. The model is adjacent to PGGs and does not include a cooperative contribution stage, so direct quantitative prediction is limited, but the qualitative mechanism is relevant for understanding the risks of antisocial punishment or spite in flexible social environments.

- source: Ma, Y. P., Gonçalves, S., Mignot, S., Nadal, J. P., & Gordon, M. B. (2009). Cycles of cooperation and free-riding in social systems. *European Physical Journal B*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_other_summaries
  findings: The model predicts that, depending on parameters, communities can settle into fixed points (Nash equilibria) with stable mixtures of cooperators and free-riders, or exhibit persistent cycles in the fractions of cooperators and free-riders. The presence and width of the distribution of moral costs (idiosyncratic disutility for free-riding) are crucial for the emergence of cycles. Stronger social interactions (bandwagon effects) can stabilize high-cooperation equilibria and suppress cycles. The model does not include explicit punishment or sanctioning actions, but models the deterrent to free-riding as an internalized moral cost. The efficiency or total surplus of the group is not directly compared across different institutional designs (e.g., with/without punishment), so the findings are not directly predictive for the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGGs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is of limited direct use for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not model explicit punishment or sanctions, nor does it report efficiency outcomes in the standard sense. However, it provides indirect evidence that internalized costs for free-riding (moral disapproval) can sustain cooperation and lead to complex group dynamics, including cycles. The model's structure and results may be informative for understanding how heterogeneity in social preferences and costs can affect cooperation, but it does not provide quantitative predictions for the effect of punishment interventions or for the mapping from control to treatment efficiency in PGGs.

- source: Aktipis, C. A. (2004). Know when to walk away: contingent movement and the evolution of cooperation. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that a simple contingent movement rule ('Walk Away') enables cooperation to evolve and persist in spatial prisoner's dilemma environments, outperforming more cognitively complex strategies like TFT and PAVLOV under many conditions. Walk Away is robust to invasion, can invade populations of defectors at lower initial frequencies than other strategies, and is most successful when movement costs, error rates, and agent density are low. The model does not include explicit punishment or reward mechanisms, and outcomes are measured by the evolutionary success of strategies (frequency in the population), not by direct group efficiency or total payoff. The findings suggest that contingent movement (exit) can substitute for more complex memory-based or punishment-based strategies in promoting cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence relevant to the prediction task. It does not study public goods games or explicit punishment, but shows that contingent movement (exit after defection) can promote cooperation and evolutionary success in spatial prisoner's dilemma environments. The findings suggest that mechanisms allowing agents to avoid defectors (analogous to exit or partner choice) can be highly effective, even without explicit punishment or memory. However, because the model does not report group efficiency or total payoff, and does not manipulate punishment cost or technology, its direct applicability to predicting efficiency in public goods games with punishment is limited. The results are most relevant for understanding the role of partner choice, mobility, and spatial structure as alternatives or complements to punishment.

- source: Huang, S. X., Liu, X. S., Hu, Y. H., & Fu, X. (2023). The influence of aggressive behavior on cooperation evolution in social dilemma. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that introducing aggressive behavior (a form of costly punishment) into a prisoner's dilemma game can promote the persistence of cooperation by deterring defectors. Aggressive cooperators are able to suppress defectors, especially when the benefit of aggression (compensation) is high and the risk (mutual loss) is not too great. The system can reach various equilibria, including coexistence of multiple strategies, depending on parameter values. When the temptation to defect is low and aggressive compensation is high, cooperation dominates. As the temptation to defect increases, defectors re-emerge, but aggressive defectors can also help limit the dominance of non-aggressive defectors, allowing some cooperation to survive. The findings suggest that punishment-like mechanisms can improve group outcomes compared to a baseline with only cooperation and defection, though the paper does not quantify efficiency in the standard sense.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect but informative evidence that introducing a punishment-like mechanism (aggressive behavior) in a prisoner's dilemma environment can increase group payoff and support cooperation, especially when the cost-benefit tradeoff of punishment is favorable and the temptation to defect is not too high. For prediction tasks, the results suggest that enabling punishment (with moderate cost and sufficient effectiveness) is likely to improve efficiency relative to a no-punishment baseline, particularly in environments where the risk of mutual punishment is not prohibitive. However, the lack of explicit efficiency ratios and the focus on evolutionary stability rather than direct payoff comparisons limit the precision of quantitative predictions.

- source: Angourakis, A., Santos, J. I., Galán, J. M., & Balbo, A. L. (2015). Food for all: An agent-based model to explore the emergence and implications of cooperation for food storage. *Environmental Archaeology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds
  findings: The model demonstrates that cooperation in food storage is most likely to emerge and persist when (1) cooperative storage is at least as efficient as household storage, (2) there is moderate to high dependency on storable food, and (3) social norms regulating access to the cooperative stock are neither too strict nor too lax. Too strict enforcement (high reciprocity requirement) can undermine cooperation by excluding agents who fail to contribute due to bad luck, while too lax enforcement allows free-riding to undermine the common stock. The model does not directly report efficiency or group payoff in the public goods sense, but shows that cooperation increases the probability of avoiding shortages and, under certain conditions, can lead to surplus. The findings suggest that moderate sanctioning (via access control) is optimal for sustaining cooperation in common-pool resource settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect but informative evidence for the prediction task. While it does not report efficiency or group payoff in a public goods game with explicit punishment, it models a closely related common-pool resource dilemma where access to the common resource is conditional on past cooperation (a form of sanction). The results suggest that intermediate levels of sanctioning (not too strict, not too lax) maximize cooperation and system performance (in terms of shortage avoidance and potential surplus). For prediction tasks, this implies that punishment or exclusion mechanisms that are too harsh may reduce efficiency by discouraging recovery from bad luck, while too little enforcement allows free-riding. The model also highlights the importance of storage efficiency and dependency on the resource, which may map onto MPCR and baseline efficiency in public goods games. However, direct quantitative predictions of efficiency with and without punishment are not available from this model.

- source: Shuvo, M. F. R., & Kabir, K. M. A. (2024). Investigating the impact of environmental feedback on the optional prisoner's dilemma for insights into cyclic dominance and evolution of cooperation. *Royal Society Open Science*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that introducing a loner (abstention) strategy and environmental feedback into a two-player, three-strategy game fundamentally alters the evolutionary dynamics compared to the classic prisoner's dilemma. Defection is no longer evolutionarily stable; instead, cyclic dominance and coexistence of strategies emerge, especially under certain environmental conditions. The state of the environment (replenished or depleted) and feedback parameters (replenishment rate, draw payoff) determine whether the system exhibits pure strategy dominance, coexistence, oscillations, or chaos. The model shows that the option to abstain and environmental constraints can promote cooperation and prevent the dominance of defection, but does not provide direct predictions or measurements of group efficiency or total welfare as in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical insights into how adding abstention and environmental feedback to social dilemma games can suppress defection and promote more complex, often cooperative, population dynamics. However, because the model is not a public goods game (it is a two-player, three-strategy game) and does not include explicit punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, its direct applicability to predicting efficiency outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment is limited. The findings suggest that introducing additional strategies (like abstention) or environmental feedback can qualitatively change the evolutionary stability of cooperation and defection, but do not provide quantitative evidence or parameter estimates for efficiency outcomes in the context of public goods games with punishment. Thus, this paper is more useful for conceptual framing and mechanism discussion than for direct prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment in PGG-like environments.

- source: Romano, A., Saral, A. S., & De Dreu, C. K. W. (2025). Reciprocity evolves more readily in competitive than cooperative socio-ecologies. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: all_or_nothing
  findings: Reciprocal matching strategies, which reward cooperation and punish defection, are more likely to evolve and persist in competitive socio-ecologies where the risk of exploitation is high (e.g., Chicken/Hawk-Dove/Attack-Defend games) than in cooperative ones (Stag Hunt). In cooperative environments, unconditional cooperation dominates, while in competitive environments, anti-reciprocity (mismatching) and defection are more successful. The success of reciprocity increases with the probability of repeated interaction. The paper does not directly report on group efficiency or total payoff, but the evolutionary success of reciprocity implies it is adaptive in competitive settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that the effectiveness of punishment (as part of reciprocal strategies) in promoting cooperation depends on the competitiveness of the environment and the risk of exploitation. While it does not report group efficiency or payoff outcomes directly, it suggests that punishment (as part of reciprocity) is more likely to be adaptive and prevalent in competitive, high-risk environments. For prediction tasks, this implies that the effect of punishment on efficiency may be more positive in competitive, high-exploitation-risk games (e.g., Chicken, Hawk-Dove) than in cooperative games (e.g., Stag Hunt). However, the lack of direct efficiency or payoff data limits quantitative prediction.

- source: Cordes, C., Richerson, P. J., & Schwesinger, G. (2010). How corporate cultures coevolve with the business environment: The case of firm growth crises and industry evolution. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count
  findings: The paper finds that cooperation within firms is sustainable up to a critical group size, after which opportunistic behavior spreads and performance declines. In innovative, uncertain environments, cooperative cultures provide a competitive advantage, but as firms grow, the influence of informal leadership wanes and cooperation collapses. In mature, stable environments, large firms can succeed by implementing monitoring regimes and realizing economies of scale, even with more opportunistic employees. The model links these dynamics to firm growth crises, industry shakeouts, and the evolution of market structure. While the model is not a direct PGG or punishment experiment, it provides theoretical insight into how group size, monitoring, and social learning affect cooperation and firm performance.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, theoretical support for the idea that group size and the presence or absence of monitoring (analogous to punishment) are key moderators of cooperation and efficiency in group settings. It suggests that in small groups, informal cooperation can sustain high performance, but as group size increases, cooperation collapses unless formal monitoring (akin to punishment) is introduced. The model implies that enabling monitoring or sanctions in large groups can restore or maintain efficiency, especially in stable environments. However, the findings are not directly transferable to PGG lab experiments, as the context is organizational and the outcomes are modeled rather than measured. The paper is most useful for understanding the qualitative effects of group size and monitoring on efficiency, rather than for quantitative prediction.

- source: Wu, J. H., Számadó, S., Barclay, P., Beersma, B., Cruz, T. D. D., Lo Iacono, S., Nieper, A. S., Peters, K., Przepiorka, W., Tiokhin, L., & Van Lange, P. A. M. (2021). Honesty and dishonesty in gossip strategies: a fitness interdependence analysis. *Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the honesty of gossip is determined by the gossiper's fitness interdependence with the receiver and target, and by the marginal cost/benefit structure of the underlying game between receiver and target. A simple matching rule is proposed: gossipers are honest when their fitness interdependence aligns with the effect of honest gossip, and dishonest when it mismatches. The model is applied to four game types, including a punishment game, but the focus is on the strategic choice of gossip honesty rather than on direct efficiency or payoff outcomes. The paper suggests that honest gossip can support cooperation, but that dishonest gossip can undermine reputation-based cooperation, especially when fitness interdependence is negative or mixed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical insight into how information transmission (gossip) can act as a low-cost reputational punishment mechanism in social dilemmas, including punishment games. However, it does not provide empirical or simulated data on efficiency or group payoff, nor does it manipulate or analyze the standard public goods game dimensions. Its main contribution is to clarify when gossip will be honest or dishonest, which can indirectly affect cooperation and efficiency, but the paper does not quantify these effects. For prediction tasks about punishment effects on efficiency in public goods games, this paper is only indirectly informative, mainly by highlighting the importance of information accuracy and fitness interdependence in the effectiveness of reputational sanctions.

- source: Zhou, X., Belloum, A., Lees, M. H., van Engers, T., & de Laat, C. (2023). The dynamics of corruption under an optional external supervision service. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The introduction of an optional external supervision service can reduce corruption and promote cooperation, but its effectiveness depends on the cost of supervision and the mutation rates of players and enforcers. Lowering the cost of supervision is not always optimal: if the cost is too low, unnecessary supervision may occur, while if it is too high, corruption may persist. The initial fraction of honest enforcers and the relative mutation rates are critical: higher mutation rates for enforcers (relative to players) favor honest enforcement and trusting cooperation. In finite populations, the system may reach stable equilibria or exhibit persistent oscillations in the fractions of cooperators, defectors, and corrupt enforcers. The model does not report efficiency or group payoff, so direct implications for efficiency are not provided.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, mechanistic evidence about how punishment, corruption, and external supervision interact in a social dilemma context with bribery and enforcement. It does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency or group payoff as a function of game design dimensions. However, it suggests that the effectiveness of punishment (via external supervision) is highly sensitive to the cost of supervision and the mutation rates of enforcers and players. The model is adjacent to public goods games, but is not a standard PGG, and the main outcomes are behavioral (strategy fractions), not efficiency. Thus, the paper is more useful for understanding mechanisms and moderators of punishment and corruption than for quantitative prediction of efficiency.

- source: Manapat, M. L., Rand, D. G., Pawlowitsch, C., & Nowak, M. A. (2012). Stochastic evolutionary dynamics resolve the Traveler's Dilemma. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper finds that in the Traveler's Dilemma, evolutionary dynamics favor higher (more cooperative) claims when the reward/penalty R is small relative to the maximum claim M, and lower (less cooperative) claims when R is large. The model reproduces the empirical finding that average claims decrease as R increases. The analysis is focused on the distribution of claims, not on group efficiency or total payoff, and the Traveler's Dilemma is structurally different from public goods games: it is a two-player, competitive undercutting game with a specific bonus/penalty mechanism, not a multi-player contribution game. The findings are relevant for understanding how incentive structures (like R) shape cooperative behavior, but do not provide direct evidence on efficiency or punishment effects in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, adjacent evidence for the prediction task. It shows that in a two-player social dilemma with a reward/penalty for undercutting (Traveler's Dilemma), increasing the size of the penalty/reward (R) leads to less cooperative behavior (lower claims). However, the game structure is not a public goods game, and the outcomes are not group efficiency or total payoff, but rather the average claim. There is no direct manipulation or analysis of punishment in a PGG or direct efficiency outcomes. Thus, the paper may inform general intuitions about how incentive structures affect cooperation, but should not be used as direct evidence for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games.

- source: Guo, H., Chu, C., Shen, C., & Shi, L. (2018). Reputation-based coevolution of link weights promotes cooperation in spatial prisoner's dilemma game. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The study finds that reputation-based coevolution of link weights in a spatial prisoner's dilemma game promotes cooperation. When link weights are allowed to vary (parameterized by delta), higher heterogeneity in link weights leads to higher levels of cooperation. Punishment is implemented as a reduction in link weight to the lowest-reputation neighbor, and reward as an increase to the highest-reputation neighbor. The main outcome is the fraction of cooperators, not efficiency or group payoff. The effect of punishment and reward is to facilitate the formation of cooperative clusters, which resist invasion by defectors. The effect is stronger for larger delta (greater possible link weight variation).
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that reputation-based punishment and reward mechanisms can increase cooperation rates in spatial social dilemma settings. However, because the model is a spatial prisoner's dilemma (not a public goods game), and the outcome is cooperation rate rather than efficiency or group payoff, its direct applicability to predicting efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games is limited. The findings may be useful for understanding the role of network structure, reputation, and coevolving relationships in promoting cooperation, but do not provide quantitative guidance for efficiency prediction in PGG-like environments.

- source: Zhang, X. C., & Lu, S. C. (2024). The effect of survival pressure-based defector reward behavior on cooperation in spatial prisoner's dilemma games. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The study finds that when defectors are forced by survival pressure to reward cooperators, the frequency of cooperation in the population can increase, especially when the survival pressure threshold is low (players are more sensitive to pressure), the reward cost is low, and the reward amount is sufficiently high. The effect of these parameters is non-linear and depends on their combination with the temptation to defect. The model shows that rewarding behavior by defectors acts as a transitional strategy that can facilitate the emergence and maintenance of cooperation, but does not analyze efficiency or group payoff. The results are based on agent-based simulations on a lattice network.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency or group payoff in public goods games with or without punishment. Its findings are limited to the effect of a specific form of reward (not punishment) on cooperation rates in a spatial prisoner's dilemma. The model and results are adjacent to the target prediction task, as they do not include payoff-based outcomes or public goods game structures, nor do they manipulate or analyze punishment. The main relevance is in showing that certain forms of reward can increase cooperation rates under specific parameter regimes, but this cannot be directly translated into efficiency predictions for PGGs with punishment.

- source: Wardil, L., & da Silva, J. K. L. (2009). Adoption of simultaneous different strategies against different opponents enhances cooperation. *Epl*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that allowing players to adopt different strategies against different opponents, combined with a 'replace the worst confrontation' update rule (model A), leads to the dominance or persistence of cooperation in spatial prisoner's dilemma games on both square lattices and rings. This mechanism acts as a form of punishment by neutralizing exploiters, but does not impose a cost on the punisher. The fraction of cooperation remains high for a wide range of defection temptations and is robust to some misjudgment. However, the outcomes are reported in terms of cooperation rates, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. Its relevance is limited to the insight that certain update rules that allow targeted responses to defectors can sustain high cooperation rates, even without explicit costly punishment. However, because the model is a spatial prisoner's dilemma (not a PGG), and the outcome is cooperation rate (not efficiency or payoff), its transfer value to the prediction task is limited and indirect. The paper does not report or analyze group payoff, efficiency, or welfare, so it cannot be used to calibrate efficiency predictions for PGGs with or without punishment.

- source: 
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the probability of both firms adopting open (cooperative) green innovation strategies increases with higher excess benefits from cooperation, higher government subsidies, and higher government penalties for non-cooperation, and decreases with higher costs of cooperation and higher spillover benefits to non-cooperators. There are threshold values for each parameter that determine whether cooperation or non-cooperation is more likely. The model is theoretical and supported by numerical simulations, but does not report efficiency or total payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, mechanistic evidence about how punishment (government penalties) and reward (subsidies) can shift the likelihood of cooperative behavior in a two-player, repeated, public-goods-like innovation game. It does not provide direct evidence on efficiency or payoff outcomes, so it cannot be used to quantitatively predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. However, it supports the qualitative claim that increasing punishment or reward increases the probability of cooperative (open) strategies, which in analogous public goods games is often associated with higher efficiency. The model is adjacent to public goods games, but is not a direct PGG or experimental result.

- source: Li, X. P., Sun, S. W., & Xia, C. Y. (2019). Reputation-based adaptive adjustment of link weight among individuals promotes the cooperation in spatial social dilemmas. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that allowing link weights between players to adaptively increase or decrease based on reputation (interpreted as a form of social reward/punishment) leads to a substantial increase in the frequency of cooperation in spatial Prisoner's Dilemma and Snowdrift games. The effect is stronger as the range of possible link weights (controlled by ε) increases, which increases heterogeneity and supports the formation of cooperative clusters. The findings are robust across both game types. However, the paper does not analyze or report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, so its direct relevance to predicting efficiency outcomes is limited.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that dynamic, reputation-based social reward and punishment mechanisms (via link weight adjustment) can promote cooperation in spatial social dilemmas. However, because the outcomes are reported in terms of cooperation frequency rather than efficiency or group payoff, and because the model is a simulation of spatial PDG/SDG rather than a public goods game, its value for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments is limited and should be considered only as suggestive, not directly predictive.

- source: Rankin, D. J., & Eggimann, F. (2009). The evolution of judgement bias in indirect reciprocity. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that judgement bias—weighting bad acts more heavily than good acts in reputation updating—can evolve in indirect reciprocity models, especially when the benefit-to-cost ratio is low. Under 'standing' reputation rules, negative judgement bias is favored over a wider range of parameters than under 'scoring.' Allowing judgement bias to evolve increases the level of cooperation in the population. The model does not include explicit costly punishment, but negative judgement bias acts as a form of social sanctioning by reducing the likelihood of future help to defectors. The model does not report group efficiency or total payoff as an outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency in public goods games. However, it suggests that mechanisms that increase the reputational cost of defection (such as negative judgement bias) can promote cooperation, especially when the cost of cooperation is high. The findings are most relevant for settings where reputation-based indirect reciprocity is the main mechanism, rather than explicit costly punishment. The model does not report efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot be used directly for quantitative prediction of efficiency outcomes in PGGs with or without punishment.

- source: Yuan, H. R., Meng, X. Z., & Alzahrani, A. K. (2023). Analysis of dynamic evolution process of the N-player division of labor game model. *Mathematical Modelling of Natural Phenomena*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The model shows that high toxicity rates (especially against cheaters), high toxin levels, and smaller group sizes promote the disappearance of cheaters and the persistence of cooperation (cooperators and/or toxin producers). The system exhibits bistability: depending on initial conditions and parameters, the population may converge to all cooperators, coexistence of cooperators and toxin producers, or coexistence of cooperators and cheaters. The interior equilibrium (all three strategies coexisting) is always unstable. Bifurcation analysis reveals thresholds in absorption rates and toxin parameters that determine which strategies persist. The main reported outcomes are the frequencies of each strategy at equilibrium, not group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, mechanistic insight into how costly sanctioning (here, toxin production) can suppress free riding and promote cooperation in N-player games. However, the model is not a standard public goods game with explicit peer punishment, and does not report efficiency or group payoff as a main outcome. The findings suggest that increasing the effectiveness or prevalence of costly sanctions (analogous to punishment) can reduce free riding and increase the fraction of task-performers, especially in smaller groups and with higher sanction effectiveness. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the idea that punishment-like mechanisms can increase cooperation, but does not provide quantitative estimates of efficiency changes or direct mapping to standard PGG dimensions.

- source: Madeo, D., & Mocenni, C. (2020). Self-regulation versus social influence for promoting cooperation on networks. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that strong self-regulation (relative to social influence from neighbors) can lead to full cooperation in networked prisoner's dilemma games, while weak self-regulation leads to defection. For intermediate self-regulation, complex behaviors such as partial cooperation, reciprocity, and anti-reciprocity emerge. Network topology matters: scale-free networks foster higher average cooperation than Erdös-Rényi or regular networks, especially when self-regulation is moderate. The model suggests that social diversity and heterogeneity in network structure can promote cooperation, even when the 'cost' of self-regulation is not high. However, the study does not analyze efficiency or group payoff directly, nor does it include explicit punishment or reward treatments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their close variants. It models a continuous-strategy prisoner's dilemma with self-regulation (an internal, not externally imposed, mechanism) and analyzes cooperation levels, not efficiency or group payoff. There is no manipulation of punishment, punishment cost, or related design dimensions. The findings may be contextually informative for understanding how intrinsic motivation or network structure can affect cooperation, but do not inform the downstream prediction task regarding punishment-enabled efficiency.

- source: Wang, Z. R., Deng, Z. H., Wang, H. B., & Qu, Y. (2021). Moderate irrational sentiment-driven fitness can promote cooperation in the prisoner's dilemma game. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, reward_exists, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study shows that time-varying reputation mechanisms driven by environmental feedback can promote and sustain higher levels of cooperation in a spatial Prisoner's Dilemma game. The effectiveness of different reputation update rules depends on the temptation to defect (b): some mechanisms are more effective under low social dilemma intensity, while others are better under high intensity. Increasing the maximum possible reputation reward/punishment (ΔR_max) and the environmental sensitivity parameter (β) can further enhance cooperation. The main outcome is the fraction of cooperators, not group efficiency or payoff. No direct analysis of group payoff, welfare, or efficiency is provided.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games or their close variants. The model is a spatial Prisoner's Dilemma, not a public goods game, and the outcome is cooperation rate, not efficiency. The reputation mechanism is analogous to a form of social sanctioning, but it is not implemented as a costly punishment with explicit payoff consequences. Therefore, while the findings suggest that adaptive, environment-dependent sanctioning can promote cooperation, they do not provide quantitative or directly transferable evidence for the efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments. The results may be useful for mechanism design intuition, but not for direct prediction of efficiency outcomes.

- source: Fu, F., Chen, X. J., Liu, L. G., & Wang, L. (2007). Promotion of cooperation induced by the interplay between structure and game dynamics. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The study finds that allowing agents to adapt their social ties by breaking links with the most frequent defectors (after a multi-round PDG) and rewiring to others can promote the emergence and dominance of cooperation in the population. The interplay between network structure adaptation and strategy evolution is key: there is a critical rate of network adaptation above which cooperators can dominate. The model explores how parameters such as temptation to defect, number of rounds, average connectivity, and selection intensity affect the prevalence of cooperation. However, the outcomes are reported in terms of cooperation rates and network properties, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. Its model is adjacent: it studies a networked PDG with social tie breaking as a form of sanction, but does not include explicit costly punishment or measure efficiency/welfare. The findings suggest that adaptive social structure (link breaking as a response to defection) can promote cooperation, but the results are not directly transferable to standard PGGs with explicit punishment and efficiency outcomes. Use this paper as weak, indirect support for the idea that social sanctions (even non-costly ones) can promote cooperation, but do not use it for quantitative prediction of efficiency effects.

- source: Bruner, J. P., & Smead, R. (2022). Tag-based spite with correlated interactions. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper finds that the introduction of observable tags and the possibility of conditioning behavior on tags (e.g., only harming out-group members) fundamentally changes the evolutionary dynamics of costly harming (spite) in the Prisoner's Delight game. Tags make pure (unconditional) spite less likely to evolve even when anti-correlation would otherwise favor it, but make conditional (tag-based) spite more likely in some regimes. Several stable equilibria are identified, including cooperative polymorphisms (mostly cooperation, little spite), pure spite, and a novel 'treacherous polymorphism' where spite is directed within groups. The presence of tags generally reduces the prevalence of widespread spiteful behavior, even under conditions that would otherwise favor it. The results are framed in terms of evolutionary stability and behavioral frequencies, not group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their close variants. The model is a two-player game (Prisoner's Delight) with costly harming, not a multi-player public goods game. The main outcomes are the evolutionary frequencies of behavioral types, not group efficiency or total payoff. However, the analysis is relevant for understanding how the structure of punishment-like mechanisms (costly harming, conditional on observable tags) and population structure (correlated/anti-correlated interactions) can affect the prevalence of cooperation and spite. The findings suggest that mechanisms allowing for conditional punishment (e.g., based on group identity) can both promote and suppress harmful behavior, depending on the context. For the downstream prediction task, this paper provides indirect theoretical context about how punishment-like behaviors may interact with population structure and signaling, but does not provide quantitative or empirical evidence for efficiency outcomes in PGG-like environments.

- source: Hu, Q., Jin, T., Jiang, Y. L., & Liu, X. W. (2024). Reputation incentives with public supervision promote cooperation in evolutionary games. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
