  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, reward_exists, reward_tech
  findings: The study finds that a reputation incentive mechanism with public supervision can significantly increase the fraction of cooperators in a spatial PDG. The effect is stronger when the evaluation intensity (the range of possible evaluation values) is lower, allowing players to more quickly update their evaluations and reputations. The model demonstrates that reputation-based rewards can compensate cooperators for losses due to defectors, promoting the formation and persistence of cooperative clusters. However, the paper does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, so its findings are limited to behavioral outcomes (cooperation rates) rather than payoff-based outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that reputation-based reward mechanisms with public supervision can increase cooperation rates in spatial PDG-like environments, especially when evaluation intensity is low. However, because the model is not a public goods game and does not report efficiency or group payoff, it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGGs. The findings may be useful for understanding the role of reputation and reward mechanisms as adjacent or complementary to punishment, but should not be used as direct evidence for efficiency outcomes in PGGs with punishment.

- source: Zhang, N., Zhang, X. X., Lei, M., & Yang, Y. J. (2020). Multiagent Collaborative Governance for Targeted Poverty Alleviation from the Perspective of Stakeholders. *Complexity*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that: (1) Increasing support from local governments and social organizations to poverty groups can promote the willingness of poverty groups to cooperate, but excessive support from higher-level government does not necessarily lead to more cooperation or better outcomes. (2) Moderate increases in punishment (negative returns) for noncooperation by social organizations and poverty groups can encourage cooperative behavior. (3) Increasing the basic economic benefits for social organizations and poverty groups significantly increases their willingness to cooperate. The model suggests that a combination of incentives and moderate sanctions is more effective than top-down subsidies alone. However, the main outcomes are the equilibrium rates of cooperation, not efficiency or group payoffs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, contextual evidence for the prediction task. It does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, but it does analyze how punishment (modeled as negative returns for noncooperation) and other design parameters affect the evolution of cooperation in a multi-agent setting. The findings suggest that moderate punishment can increase cooperation rates, which in standard public goods games is often associated with higher efficiency, but this is not directly demonstrated here. The model is more complex than standard PGGs, involving three types of agents and context-specific payoffs. Thus, the paper is best used for qualitative, indirect support: it reinforces that punishment and incentives can promote cooperation, but does not provide quantitative estimates of efficiency effects for PGG-like environments.

- source: Wang, Q., & Mao, C. M. (2024). Research on Cooperative Water Pollution Governance Based on Tripartite Evolutionary Game in China's Yangtze River Basin. *Water*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, reward_exists
  findings: The study finds that the evolution toward cooperative governance (strict regulation, green production, active public participation) is promoted by lower regulatory costs, higher fines for non-compliance, appropriate subsidies for green production, and material rewards for public participation. Excessively high or low subsidies or rewards can destabilize cooperation. The model identifies five equilibrium points, with the ideal being all stakeholders choosing the cooperative strategy. The findings are based on theoretical modeling and simulation, not empirical data or observed payoffs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, contextual evidence about the mechanisms by which punishment (fines), rewards, and regulatory costs affect the evolution of cooperation in a multi-stakeholder environmental governance setting. While the model structure is related to public goods games and includes punishment and reward analogues, it does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, nor does it provide empirical estimates. The results may inform qualitative expectations about the effects of punishment and reward mechanisms, but cannot be used for quantitative prediction of efficiency changes in PGG-like experiments.

- source: Li, G. Y., Du, H. F., & He, X. C. (2024). The evolutionary prisoner's dilemma game in continuous signed networks. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that negative relationships, modeled as punitive ties that reduce payoffs for defectors, can promote the emergence and stability of cooperation in networked prisoner's dilemma games. The effect is stronger when the negative coefficient (punishment strength) or the proportion of negative ties is higher. Adaptive networks, where agents can adjust their ties, further facilitate cooperation. Structural balance (alignment of positive ties within groups and negative ties between groups) also supports higher cooperation. However, the paper does not report on group efficiency, welfare, or total payoff, focusing instead on the behavioral outcome of cooperation rates.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that punishment-like mechanisms (here, negative relationships that reduce payoffs) can increase cooperation rates in networked social dilemmas. However, because the main outcome is the proportion of cooperators rather than efficiency or group payoff, and because the model is a prisoner's dilemma rather than a public goods game, its direct value for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments is limited. The findings suggest that increasing the cost or prevalence of punishment can promote cooperation, but do not quantify the effect on group efficiency or welfare.

- source: Koike, S., Nakamaru, M., & Tsujimoto, M. (2010). Evolution of cooperation in rotating indivisible goods game. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that in a rotating indivisible goods game (a model of ROSCAs), cooperation collapses unless both a peer selection rule (based on reputation) and a forfeiture rule (excluding non-contributors from receiving goods) are present. Peer selection alone or forfeiture alone is insufficient. The model tracks the evolution of cooperation probabilities before and after receiving goods, showing that only the combination of both rules leads to sustained high cooperation. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, so its findings are limited to behavioral outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, contextual evidence for the prediction task. While it does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes, it shows that in a public-goods-adjacent environment with indivisible goods, the presence of both peer selection (reputation-based group formation) and a forfeiture rule (exclusion of non-contributors) is necessary to sustain high cooperation rates. For prediction, this suggests that punishment-like mechanisms (here, exclusion and peer selection) may only be effective in combination, and that the structure of the game (indivisible goods, rotation, exclusion) is critical. However, the lack of direct efficiency or payoff data limits its direct applicability to efficiency prediction.

- source: Testori, M., Giardini, F., Cruz, T. D. D., & Beersma, B. (2023). What Matters Most in Supporting Cooperation, the Gossip Content or the Gossiper's Intention? Simulating Motive Interpretation in Gossip Dynamics. *Jasss-the Journal of Artificial Societies and Social Simulation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_other_summaries
  findings: The model shows that cooperation can be sustained or undermined depending on how agents interpret the motives behind gossip and how they update reputations. If gossip is interpreted as pro-social and agents use a single reputation system, cooperation can rise to high levels. If gossip is interpreted as selfish or negative, a double reputation system (where agents discount unreliable gossip but do not punish the gossiper by refusing to cooperate) helps maintain initial cooperation levels. The model highlights the importance of motive interpretation and the structure of reputation systems for sustaining cooperation, but does not analyze efficiency or payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency or payoff effects of punishment in public goods games. However, it offers indirect insights into how information transmission, reputation updating, and the interpretation of motives can moderate the effects of social information on cooperation. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that mechanisms which allow agents to discount unreliable information without ostracizing the source may help sustain cooperation, but the model's outcomes are in terms of cooperation rates, not efficiency or group payoff.

- source: Shutters, S. T., & Hales, D. (2013). Tag-Mediated Altruism is Contingent on How Cheaters Are Defined. *Jasss-the Journal of Artificial Societies and Social Simulation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that the sustainability of tag-mediated altruism is highly sensitive to how cheaters are defined. When cheaters are defined as agents who can never donate (strong cheaters), altruism collapses completely and rapidly. With medium cheaters (who can revert to altruism via mutation), donation rates are very low. Only with ultraweak or weak cheaters do substantial donation rates persist. The paper also finds that parameters such as neighborhood size, number of pairings, and mutation rate affect donation rates, but the presence of strong cheaters overwhelms these effects. The authors suggest that additional mechanisms, such as punishment or multi-level selection, may be necessary to sustain cooperation in the presence of strong cheaters, but these are not modeled directly.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or close variants. It does not implement punishment or sanctions, nor does it report group efficiency or payoff outcomes. However, it is informative in showing that, in tag-based donation models, the presence of strong cheaters (analogous to unpunished defectors) eliminates cooperation, and that additional mechanisms (such as punishment) may be required to sustain altruism. For the prediction task, this paper suggests that in environments where strong, unpunished defection is possible, efficiency will be low unless additional mechanisms (like punishment) are present. However, it does not provide quantitative estimates or direct manipulation of punishment, so its value for prediction is mainly conceptual and indirect.

- source: Huang, K. K., Zheng, X. P., Li, Z. J., & Yang, Y. Q. (2015). Understanding Cooperative Behavior Based on the Coevolution of Game Strategy and Link Weight. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that allowing link weights between players to coevolve with strategies in a spatial prisoner's dilemma can significantly enhance the fraction of cooperators, especially at intermediate values of link weight amplitude. The mechanism works by reinforcing links to successful neighbors and weakening links to less successful ones, which leads to the formation and expansion of cooperator clusters. The effect is non-monotonic: too little or too much link weight adaptation is less effective than an intermediate level. The study does not report on efficiency or group payoff, so its findings are only indirectly relevant to predicting efficiency outcomes in public goods or punishment games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It uses a spatial prisoner's dilemma (not a PGG) and studies the coevolution of strategies and link weights (not explicit punishment or sanctions). The main outcome is cooperation rate, not efficiency or payoff. However, the findings may be contextually informative for understanding how dynamic network structures or adaptive relationships can promote cooperation, which could be relevant for models where network adaptation or endogenous partner selection is a feature. For the core prediction task, this paper offers only adjacent, indirect support.

- source: Wu, Y. E., Zhang, S. H., & Zhang, Z. P. (2018). Environment-based preference selection promotes cooperation in spatial prisoner's dilemma game. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that introducing environment-based preference selection (parameter \alpha) and environmental heterogeneity (parameter u) into a spatial prisoner's dilemma game increases the fraction of cooperators. The combination of these mechanisms is more effective than either alone. The effect is robust across a range of parameters and network sizes. However, the paper does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or any payoff-based outcome, and does not include punishment or reward mechanisms. The findings are thus relevant for understanding cooperation dynamics but not directly for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not include punishment, reward, or efficiency outcomes. Its findings on cooperation rates in spatial prisoner's dilemma games with preference selection and heterogeneity may be contextually informative for understanding mechanisms that promote cooperation, but are not directly transferable to the prediction task focused on punishment and efficiency.

- source: Huang, S. X., Liu, X. S., Hu, Y. H., & Fu, X. (2023). The influence of confidence in aspiration-fulfillment strategy on prisoner's dilemma evolution. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that a confidence mechanism—where confidence in a strategy is updated based on the difference between payoff and aspiration—can promote and stabilize cooperation in a spatial prisoner's dilemma game. The effect of the confidence increment parameter (alpha) is non-monotonic: smaller alpha can lead to higher cooperation under some conditions, but too small an alpha or high temptation to defect undermines cooperation. Common aspirations in the group support higher cooperation than heterogeneous aspirations. There is a threshold effect for aspiration: below the threshold, increasing aspiration increases cooperation, but above it, cooperation collapses. However, all findings are in terms of cooperation rates, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not manipulate or analyze punishment, sanctions, or payoff-based outcomes. Its findings on confidence and aspiration mechanisms in spatial prisoner's dilemma games may be of limited indirect relevance for understanding how psychological or updating mechanisms affect cooperation, but not for the efficiency prediction task as defined. The absence of payoff or efficiency outcomes and the lack of punishment or reward mechanisms mean this paper should not be weighted for the core prediction task.

There are optimal values for the link weight adjustment intensity and memory length that maximize cooperation. - source: Wu, Y., Zhang, Z. P., Wang, X. Y., & Chang, S. H. (2019). Impact of probabilistic incentives on the evolution of cooperation in complex topologies. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, reward_exists
  findings: The study finds that probabilistic positive incentives (rewards) for cooperators promote the evolution and maintenance of cooperation, while probabilistic negative incentives (anti-social punishment) undermine it. These effects are robust across different network structures (square lattice, Erdös-Rényi, Barabási-Albert) and for both the prisoner's dilemma and snowdrift games. The main dependent variable is the stationary fraction of cooperators. The paper does not report on group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, so its findings are limited to behavioral outcomes (cooperation rates) rather than payoff-based outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the likely direction of punishment and reward effects on cooperation rates in two-player evolutionary games, but does not provide direct evidence about efficiency or group payoff. Its results suggest that positive incentives (rewards) increase cooperation and negative incentives (anti-social punishment) decrease it, but the transferability to public goods games and to efficiency outcomes is limited. The absence of payoff or efficiency analysis means this paper should be used only as weak, indirect support for predicting efficiency effects of punishment or reward in public goods game-like environments.

- source: Deng, L. L., Zhang, X. X., & Wang, C. (2021). Coevolution of spatial ultimatum game and link weight promotes fairness. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, reward_exists
  findings: The coevolution of game strategy and link weight in a spatial ultimatum game model leads to higher average proposal and acceptance levels, interpreted as greater fairness. The key driver is the link weight boundary control parameter (\delta), which, when increased, allows for greater heterogeneity in social ties and promotes fairer strategies. The adjustment amplitude (\Delta) has a smaller or context-dependent effect. The model does not analyze or report group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, so its direct relevance to efficiency prediction in public goods or similar games is limited.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. The model is an ultimatum game (not a PGG or direct variant), and the main outcomes are behavioral (fairness, proposal/acceptance levels) rather than payoff-based. The 'punishment' in this context is the weakening of social ties, not costly punishment of the type used in PGGs. Therefore, while the findings may be conceptually adjacent—suggesting that dynamic social relationships can promote fairer behavior—they do not inform quantitative predictions about efficiency or payoff outcomes in PGGs with or without punishment.

Higher reputation factors and higher upper bounds on consistent strategy times both further promote cooperation. The mechanism is more effective and leads to faster stabilization of cooperation than traditional reputation mechanisms. However, because it does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. Its relevance is further limited by the use of a spatial PDG rather than a PGG or direct payoff-based analysis. It may be useful for mechanism design intuition, but not for quantitative prediction of efficiency outcomes.

- source: Calderón, J. P., & Zarama, R. (2006). How learning affects the evolution of strong reciprocity. *Adaptive Behavior*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in evolutionary simulations of the ultimatum game, agents with greater phenotypic plasticity (ability to adapt their offer and acceptance strategies during their lifetime) evolve to make higher offers and demand higher minimum acceptable offers, compared to rigid agents. This is interpreted as the emergence of strong reciprocity and altruistic punishment. The diversity of behaviors also increases with plasticity. However, the study does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, and all outcomes are at the level of individual behavioral strategies.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the evolution of punishment and cooperation in a bargaining game (ultimatum game), not a public goods game. It does not report efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. However, it suggests that mechanisms increasing behavioral diversity and adaptability can promote more cooperative and punishing behaviors. The findings are only adjacent to the prediction task, as the game structure, outcome measures, and focus differ from standard PGGs.

- source: Zhang, Y. L., Yang, S., Chen, X. J., Bai, Y. B., & Xie, G. M. (2023). Reputation update of responders efficiently promotes the evolution of fairness in the ultimatum game. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that indirect reciprocity mechanisms based on reputation can strongly promote fairness in the ultimatum game. Specifically, updating responders' reputation via image scoring leads to a very high level of fairness (over 95%), while updating proposers' reputation alone increases fairness above the baseline (no reputation) for many social norms, with a few norms achieving over 80% fairness. When both proposers' and responders' reputations are updated, most social norms yield even higher fairness than updating proposers' reputation alone, but never exceed the effect of updating responders' reputation alone. The results are based on evolutionary game-theoretic modeling and analysis of strategy dynamics, not on empirical or experimental data. The main dependent variable is the frequency of fair offers and rejections of unfair offers, not group payoff or efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, adjacent evidence for the prediction task. While it does not study public goods games or efficiency/payoff outcomes directly, it analyzes how indirect reciprocity and reputation mechanisms affect fairness (a behavioral outcome) in the ultimatum game, which is a related but distinct environment. The findings suggest that mechanisms analogous to punishment (here, rejection of unfair offers, i.e., altruistic punishment) can strongly promote norm compliance and fairness, especially when reputation is at stake. However, because the outcome is not efficiency or group payoff, and the game is not a public goods game, the results should be used only as weak, indirect evidence for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments.

- source: Podder, S., Righi, S., & Takács, K. (2021). Local reputation, local selection, and the leading eight norms. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that the leading eight social norms for indirect reciprocity can maintain high levels of cooperation in networked populations, even when reputation and strategy updating are local rather than global. However, the ability to sustain cooperation depends on the accuracy of reputation transmission, the speed of evolutionary updating, and the specific norm used. Norms that reward justified defection are less robust to noise and invasion by defectors. The main outcome is the proportion of cooperation, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games with or without punishment. Its findings are adjacent: they show that certain reputation-based norms can sustain high cooperation in repeated pairwise Prisoner's Dilemma games on networks, and that the robustness of cooperation depends on information and evolutionary parameters. For prediction tasks about efficiency in public goods games with punishment, this paper suggests that the structure of information and the harshness of social norms can affect the sustainability of cooperation, but it does not quantify efficiency or payoff outcomes. Use this paper for qualitative context about the importance of information structure and norm design, not for quantitative prediction of efficiency.

- source: Wang, J., Tian, J. C., & Li, X. P. (2024). Enhancement of cooperation induced by information-payoff evolution on two-layer complex networks. *Physics Letters A*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that introducing dynamic elimination (removal of defectors) in a networked donation game greatly increases cooperation frequency, but at the cost of eliminating many players. Adding the ZD strategy further increases cooperation and allows more players to remain in the game. The combination of elimination and ZD strategy is robust to changes in game parameters and identification errors. Larger-degree nodes are more likely to survive, and the optimal initial strategy for survival depends on whether ZD is present. However, the study does not report group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, focusing instead on behavioral and structural outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that mechanisms analogous to punishment (elimination of defectors) and sophisticated strategies (ZD) can increase cooperation and player retention in dynamic networked games. However, because it does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. The findings may be useful for understanding the behavioral and structural consequences of punishment-like mechanisms, but not for quantitative prediction of efficiency outcomes.

- source: Vukov, J. (2014). Accurate reactions open up the way for more cooperative societies. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The study finds that agents with more accurate and detailed memory (mem-1 strategies) can achieve much higher levels of cooperation in a spatial prisoner's dilemma than agents with only reactive memory. This effect is robust across a wide range of temptation-to-defect values, provided that the copying process is accurate (low error/noise). When copying errors increase, cooperation collapses. The paper interprets these results as showing that cognitive sophistication enables more stable and widespread cooperation, which in turn is associated with higher average payoffs, though payoff is not the primary outcome. There is no explicit punishment or sanctioning institution; instead, retaliation and forgiveness are embedded in the strategy space.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is only indirectly relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It does not study public goods games or explicit punishment mechanisms. Instead, it analyzes how cognitive sophistication in memory and strategy (allowing for retaliation and forgiveness) affects cooperation in a spatial prisoner's dilemma. The findings suggest that mechanisms enabling accurate, context-sensitive responses (such as retaliation or forgiveness) can promote cooperation and, by implication, higher payoffs, but this is not directly mapped to efficiency or to institutional punishment. The results may be useful for understanding the role of memory, strategy complexity, and error in sustaining cooperation, but do not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment interventions on efficiency in PGG-like environments.

- source: Wardil, L., & da Silva, J. K. L. (2010). Distinguishing the opponents promotes cooperation in well-mixed populations. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that allowing players to distinguish between opponents and update their strategies in the interaction that gave the worst payoff leads to the evolutionary stability of cooperation in well-mixed populations. Under synchronous update, cooperation eventually dominates, while under asynchronous update, the fraction of cooperation remains at the lower bound set by the initial mutual cooperation. The mechanism of 'implicit punishment'—where exploitative (C,D) interactions are replaced by mutual defection (D,D)—prevents defectors from exploiting cooperators indefinitely, but does not involve explicit costly punishment. The model does not include explicit payoff-based punishment or reward mechanisms, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is of limited direct use for predicting the effect of explicit punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their close variants, as it studies a repeated Prisoner's Dilemma with a novel update rule and no explicit costly punishment or reward. The 'implicit punishment' mechanism is a structural feature of the strategy update process, not a player choice to pay a cost to reduce another's payoff. The main outcome is the fraction of cooperation, not efficiency or group payoff. The findings may be relevant for understanding how mechanisms that neutralize exploitation without explicit costs can stabilize cooperation, but do not provide quantitative evidence on the efficiency effects of punishment in standard PGG-like environments.

- source: Khatun, K., Shen, C., Shi, L., & Tanimoto, J. (2024). Stability of pairwise social dilemma games: Destructive agents, constructive agents, and their joint effects. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that in pairwise social dilemma games, introducing destructive agents (who opt out and harm others) does not promote cooperation, in contrast to their effect in public goods games. Instead, destructive agents destabilize or replace defection (in prisoner's dilemma and stag-hunt) or undermine cooperation (in harmony and chicken games). Constructive agents (who opt out and benefit others) can have similar destabilizing effects if their own payoff is high, but if their payoff is lower than what they give, they mainly destabilize defection without harming cooperation. When both agent types are present, the system exhibits complex multistable equilibria, but cooperation is not promoted. The analysis is entirely theoretical, using replicator dynamics and stability analysis. No efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their close variants. It does not model punishment, sanctions, or payoff-based outcomes, and its focus is on pairwise (dyadic) games rather than multiplayer public goods games. The closest relevance is in its discussion of how certain opt-out strategies (destructive/constructive agents) affect the stability of cooperation, but not efficiency or group payoff. Therefore, this paper should not be weighted strongly in predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments. It may be useful for understanding the limits of voluntary participation and opt-out mechanisms in pairwise games, but not for the core prediction task.

  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The study finds that increasing the length of historical memory (M) and the weight of memory information (β) in a spatial prisoner's dilemma model leads to higher cooperation rates and more stable cooperative clusters. The effect saturates at high memory lengths, and excessive memory does not further promote cooperation. The model does not include explicit punishment or reward mechanisms, and all outcomes are reported in terms of cooperation rate and strategy stability, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their close variants. It models a spatial prisoner's dilemma with memory-based strategy updating, focusing on how memory and its weight affect cooperation rates. There is no manipulation or analysis of punishment, reward, or efficiency outcomes. Thus, its relevance to the downstream prediction task is limited to providing indirect, adjacent evidence that memory and historical information can promote cooperation, but it does not inform the effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Danku, Z., Perc, M., & Szolnoki, A. (2019). Knowing the past improves cooperation in the future. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that when agents in spatial social dilemma games (prisoner's dilemma, snowdrift, stag-hunt) update their strategies based on a combination of current and past payoffs, cooperation is strongly promoted compared to the standard model where only current payoffs matter. This effect is robust to the details of how the past is incorporated (weighted average or single past payoff with decaying probability). The mechanism works by slowing down the invasion of defectors into cooperative clusters, allowing cooperation to persist and spread. The effect is most pronounced when the memory window is sufficiently long or the selected past payoff is not too far in the past. The results are shown for the stationary fraction of cooperators, not for efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or closely related environments. It does not manipulate or analyze punishment, nor does it report efficiency, welfare, or group payoff outcomes. Its findings are about how memory of past payoffs (a strategy-neutral mechanism) can promote cooperation in spatial social dilemmas. While the results may be conceptually relevant for understanding how information and history affect cooperation, they do not inform the prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment in public goods games. Thus, this paper should not be used as direct evidence for the downstream prediction task, except perhaps as very weak, indirect context for the role of history or memory in cooperation.

- source: Tanimoto, J., & Sagara, H. (2015). How the indirect reciprocity with co-evolving norm and strategy for 2 x 2 prisoner's dilemma game works for emerging cooperation. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The study finds that stable cooperation via indirect reciprocity is more robust in environments with a stronger dilemma (higher temptation to defect), as this encourages the evolution of norms that punish second-order free-riders. In contrast, weaker dilemmas allow permissive norms to dominate, leading to fragile cooperation. Allowing agents to interact with others holding different norms undermines indirect reciprocity unless the image score is evaluated by the focal agent. Introducing a small action error (randomness) can help robust, punishing norms emerge by preventing the early dominance of overly permissive norms. The main dependent variable is the cooperation fraction; average payoff is shown but not analyzed in depth.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the role of norm-based punishment (targeting second-order free-riders) in sustaining cooperation in social dilemma environments. However, it does not use a public goods game, does not implement explicit costly punishment, and does not report efficiency or group payoff as a main outcome. The findings suggest that mechanisms which allow for the punishment of second-order free-riders (whether via norms or explicit punishment) may be important for robust cooperation, especially in more challenging (stronger dilemma) environments. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the idea that the structure of sanctioning (including norm evolution and the ability to punish non-punishers) can be critical, but it does not provide direct quantitative evidence for efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like games.

- source: Li, S. L., He, Z. X., Jia, D. Y., Shen, C., Shi, L., & Tanimoto, J. (2024). Granting leaders priority exit options promotes and jeopardizes cooperation in social dilemmas. *Neurocomputing*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that granting exit rights to high-degree (leader) nodes in a spatial prisoner's dilemma can either suppress or promote cooperation, depending on the strength of the social dilemma and the network's assortativity. In weak dilemmas, leader-priority exit reduces cooperation, while in strong dilemmas, it can promote cooperation by isolating defectors and allowing cooperative clusters to survive among low-degree nodes. Random and fringe-priority exit have weaker or negligible effects. The main outcome is the relative cooperation ratio among non-exiters. The model does not report group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, and is not a public goods game. The exit option is not a punishment mechanism per se, but can act as a hard sanction by denying payoffs to others.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is not directly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. The game is a spatial prisoner's dilemma with an exit option, not a PGG, and the main outcome is cooperation rate, not efficiency or group payoff. The exit option is not a punishment mechanism, though it can function as a hard sanction. The findings may be relevant for understanding how voluntary exclusion or exit options affect cooperation in networked dilemmas, but do not provide direct evidence for the efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments. Use this paper only for very indirect, mechanistic insights about how exclusion or exit options might interact with network structure and dilemma strength.

- source: Lu, S. N., & Wang, Y. (2024). Adjustment of link weights based on tolerance promotes cooperation in spatial prisoner's dilemma game. *Biosystems*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The study finds that allowing individuals to reduce interaction intensity with neighbors who defect beyond a tolerance threshold increases the frequency of cooperation in a spatial prisoner's dilemma. However, if the tolerance threshold is too high (i.e., individuals are too forgiving), cooperation is undermined. The effect is stronger when more individuals in the population are 'environmental adaptors' who adjust their interaction intensity. The main outcome is the equilibrium frequency of cooperators, not group payoff or efficiency. The findings are robust to changes in network size and initial cooperator fraction.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about mechanisms that may promote cooperation in repeated social dilemmas, specifically through adaptive partner selection or ostracism based on tolerance for defection. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff, and the game is a spatial prisoner's dilemma, not a public goods game. The results suggest that mechanisms allowing individuals to reduce interaction with defectors (after a tolerance threshold) can increase cooperation rates, but excessive tolerance undermines this effect. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency in public goods games with punishment, this paper is only weakly informative, as it does not analyze punishment costs, efficiency, or public goods structures.

  findings: The study finds that incorporating both direct and indirect experience in interaction and strategy update rules, as well as introducing tolerance in link weight adjustment, significantly promotes cooperation in spatial Prisoner's Dilemma simulations. High sensitivity to defection (low tolerance) and greater weight on indirect experience both increase the frequency of cooperation and the average payoff of cooperators relative to defectors. However, the model does not include explicit punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, and does not report group efficiency or total welfare outcomes. The findings are based on simulation results and are not directly transferable to public goods games with punishment.

However, it does not study public goods games, does not implement explicit punishment or sanctioning, and does not report group efficiency or total welfare. Thus, its relevance for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games is limited and should be considered only as weak, mechanistic context.

- source: Zhang, Y., Hao, Q. Y., Qian, J. L., Wu, C. Y., Guo, N., & Ling, X. (2024). The cooperative evolution in the spatial prisoner's dilemma game with the local loyalty of two-strategy. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.

  findings: The study finds that incorporating local loyalty to both cooperation and defection strategies in a spatial prisoner's dilemma game can promote the emergence and stability of cooperation, especially when the strength index of local loyalty is positive and the weighting between payoff and loyalty is balanced. There are optimal parameter values that maximize cooperation frequency, and the system can reach full cooperation under these conditions. However, the paper does not report on group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, and all results are based on simulation of behavioral outcomes (cooperation rates and loyalty patterns).

Its findings may be contextually informative for understanding how social mechanisms can promote cooperation, but it does not provide quantitative or qualitative evidence for the efficiency prediction task.

- source: Gao, L. Y., Pan, Q. H., & He, M. F. (2021). Environmental-based defensive promotes cooperation in the prisoner's dilemma game. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.

  findings: The paper finds that allowing cooperators to take defensive actions (at a cost) in response to environmental cues (number of other cooperators) can prevent the collapse to full defection and promote higher levels of cooperation. The effectiveness of this mechanism depends on the defensive cost, defensive benefit, defensive loss, and the threshold for defensive awareness. When defensive measures are sufficiently beneficial and not too costly, and when cooperators are sufficiently vigilant (high threshold and probability), the system can reach full cooperation or stable coexistence. However, the main reported outcome is the fraction of cooperators, not group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect theoretical evidence that mechanisms allowing individuals to reduce exploitation (analogous to defensive or sanctioning actions) can increase cooperation rates in social dilemmas. However, because the model is based on the prisoner's dilemma (not a public goods game) and reports cooperation fraction rather than efficiency or group payoff, its direct value for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments is limited. The results suggest that the effectiveness of defensive/sanctioning mechanisms depends on their cost, benefit, and the conditions under which they are triggered, which may inform the expected direction of punishment effects in related models. However, quantitative predictions for efficiency cannot be extracted from this paper.

  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A

  findings: The introduction of a defensive cooperation strategy (where cooperators pay a cost to reduce losses against defectors) can promote and sustain higher levels of cooperation in both well-mixed and structured populations, compared to the standard prisoner's dilemma. The effectiveness of this strategy depends on the balance between the defensive cost and defensive benefit. When the defensive benefit exceeds the cost, defensive cooperators can suppress defectors and allow cooperation to persist. However, excessive defensive benefit can lead to second-order free-riding by unconditional cooperators. The model shows cyclic dominance among the three strategies in structured populations. The paper does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, so its findings are not directly translatable to efficiency prediction tasks.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. Its model is a theoretical extension of the prisoner's dilemma with a defensive cooperation strategy, which is conceptually adjacent to punishment but not equivalent. The outcomes are behavioral (strategy fractions), not payoff-based. Therefore, while the paper suggests that self-interested defensive strategies can promote cooperation under certain conditions, it does not provide quantitative or qualitative evidence about efficiency or group payoff that can be directly used for the downstream prediction task. Its relevance is limited to mechanism discussion and indirect contextualization.

- source: Li, W. J., Jiang, L. L., & Perc, M. (2021). A limited mobility of minorities facilitates cooperation in social dilemmas. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.

  findings: The paper finds that in a spatial Prisoner's Dilemma model, allowing individuals to move away from defectors with low probability (limited mobility) increases the frequency of cooperation by enabling the formation and expansion of cooperative clusters. High mobility, however, undermines cooperation by disrupting network reciprocity. The authors interpret mobility away from defectors as a form of punishment, but the model does not include explicit punishment or reward mechanisms. The main reported outcome is cooperation frequency, not efficiency or group payoff. The paper claims that limited mobility of minorities can promote cooperation more effectively and with less public resource cost than explicit punishment or reward, but this is not empirically tested.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. The model is a spatial Prisoner's Dilemma, not a PGG, and the main outcome is cooperation frequency, not efficiency or group payoff. The analogy between mobility/alienation and punishment is discussed, but there is no explicit manipulation of punishment cost, effectiveness, or related design dimensions. The findings may be contextually informative for understanding how indirect forms of sanctioning (like alienation) can promote cooperation, but they do not provide quantitative or direct evidence for the prediction task focused on efficiency outcomes in PGGs with or without punishment.

For some parameterizations, stable intermediate levels of cooperation emerge; in others, the system converges to all-cooperation or all-defection. The results are often unpredictable except in special cases (e.g., greedy or conformist agents with global neighborhoods). The paper does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, nor does it include explicit punishment or reward mechanisms as in standard public goods games.

The game studied is adjacent to PGGs in that it is an N-person game with binary choices and payoff externalities, but it lacks explicit punishment/reward actions and does not report efficiency or group payoff. The findings may be useful for understanding how nonlinear payoff structures and local interactions affect cooperation rates, but they do not inform the prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment interventions.

  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  findings: The paper finds that the introduction of a 'three-faced' strategy—one that defects against cooperators, punishes defectors, and cooperates with its own kind—can suppress defection and promote cooperative behavior in a population, depending on parameter values. Low role-switching costs or high government rewards for punishing defectors favor the spread of the three-faced strategy and/or cooperators. High punishment costs or high temptation to defect favor defectors. The model identifies parameter regimes where cooperators, defectors, and three-faced individuals coexist, or where one strategy dominates. However, the paper does not report group efficiency or total payoff, focusing instead on the evolutionary stability and proportions of each strategy.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, theoretical evidence about the effects of punishment-like mechanisms in a prisoner's dilemma context with a novel adaptive strategy. It does not report efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot be used directly for predicting efficiency outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment. However, it suggests that punishment-like behaviors (when not too costly and/or when subsidized) can suppress defection and support the maintenance of cooperation, especially when adaptive strategies are possible. The findings may inform qualitative expectations about the role of punishment cost, effectiveness, and incentives, but do not provide quantitative efficiency data for prediction.

- source: Godino, I. B. I., Santos, J. I., Galán, J. M., Caro, J., Alvarez, M., & Zurro, D. (2014). Social Cooperation and Resource Management DynamicsAmong Late Hunter-Fisher-Gatherer Societies in Tierra del Fuego (South America). *Journal of Archaeological Method and Theory*.

  findings: The ABM shows that cooperation (publicly calling others to share a resource) is stable when social capital (reputation) is valued in the fitness function and when the visibility of actions (vision) is high, making it easier to detect defectors. When only material gain matters, defection dominates. The system tends to polarize into either high cooperation or high defection regimes, with intermediate states rare. The model highlights the importance of indirect reciprocity and the public observability of actions for sustaining cooperation. However, the model does not report group efficiency or total payoff as outcomes, focusing instead on behavioral regimes.

It suggests that punishment via social exclusion or reputation loss (rather than explicit costly punishment) can sustain cooperation if actions are observable and social capital is valued. However, because the model does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. Its main value is in highlighting the importance of observability and social capital as moderators of cooperation, which may inform the interpretation of similar dimensions in empirical studies.

- source: Cui, G. H., Wang, Z., Ren, J. K., Lu, K., & Li, M. C. (2016). Promotion of cooperation induced by discriminators in the spatial multi-player donor-recipient game. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.

  findings: The introduction of a discriminative cooperation strategy in a spatial multi-player donor-recipient game can maintain high levels of cooperation and prevent the dominance of defectors, especially when the cost-to-benefit ratio is low and the cost of information collection is small. Discriminators act as conditional cooperators who deny resources to defectors, which functions as a form of indirect punishment. The model shows that, under certain parameter regimes, all three strategies (cooperation, defection, discrimination) can coexist in a cyclic dominance pattern, and the ratio of donated to total resources can remain high. However, the model does not directly report group efficiency or total welfare in the sense of public goods games, and the structure is not a standard PGG.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that conditional, discriminative strategies (akin to targeted punishment or exclusion) can sustain cooperation and high resource donation in spatial multi-player settings, especially when the cost of such discrimination is low. However, because the model is not a standard public goods game and does not report group efficiency or total payoff, its findings should be transferred to PGG-like prediction tasks with caution. The main implication is that mechanisms which allow players to conditionally withhold benefits from defectors (even at a cost) can be effective in maintaining cooperation, but the quantitative effect on efficiency in PGGs is not directly established.

  findings: The main finding is that spatial structure in the ultimatum game leads to the evolution of fairer offers and higher acceptance thresholds compared to non-spatial (random encounter) settings. In well-mixed populations, average offers and acceptance levels are low, approaching zero as mutation error decreases. In spatially structured populations (1D and 2D lattices), average offers and acceptance levels are much higher, approaching a fair split in some cases. The paper interprets rejection of low offers as a punishment mechanism that, when combined with spatial structure, supports the evolution of fairness. However, the paper does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or total earnings, so its findings are only indirectly relevant to payoff-based prediction tasks.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that spatial structure and local competition can support the evolution of fairer, more cooperative behavior in bargaining games where punishment is endogenous (via rejection). However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. Its main value is in highlighting the importance of spatial structure and local interactions for the evolution of fairness and the effectiveness of endogenous punishment mechanisms.

The model shows that the presence of random disturbances (stochasticity) leads to fluctuations in strategies but does not fundamentally alter the stable outcomes. The analysis highlights the importance of effective incentive and punishment mechanisms for coalition stability, but these are discussed at a conceptual level rather than modeled as explicit game interventions. The main findings are about the conditions for stable coalition formation, not about the effect of punishment on efficiency.

  findings: The study finds that when individuals in a network break social conventions for choosing partners, the effects depend on the convention in use. In 'intermediary-based' (betweenness) networks, rule-breakers primarily affect their own social position, and only at higher frequencies do they impact group organization. In 'popularity-based' (in-degree) networks, rule-breakers do not affect their own or others' individual positions, but as their frequency increases, they significantly alter the overall network structure (decentralization). The paper discusses the potential for rule-breaking to transform social systems, but does not measure or report efficiency, payoff, or welfare outcomes.

The paper may be useful for mechanism discussion or for understanding the importance of social structure, but not for direct prediction in the target task.

- source: Newton, P. K., & Ma, Y. (2021). Maximizing cooperation in the prisoner's dilemma evolutionary game via optimal control. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds
  findings: The paper shows that by optimally controlling the off-diagonal elements of the prisoner's dilemma payoff matrix (interpreted as dynamically changing incentives and penalties), it is possible to minimize the defector population (maximize cooperation) over a fixed time horizon. The optimal control is bang-bang and depends on the initial state. The reduction in defectors is most efficient for small initial defector populations. The method cycles through a sequence of different games (PD, Leader, Deadlock, etc.) to achieve the optimal outcome. The approach is adaptive over multiple cycles, recalculating the optimal schedule based on the current state. However, the study does not report on group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, and is not based on experimental or empirical data.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It is a theoretical study of optimal control in a two-population evolutionary prisoner's dilemma, focusing on minimizing defection via dynamic changes to the payoff matrix. There is no explicit punishment mechanism, no group payoff or efficiency outcome, and the setting is not a public goods game. The findings may be conceptually relevant for understanding how dynamic incentives can shape cooperation, but do not provide quantitative or experimental evidence for the prediction task.

- source: Sinatra, R., Iranzo, J., Gómez-Gardeñes, J., Floría, L. M., Latora, V., & Moreno, Y. (2009). The Ultimatum Game in complex networks. *Journal of Statistical Mechanics-theory and Experiment*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds
  findings: The main findings are that network topology and the type of evolutionary update rule strongly influence the distribution of offers and acceptance thresholds in the Ultimatum Game. Under natural selection, strategies converge toward fair offers (around 50%) in both homogeneous and heterogeneous networks, but scale-free networks allow for a broader range of surviving strategies due to the influence of hubs. Under the social penalty rule, which penalizes the lowest-payoff player and its neighbors, scale-free networks promote polarization: hubs can afford to be highly generous, while low-degree nodes may be more selfish. When players can independently choose offers and thresholds, the population tends to self-organize into pragmatic (role-ignoring) strategies, especially under social penalty. However, the paper does not report on group efficiency or total payoff, so the implications for efficiency are not directly addressed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments, as it studies the Ultimatum Game and focuses on the evolution of behavioral strategies rather than payoff or efficiency outcomes. The 'social penalty' mechanism is a form of network-level selection rather than direct costly punishment. While the findings may be informative about how network structure and update rules affect the evolution of cooperation and fairness-related behaviors, they do not provide quantitative or qualitative evidence about efficiency changes due to punishment in PGGs. Therefore, this paper should be considered only as weak, adjacent evidence for the prediction task.

- source: Wang, Q., He, N. R., & Chen, X. J. (2018). Replicator dynamics for public goods game with resource allocation in large populations. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent

  findings: The paper finds that in structured populations, the coevolutionary dynamics of cooperation and environmental state are more diverse than in well-mixed populations. Depending on payoff parameters and the number of neighbors, the system can exhibit persistent oscillations, bistability (with both depleted and replete environmental states as attractors), coexistence of oscillation and dominance, or stable coexistence of cooperation and defection. The number of neighbors (degree) can either expand or shrink the basin of attraction for favorable environmental states, depending on the ratio of enhancement/degradation rates or payoff parameter ratios. However, the model does not include explicit punishment or reward mechanisms, and does not report group efficiency or total payoff as an outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It does not manipulate or model costly punishment or sanctions, nor does it report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes. The model is adjacent to PGGs in that it studies cooperation and defection in a structured population with environmental feedback, but the 'punishment' parameter is the matrix payoff for mutual defection, not a sanctioning institution. The findings may inform how structural parameters (e.g., number of neighbors) and payoff ratios affect the maintenance of cooperation and environmental quality, but do not provide quantitative or qualitative evidence about the effect of punishment interventions on efficiency.

- source: Wang, R. W., He, J. Z., Wang, Y. Q., Shi, L., & Li, Y. T. (2010). Asymmetric interaction will facilitate the evolution of cooperation. *Science China-life Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper develops a theoretical model of the asymmetric Hawk-Dove game, showing that the frequency of cooperation increases with greater asymmetry between players (i.e., when one player is much stronger or more dominant than the other). The model also shows that a lower cost-to-benefit ratio of cooperation increases cooperation frequency. The discussion links these findings to real-world systems where dominant individuals can punish non-cooperators, suggesting that credible punishment and asymmetry can stabilize cooperation. However, the paper does not provide direct predictions or data on efficiency or group payoff, focusing instead on the probability of cooperation and conflict.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect theoretical support for the idea that asymmetry and credible punishment can increase cooperation rates in social dilemmas, but it does not provide quantitative predictions about efficiency or group payoff. Its relevance to predicting efficiency in public goods games with punishment is limited, as it analyzes a two-player Hawk-Dove game rather than a multi-player public goods game, and focuses on cooperation frequency rather than efficiency. The discussion of punishment is conceptual, not formalized as a game design dimension. Thus, while the paper suggests that punishment and asymmetry can facilitate cooperation, it does not provide direct evidence for the efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games.

- source: Gong, H. W., Xiang, H. J., Wang, Y. F., Gao, H. J., & Meng, X. Z. (2024). Strategy evolution of a novel cooperative game model induced by reward feedback and a time delay. *Aims Mathematics*.

  findings: The paper finds that in a theoretical evolutionary game with dynamic, feedback-based rewards (but no punishment), the system can exhibit multiple stable equilibria, bistability, and oscillatory dynamics depending on parameters and time delay. Increasing the reward does not always increase cooperation; under some conditions, even maximal rewards do not induce cooperation. The initial state can determine which equilibrium is reached. Introducing time delay can destabilize previously stable equilibria and induce stable limit cycles (oscillations) in the proportion of cooperators and reward intensity. The model does not analyze group payoff, efficiency, or welfare, and does not include punishment or sanctions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their close variants. It is a theoretical analysis of a different class of evolutionary games (general 2x2 matrix) with dynamic rewards and time delay, focusing on the evolution of cooperation and reward intensity. There is no analysis of efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, and no implementation of punishment or sanctions. The findings may be of limited, indirect interest for understanding how dynamic incentives and time delays affect cooperation dynamics, but they do not inform the prediction task regarding punishment effects on efficiency.

- source: Martinez-Vaquero, L. A. (2023). Inequality leads to the evolution of intolerance in reputation-based populations. *Chaos*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that economic inequality is a strong enhancer of intolerance in reputation-based populations. Intolerant strategies can invade tolerant groups more easily when inequality is high, even if overall economic stress is moderate. Redistribution of wealth can prevent or reverse the spread of intolerance, but only if implemented before intolerance becomes widespread. The model shows that tolerant minorities under economic stress may reduce their willingness to punish defectors or may even help wealthy discriminators. The main reported outcomes are the intention to cooperate and the evolutionary stability of strategies, not group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, contextual evidence for the prediction task. It suggests that in reputation-based cooperation environments, inequality and group structure can strongly moderate the effectiveness of punishment-like mechanisms (here, reputation-based exclusion) in sustaining cooperation. However, because the model does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. The findings are most useful for understanding how inequality and group structure might interact with punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, but not for quantitative prediction of efficiency outcomes.

  findings: The paper proposes that the ability to create and enforce institutional rules, including sanctioning mechanisms, is a key driver of large-scale human cooperation and division of labor. Institutions can transform the default equilibrium of social dilemmas (such as public goods games) to more cooperative, higher-payoff outcomes by enabling coordination, information sharing, and self-enforcing sanctioning. Theoretical arguments and historical/anthropological examples are used to support the claim that institutions can increase group efficiency and welfare, but the paper does not provide new empirical evidence or quantitative estimates of efficiency changes due to punishment or institutional design.

However, it does not provide quantitative or empirical evidence that can be directly used to predict efficiency changes as a function of game design dimensions or control efficiency. Its value for prediction is in supporting the plausibility and mechanism of positive effects of institutions (including punishment) on efficiency, but it does not inform the magnitude or specific moderators of these effects.

- source: Gardner, A., West, S. A., & Barton, N. H. (2007). The relation between multilocus population genetics and social evolution theory. *American Naturalist*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper demonstrates that multilocus population genetics provides a general framework for modeling the evolution of social behaviors, including cooperation and punishment. In a simple two-locus model (one for cooperation, one for punishment), it finds that cooperation and punishment cannot invade unless cooperation is already favored by relatedness (i.e., punishment does not expand the parameter space for cooperation). Moreover, increased relatedness directly selects against punishment, as punishment is mutually costly. The only benefit to punishment comes from its association with cooperation, but this is insufficient to overcome the direct costs. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, focusing instead on evolutionary stability and gene frequency change.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. Its findings are relevant for understanding the evolutionary logic of cooperation and punishment, and suggest that costly punishment may not increase cooperation or group benefit except under restrictive conditions. However, because it does not analyze efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, it should not be used as a primary source for quantitative prediction of efficiency outcomes in experimental public goods games. It may be useful for mechanistic or theoretical context, especially regarding the evolutionary logic of punishment and cooperation.

- source: 
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
