  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that spiteful and conditionally spiteful strategies can evolve and predominate in finite, fluctuating populations, especially when population sizes are small and/or pre-play signaling is possible. Spite increases the risk of extinction, but conditional spite (which targets out-group members based on signals) can persist and even dominate under a wide range of conditions. The presence of signaling allows for the evolution of conditional spite, which the authors suggest may be a proto-form of punishment. However, the outcomes are about evolutionary prevalence and extinction risk, not about efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. Its models are adjacent, focusing on the evolution of spite and conditional spite (as a possible origin of punishment) in a Prisoner's Delight game, not a public goods game. The outcomes are about which strategies predominate and the risk of extinction, not about group efficiency or payoff. The findings may inform high-level reasoning about the evolutionary origins of punishment and the role of signaling, but do not provide quantitative or direct evidence for the efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments.

- source: Wittman, D. (2005). The internal organization of the family: Economic analysis and psychological advice. *Kyklos*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that advice from clinical psychology on setting boundaries and using natural or logical consequences (rather than punishment) in families is consistent with economic reasoning about property rights and efficiency. It argues that punishment systems in families create externalities and deadweight losses, and that structuring choices so that parents are indifferent to children's decisions (the 'indifference principle') minimizes manipulation and strategic behavior. The paper suggests that minimizing emotional involvement and not using punishment leads to more functional, efficient family relationships, but these claims are theoretical and not empirically tested.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide empirical evidence or quantitative findings relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. Its arguments are theoretical and analogical, suggesting that punishment may introduce inefficiencies (externalities, deadweight loss) in small-group settings, and that structuring choices to avoid punishment may be more efficient. However, these claims are not tested in controlled experiments or public goods game variants, so the paper offers only indirect, conceptual support for the idea that punishment can reduce efficiency by increasing transaction costs and strategic manipulation.

- source: Forber, P., & Smead, R. (2016). The Evolution of Spite, Recognition, and Morality. *Philosophy of Science*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that mechanisms enabling recognition and conditional behavior can support both altruism and spite, but with important asymmetries. Recognition based on exogenous signals (e.g., greenbeard effects) is more evolutionarily stable with spite than with altruism, because mimics threaten altruism more than spite. In contrast, reciprocity based on past behavior (as in repeated games) is more conducive to the evolution of altruism than spite. The models show that reciprocal altruism evolves readily in repeated games, while reciprocal spite is rare. The paper does not analyze efficiency or group payoff, but rather the evolutionary stability and frequency of these behaviors.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. Its models are adjacent, focusing on the evolutionary conditions for the emergence and stability of conditional harming (spite) and helping (altruism) behaviors, and the coevolution of recognition mechanisms. While it discusses repeated games and conditional strategies, it does not report or predict group efficiency, welfare, or payoff outcomes. The main relevance is conceptual: it suggests that the evolutionary stability of punishment-like behaviors depends on the mechanism of recognition and the type of conditionality (signal-based vs. reciprocity), but it does not provide quantitative or empirical evidence for efficiency effects in PGG-like games.

- source: Pandula, N., Akcay, E., & Morsky, B. (2024). Indirect reciprocity with abductive reasoning. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that abductive reasoning in indirect reciprocity models can, under certain social norms and low error rates, promote higher levels of cooperation than non-reasoning models. For the Scoring norm, abductive reasoning enables stable cooperative states that are not possible without reasoning. For other norms (Simple Standing, Staying, Stern Judging), abductive reasoning can outperform or underperform non-reasoning depending on the error rates and the degree of confirmation/falsification bias. Under the Shunning norm, abductive reasoning leads to only defection. The model is more robust to errors in observation/assessment than to errors in action. The main reported outcomes are the equilibrium frequencies of cooperative strategies and intended cooperation rates, not group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, mechanistic insight into how reasoning about errors and reputation assignment can affect the evolution of cooperation in indirect reciprocity settings. While it does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes, it suggests that the cognitive process for assigning reputations (abductive reasoning) and the social norm in use can be critical moderators of cooperation rates. For prediction tasks involving efficiency in PGG-like games with punishment, this paper is only adjacent: it does not model explicit PGGs, group-level payoffs, or direct costly punishment, but it does highlight the importance of error handling and norm choice for sustaining cooperation. Its findings may inform mechanistic priors about the robustness of cooperation to errors and the role of cognitive biases, but cannot be used for quantitative prediction of efficiency.

- source: Falk, A., & Fischbacher, U. (2006). A theory of reciprocity. *Games and Economic Behavior*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists
  findings: The paper develops a psychological game-theoretic model of reciprocity, where individuals' utility depends on both material payoffs and the perceived kindness or unkindness of others' actions, which in turn depends on both outcomes and intentions. The model explains why people sometimes punish defectors or reward cooperators, even at a cost to themselves, and why the same material outcome can elicit different responses depending on the perceived intention behind it. The theory is shown to be consistent with a range of experimental findings, such as higher rejection rates for unfair offers in the ultimatum game, positive wage-effort relationships in gift-exchange, and conditional cooperation in public goods games. However, the paper does not provide new empirical data or direct predictions about efficiency or group payoff changes due to punishment in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides a theoretical framework for understanding when and why individuals will punish or reward others in social dilemmas, including public goods games. It highlights the importance of both outcomes and intentions in driving reciprocal behavior. However, it does not provide quantitative predictions or empirical estimates of how punishment affects efficiency or group payoff in public goods games. For the downstream prediction task (predicting efficiency with punishment enabled), this paper offers conceptual guidance on the psychological mechanisms underlying punishment, but does not supply direct evidence or parameter values for efficiency outcomes. Its value is mainly in informing the types of behavioral responses to expect under different game structures, especially regarding the role of intentions and available alternatives.

- source: Liu, L. L. (2018). The dynamic of general compliance with the OECD anti-bribery convention: two interpretative approaches. *Crime Law and Social Change*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper argues that the enforcement of the OECD Anti-Bribery Convention is best understood through a combination of rational-choice and institutional approaches. It highlights the collective action problem inherent in international anti-bribery efforts, likening it to a public goods game with free-rider issues. The rational-choice approach explains 'under-enforcement' as a result of insufficient incentives and ineffective centralized monitoring or sanctioning. The institutional approach emphasizes the role of domestic institutional contexts and 'unconscious enforcement' by agencies like the US SEC, whose evolving missions and regulatory environments lead to increased enforcement over time. The paper suggests that decentralized monitoring involving private sector whistleblowers and inter-country competition may be more effective than centralized mechanisms. However, it does not provide empirical data or analyze efficiency or payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides conceptual and theoretical context for understanding how punishment, monitoring, and institutional design might affect cooperation and compliance in public-goods-game-like environments, especially at the international treaty level. However, it does not provide empirical evidence or quantitative estimates of efficiency or payoff effects of punishment or monitoring mechanisms. Its relevance to predicting efficiency in experimental public goods games is limited to its discussion of collective action, monitoring, and sanctioning as mechanisms, rather than direct evidence. It may inform mechanism design or the interpretation of monitoring and punishment variables, but does not support direct prediction of efficiency outcomes.

  findings: The paper finds that gossip, understood as the spread of reputation (meta-evaluations with hidden sources), enables more efficient social control by reducing the costs and risks (especially retaliation) associated with punishing or excluding cheaters. Ethnographic evidence shows that gossip is structured to avoid direct responsibility and retaliation, while agent-based simulations demonstrate that reputation-based information exchange can maintain higher product quality and more robust partner selection even with many cheaters. However, these findings are in the context of market-like artificial societies and do not directly address efficiency or payoff in public goods games with or without punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, contextual evidence that decentralized, reputation-based information exchange (gossip) can improve the effectiveness and reduce the costs of social control mechanisms, such as punishment or exclusion, in environments where cheating is possible. However, it does not provide direct evidence or quantitative estimates for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their close variants. The agent-based simulation results suggest that reputation mechanisms can sustain higher system performance (e.g., product quality) under high cheating rates, but these are not directly translatable to PGG efficiency outcomes. Thus, the paper may inform mechanistic reasoning about the role of information and retaliation risk in punishment systems, but does not provide parameter-level evidence for the prediction task.

- source: Taylor, C., & Nowak, M. A. (2007). Transforming the dilemma. *Evolution*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

For each mechanism, it derives the threshold conditions under which cooperation can be stable or invade. The analysis is mathematical and does not include empirical or simulation results. Punishment is discussed only as an extension, not as a primary focus, and is said to be subsumed under direct or indirect reciprocity. The paper does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or the effect of punishment on these outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It is a theoretical synthesis of mechanisms for cooperation in the Prisoner's Dilemma, with only a brief, conceptual discussion of punishment. The paper does not analyze or model the effect of punishment on group payoff, efficiency, or related outcomes, and does not provide parameterized results for game design dimensions relevant to the prediction task. Its main value is in clarifying that punishment is not a separate mechanism but an extension of reciprocity, and that efficiency or payoff outcomes are not directly addressed.

- source: West, S. A., Griffin, A. S., & Gardner, A. (2007). Social semantics: altruism, cooperation, mutualism, strong reciprocity and group selection. *Journal of Evolutionary Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=N/R | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that much confusion in the literature on cooperation, altruism, and punishment arises from inconsistent or ambiguous use of key terms. It clarifies that in many models (including public goods games), behaviors labeled as 'altruistic' or 'strong reciprocity' may actually be mutually beneficial, providing direct fitness benefits to the actor as well as to the group. Punishment, often called 'altruistic punishment,' can also be selfish or spiteful, depending on the context. The paper argues that clear definitions and careful distinction between proximate and ultimate explanations are essential for progress in the field. It also notes that the group selection and kin selection frameworks are mathematically equivalent in most cases relevant to cooperation and public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct empirical evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. However, it offers important theoretical context: it cautions that the effect of punishment on cooperation and efficiency depends on the underlying fitness consequences and the structure of the game. The paper suggests that punishment can increase cooperation and group benefit if it provides a direct or indirect fitness advantage, but the terminology used in the literature may obscure whether the effect is truly altruistic or mutually beneficial. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the need for precise definitions of efficiency and careful mapping of model assumptions to experimental designs. It also highlights that the effect of punishment may depend on whether it is costly, whether it increases group survival or productivity, and how benefits are distributed.

- source: Santos, F. P., Pacheco, J. M., & Santos, F. C. (2021). The complexity of human cooperation under indirect reciprocity. *Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper reviews the literature on indirect reciprocity, emphasizing how the complexity of social norms and strategies affects the evolution and maintenance of cooperation. It synthesizes theoretical and computational results showing that: (1) more complex social norms can, in principle, sustain higher cooperation, but are harder for individuals to implement; (2) the costliness of reputation spreading, the observability of interactions, and empathy are key moderators of cooperation under indirect reciprocity; (3) when complexity costs are introduced, simpler norms may outperform more complex ones in sustaining cooperation. The paper provides a proof-of-concept simulation showing that cooperation rates under different social norms decline as the cost of implementing complex strategies increases. However, the paper does not report efficiency, group payoff, or direct welfare outcomes, and does not manipulate or analyze punishment in a public goods context.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is not directly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not report or synthesize results on group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, nor does it manipulate punishment or sanctions. Its main relevance is in highlighting that the complexity of social norms and the cognitive costs of implementing strategies can moderate cooperation rates in reputation-based systems. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency or payoff outcomes in PGG-like games with punishment, this paper provides only indirect, contextual background, not quantitative or directly transferable evidence.

  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  findings: The paper finds that conditional cooperation mechanisms (including punishment and reward) can select for the evolution of tactical deception, as deceivers can exploit conditional cooperators by hiding their defection. Theoretical results show that, under certain conditions (notably negative frequency dependence in deception detection), cooperation and deception can coexist at equilibrium. Comparative analysis in primates supports the prediction that more cooperative species exhibit more tactical deception. However, the paper does not analyze efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, nor does it experimentally manipulate punishment or public goods game parameters.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. Its theoretical model is based on the iterated prisoner's dilemma and focuses on the evolutionary dynamics of deception and cooperation, not on payoff or efficiency outcomes. While it discusses punishment as one of several conditional cooperation mechanisms, it does not analyze the quantitative impact of punishment on group efficiency or payoff. Therefore, it offers only indirect, contextual support for the idea that enforcement mechanisms can have complex evolutionary consequences, but it does not inform the prediction task regarding efficiency changes due to punishment in public goods games.

- source: Yan, M. H., Mathew, S., & Boyd, R. (2023). Doing what others do does not stabilize continuous norms. *Pnas Nexus*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that in models where norm-relevant behaviors are continuous (e.g., contribution levels in a public goods context), similarity-based payoffs (including social sanctions or punishment for deviating from the mean) do not stabilize arbitrary or costly norms. Instead, the equilibrium norm is determined by direct effects (such as individual or group payoffs, moral preferences, or cognitive/cultural attractors). Only in discrete norm spaces, or when categorization or multiple cognitive attractors are present, can similarity-based payoffs maintain multiple stable equilibria or persistent between-group differences. For public goods-like environments, this suggests that punishment or social sanctions alone are insufficient to maintain high-cooperation norms unless additional forces (e.g., moral preferences, discrete categories, or multiple optima) are present.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that, in continuous public goods games or similar environments, enabling punishment or social sanctions (modeled as similarity-based payoffs) will not, by itself, maintain high-efficiency (cooperative) equilibria. The equilibrium will be determined by direct payoff incentives, not by the presence of punishment, unless the game is discretized or there are additional forces (e.g., moral preferences, cognitive attractors) that create multiple optima. For the downstream prediction task, this suggests that, in continuous-choice PGGs, the presence of punishment may not increase efficiency unless the design includes discrete choices, strong moral framing, or other mechanisms that create stable high-cooperation equilibria. The paper does not provide quantitative predictions of efficiency, but it strongly suggests that punishment alone is not sufficient for efficiency gains in continuous settings.

- source: Sterelny, K. (2016). Cooperation, Culture, and Conflict. *British Journal for the Philosophy of Science*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper argues that human cooperation evolved in two major transitions: first, among Pleistocene foragers, where cooperation was individually advantageous and stabilized by mutualism, reciprocation, and punishment of freeriders (both shirkers and bullies); and second, in the Holocene, where larger, more hierarchical societies required new mechanisms (including group selection and intergroup conflict) to maintain cooperation. Punishment is discussed as a key mechanism for controlling freeriding and maintaining cooperation, but the analysis is conceptual and evolutionary rather than experimental or quantitative. The paper references experimental public goods games and their findings (e.g., punishment stabilizes cooperation), but only as background for theoretical arguments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical context for how punishment and norm enforcement may affect cooperation and, by extension, efficiency in public-goods-like environments. It does not provide quantitative or empirical evidence that can be directly used to predict efficiency outcomes from game design dimensions. However, it supports the idea that punishment is more likely to stabilize cooperation (and thus efficiency) in small, transparent, repeated-interaction groups, and that the effectiveness of punishment may depend on social structure, group size, and transparency. For prediction tasks, this paper is best used as background for mechanism hypotheses rather than as a source of direct predictive evidence.

- source: Jagers, S. C., Harring, N., Löfgren, Sjöstedt, M., Alpizar, F., Brülde, B., Langlet, D., Nilsson, A., Almroth, B. C., Dupont, S., & Steffen, W. (2020). On the preconditions for large-scale collective action. *Ambio*.

  findings: The paper argues that as collective action problems become larger in scale (more actors, greater spatial/temporal distance, more complexity), spontaneous cooperation becomes less likely due to increased stressors such as anonymity, lack of accountability, heterogeneity, and uncertainty. Third-party interventions (which may include sanctions, incentives, or other mechanisms) become increasingly necessary to generate and sustain collective action. The framework is intended to guide future empirical research and policy design, but does not itself provide new empirical evidence or quantitative predictions about efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods games or their variants.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct empirical evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. However, it offers theoretical context: it suggests that in larger-scale settings, informal mechanisms (like peer punishment) are less effective, and formal third-party interventions (which may include sanctions or rewards) become more important for sustaining cooperation. For prediction tasks, this implies that the effectiveness of punishment in increasing efficiency may diminish as group size, spatial/temporal distance, and complexity increase, unless punishment is institutionalized or externally enforced. The paper does not provide parameter-level guidance for the specific game design dimensions required for quantitative prediction.

- source: Golman, R. (2016). Good manners: signaling social preferences. *Theory and Decision*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A

  findings: The paper develops a formal model of social preferences that incorporates both other-regarding preferences and social image concerns, showing that these together create incentives for individuals to credibly signal their social preferences through both actions (such as contributing to a public good, punishing, or rewarding) and communication (cheap talk). The model predicts that individuals will use both costly and costless signals to communicate their type (altruistic or spiteful), and that the availability of communication can affect the use of costly signals like punishment or reward. The paper discusses, but does not empirically test, the implications for public goods games, including the idea that punishment may serve as a signal of ill will and that communication may substitute for punishment. No direct findings on efficiency or payoff outcomes are provided.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide empirical evidence or quantitative predictions relevant to forecasting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. Its main contribution is a theoretical framework for understanding how social preferences and signaling motives may influence the use of punishment, reward, and communication. While it suggests that communication opportunities may reduce the use of costly punishment (and vice versa), and that punishment can serve as a signal of ill will, it does not provide data or direct analysis of efficiency outcomes. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper is only indirectly informative, mainly by highlighting possible mechanisms and moderators (such as the availability of communication or the signaling value of punishment/reward), but not by providing parameter estimates or outcome data.

  findings: The paper finds that large-scale cooperation (hundreds of individuals) in the production of public goods (e.g., communal hunts, construction of drivelines, warfare, habitat improvement) was common in Holocene and likely Pleistocene foraging societies, even at low population densities. Mechanisms such as direct sanctions (ostracism, denial of aid, physical punishment), social norms, and leadership roles were important for sustaining cooperation beyond the scale where mutualistic or reciprocal incentives suffice. The evidence challenges the view that ancestral human cooperation was limited to small groups. However, the paper does not report or analyze efficiency or payoff outcomes in a way that would directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments.

However, it does not provide quantitative or experimental evidence on efficiency or payoff outcomes, nor does it analyze the effect of specific game design dimensions (e.g., punishment cost, MPCR, player count) on efficiency. Thus, while it supports the plausibility of punishment as a mechanism for sustaining cooperation in large groups, it does not provide data that can be directly used to predict efficiency changes in public-goods-game-like experiments. Its main value is in highlighting the importance of sanctions and institutions for large-scale cooperation and the likely need for additional mechanisms as group size increases.

- source: Li, T., Li, S. Q., Li, B. J., Zhang, Z. X., Luo, Y. J., & Feng, C. L. (2023). Navigating uncertainty in human social decision-making: Consequences and coping strategies. *Social and Personality Psychology Compass*.

  findings: The review finds that both social and nonsocial uncertainty can reduce prosocial behaviors by providing excuses for selfishness or increasing loss aversion, but in some contexts, uncertainty can also increase prosociality (e.g., by promoting forgiveness or overestimating one's impact on others). Coping strategies for social uncertainty include impression formation, impression updating, and compliance with social norms. Punishment and reward are discussed as norm enforcement tools, but the review does not analyze their effects on efficiency or group payoff. The review highlights the complexity and context-dependence of uncertainty's effects on cooperation and norm enforcement.

However, it does not provide quantitative or direct evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff, nor does it systematically analyze how game design dimensions moderate these effects. Thus, it is not directly informative for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions and control efficiency, but it may be useful for understanding mechanisms and generating hypotheses about the role of uncertainty and norm enforcement.

- source: Alonso, J., Fernández, A., & Fort, H. (2006). Prisoner's Dilemma cellular automata revisited:: evolution of cooperation under environmental pressure. *Journal of Statistical Mechanics-theory and Experiment*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that requiring agents to achieve a minimum payoff threshold (environmental pressure) in spatial Prisoner's Dilemma games can lead to the emergence of cooperation, even when the payoff for mutual defection is not very harsh. The fraction of cooperators increases with the threshold up to a point, and the addition of a Pavlovian update rule (win-stay, lose-shift) further increases cooperation and leads to power-law cluster size distributions in some parameter regions. However, the paper does not report on group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, and does not include explicit punishment or reward mechanisms beyond the standard PD payoffs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is of limited direct use for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not study public goods games or explicit punishment mechanisms. The model is a spatial Prisoner's Dilemma with environmental thresholds and focuses on the fraction of cooperators as the main outcome. There is no analysis of group efficiency or total payoff, and no manipulation of punishment or sanctioning technology. The findings may be of some indirect relevance for understanding how environmental constraints can promote cooperation, but do not provide evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGG-like environments.

- source: Jordan, J. J. (2023). A pull versus push framework for reputation. *Trends in Cognitive Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=N/R | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: num_rounds, reward_exists, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper argues that reputation systems can incentivize cooperation through two distinct mechanisms: 'pull' (where evaluators are motivated to cooperate with good actors for direct reciprocal benefit) and 'push' (where evaluators are motivated by normative pressure to cooperate with good actors to maintain their own reputation). It reviews how these mechanisms map onto different game-theoretic models (e.g., coordination games, indirect reciprocity) and discusses their implications for psychology, behavior, and the design of reputation-based interventions. The framework is used to interpret existing empirical findings and to generate predictions about when and how reputation will promote cooperation or punishment, but does not test these predictions directly.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct empirical evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. However, it offers a conceptual framework that may help interpret when and why reputation, cooperation, and punishment mechanisms (including those in PGGs) are likely to be effective, depending on whether incentives are primarily 'pull' or 'push'. For prediction tasks, the paper suggests that the structure of the game (e.g., whether it is a coordination game, whether social norms are salient, whether actions are observable) will moderate the effectiveness of reputation and punishment, but does not provide quantitative estimates or direct evidence for efficiency outcomes.

- source: Schimit, P. H. T., Santos, B. O., & Soares, C. A. (2015). Evolution of cooperation in Axelrod tournament using cellular automata. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The study finds that in a spatial evolutionary Prisoner's Dilemma, the success of cooperative strategies depends strongly on the fitness function used for strategy updating. When fitness functions penalize aggressive (high 'caused death') strategies, cooperation rates and the prevalence of cooperative strategies increase. When there is no such penalty, defectors dominate. However, even with punishment-like strategies or fitness penalties, extreme punishment is not always effective at promoting cooperation. Tit-for-tat is not the most successful strategy in this spatial, evolutionary context. The model does not directly measure group efficiency or total group payoff, but higher cooperation is associated with longer average lifetimes (a proxy for individual fitness). The results are not directly transferable to public goods games with explicit costly punishment, but suggest that the structure of incentives for aggression and retaliation can shape cooperation outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides only indirect and adjacent evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. The model is a spatial evolutionary Prisoner's Dilemma, not a public goods game, and does not implement explicit costly punishment as a player choice. Instead, some strategies are retaliatory, and some fitness functions penalize aggression. The main outcomes are cooperation rates and strategy prevalence, not group efficiency or total payoff. The findings suggest that penalizing aggression (analogous to punishing defectors) can increase cooperation, but the effect on efficiency is not directly measured. For prediction tasks, this paper may inform the qualitative expectation that punishment or penalties for defection can promote cooperation, but the quantitative effect on efficiency in public goods games cannot be inferred from these results.

- source: Isoni, A., & Sugden, R. (2019). Reciprocity and the Paradox of Trust in psychological game theory. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The main finding is that models of reciprocal kindness, as formalized in psychological game theory (e.g., Rabin 1993, Charness & Rabin 2002, Falk & Fischbacher 2006), cannot explain mutually beneficial trust and trustworthiness in Trust Games—this is termed the 'Paradox of Trust.' The paper argues that trust and trustworthiness are better explained by a different form of reciprocity, namely reciprocal cooperation, where individuals are motivated to play their part in mutually beneficial practices conditional on others doing the same. The paper sketches how this approach can be applied to public goods games, but does not provide empirical results or efficiency data.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide empirical evidence or quantitative results that can be used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their variants. However, it is conceptually relevant for understanding the psychological underpinnings of cooperation and the limitations of certain reciprocity models. For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that models focusing solely on rewarding/punishing intentions (reciprocal kindness) may not capture the mechanisms that lead to efficient cooperation in trust or public goods games. Instead, models of reciprocal cooperation may be more appropriate for predicting when mutually beneficial outcomes (i.e., high efficiency) can arise. The paper does not address specific game design dimensions or provide data on how punishment affects efficiency.

- source: Fan, X., Wang, L. D., Liu, X. S., & Zheng, A. H. (2025). Exemplary cooperation strategy with positive influence enhances cooperation quality in social dilemma. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.

  findings: The introduction of exemplary cooperation (a strategy that pays an unconditional cost and exerts a positive influence on defectors) can prevent the collapse into full defection, even under high temptation to defect. The model shows that, depending on parameters (cost of exemplary cooperation, unforced-feedback from defectors, benefits of mutual cooperation, temptation to defect), the population can reach stable states of coexistence between strategies, or even full cooperation. The presence of exemplary cooperators increases the resilience of cooperation and can enhance the 'quality' of cooperation, but the paper does not quantify efficiency or group payoff. The results are based on theoretical analysis and numerical simulations of replicator dynamics and spatial evolutionary games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, theoretical evidence that introducing a strategy with positive influence (akin to a non-punitive, exemplary cooperator) can promote the persistence of cooperation and prevent full defection, even when defection is highly tempting. However, the model does not include explicit punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff. The findings may be relevant for understanding how non-punitive, positive influence strategies affect cooperation, but are only adjacent to the prediction task of estimating efficiency changes due to punishment in public goods games. The results suggest that mechanisms other than punishment (e.g., exemplary behavior) can also sustain cooperation, but do not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency.

  findings: The study finds that introducing a reputation-based imitation mechanism in a spatial prisoner's dilemma game with both node-type and link-type strategists increases the frequency of cooperation, especially when the population is a balanced mix of both types. Link-type strategists can selectively cooperate or defect with neighbors, effectively punishing defectors and protecting cooperative clusters. The strength of the reputation effect and the reputation adjustment factor both positively influence cooperation rates. However, all reported outcomes are in terms of cooperation frequency and network structure, not group payoff or efficiency.

While it models a form of punishment (link-type strategists defecting against defectors) and shows that this increases cooperation rates, it does not report on efficiency, welfare, or group payoff. The model is a spatial prisoner's dilemma, not a public goods game, and the punishment mechanism is endogenous to strategy choice rather than an explicit costly punishment option. Thus, the paper may provide some indirect support for the idea that targeted sanctions can promote cooperation, but it does not inform quantitative prediction of efficiency outcomes in PGGs with or without punishment.

- source: Suratin, A., Utomo, S. W., Martono, D. N., & Mizuno, K. (2023). Indonesia's Renewable Natural Resource Management in the Low-Carbon Transition: A Conundrum in Changing Trajectories. *Sustainability*.

  dimensions: reward_exists, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that Indonesia's NRM practices are primarily allocation-focused and legal-normative, which limits their effectiveness in promoting sustainable, cooperative behavior. By integrating the PD/game theory approach into a socio-ecological governance framework, the authors argue that institutions can be designed to foster trust, reciprocity, and cooperation. They highlight that social punishment (e.g., social disapproval) may be more effective than financial sanctions in promoting norm compliance and cooperation, and that non-utilitarian values (e.g., stewardship, feelings of guilt) should be incorporated into NRM incentive structures. However, the paper does not present new empirical data or analyze efficiency or payoff outcomes directly.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides conceptual and theoretical support for the idea that punishment (especially social punishment) can increase cooperation in NRM dilemmas, and that institutional and value-based design features are important moderators. However, it does not provide empirical estimates or quantitative predictions of efficiency or payoff changes due to punishment in PGG-like games. For downstream prediction tasks, this paper suggests that the effectiveness of punishment may depend on whether it is social or financial, the presence of trust and reciprocity, and the integration of non-utilitarian values, but it does not provide direct evidence to calibrate efficiency outcomes. Its main value is in highlighting relevant mechanisms and moderators for consideration in empirical synthesis.

- source: MOLM, L. D. (1994). DEPENDENCE AND RISK - TRANSFORMING AND STRUCTURE OF SOCIAL-EXCHANGE. *Social Psychology Quarterly*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=N/R | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that the risk inherent in social exchange relations (mutual dependence) can be reduced by structuring transactions as negotiated (joint decision, binding agreements) or as serially dependent (allowing for contingent action and trust development). Negotiated transactions most directly transform dependence into interdependence, reducing risk and making outcomes more predictable. Serially dependent transactions (such as repeated reciprocal exchanges) also reduce risk, but less so, and rely more on emergent trust and norms. Generalized exchange (indirect reciprocity) is riskier and less amenable to these risk-reducing mechanisms. The paper suggests that these structural features influence the likelihood of stable cooperation, trust, and solidarity, but does not provide empirical evidence or direct predictions about efficiency or payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical context for how structural features of social exchange (e.g., repeated interaction, negotiation, direct vs. generalized exchange) may moderate the risk of defection and the emergence of cooperation. While it does not address public goods games or punishment directly, it suggests that mechanisms which increase interdependence (such as repeated play or enforceable agreements) should improve group outcomes and stability. For prediction tasks, the paper implies that game designs with more interdependence, repeated interaction, or enforceable agreements are likely to see higher efficiency or more stable cooperation, but it does not provide quantitative or empirical evidence. The relevance to punishment is indirect: punishment is mentioned as a possible influence tactic in negotiation, but not as a central mechanism.

  findings: The paper finds that a wide range of unselfish behaviors in one-shot, anonymous economic games are better explained by a 'moral preference hypothesis'—that is, people have preferences for following their own personal norms about what is right or wrong, beyond the monetary consequences of their actions. This framework organizes and explains empirical deviations from standard social preference models in games such as the dictator game, ultimatum game (altruistic punishment), Prisoner's Dilemma (cooperation), trust game (trustworthiness), sender-receiver game (truth-telling), and trade-off games (equality vs. efficiency). The review also discusses the practical implications of targeting personal norms (rather than social or injunctive norms) in interventions to promote pro-sociality, and proposes a formal utility function incorporating personal norms. However, the paper does not provide new data or analyze efficiency or payoff outcomes.

It does not report efficiency, group payoff, or related outcomes, nor does it analyze the effect of punishment or sanctions in multi-player, repeated, or public goods games. Its main contribution is theoretical: it suggests that personal moral norms are a key driver of unselfish behavior (including punishment) in one-shot, anonymous games, and that interventions targeting personal norms may be more effective than those targeting social norms. For the downstream prediction task, this paper provides only indirect, mechanistic context about why people might punish or cooperate, but not quantitative or design-specific evidence about efficiency effects.

- source: Yang, Z. H., & Zhang, L. P. (2023). Random migration with tie retention promotes cooperation in the prisoner's dilemma game. *Chaos*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing
  findings: The study finds that tie retention (the ability to keep social ties with ex-neighbors after migration) can facilitate the emergence and maintenance of cooperation in the prisoner's dilemma game, even when migration is random. The maximum number of retained ex-neighbors (m) is a key parameter: higher m leads to higher cooperation rates. Social diversity in tie retention (variation in m across players) further enhances cooperation. Migration probability (p) has a non-monotonic effect: lower p generally favors cooperation, but diversity in p can create optimal conditions for cooperation. The results challenge the previous view that random migration is always detrimental to cooperation. However, all findings are in terms of cooperation rates, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not manipulate or model punishment, nor does it report efficiency, group payoff, or related outcomes. Its main relevance is in showing that network structure, migration, and tie retention can strongly affect cooperation rates in the prisoner's dilemma game. For the downstream prediction task, this paper suggests that social stickiness (tie retention) and migration patterns are important moderators of cooperation, but it does not inform the quantitative effect of punishment on efficiency.

  findings: The paper finds that in a repeated RSP game modeling P2P interactions, the introduction of a 'loner' strategy (voluntary withdrawal) allows for stable coexistence of cooperators, non-cooperators, and loners, preventing the collapse of cooperation even in the absence of incentives or punishment. The system exhibits cyclic dominance of strategies, and the stability of cooperation is sensitive to factors such as population size, network overlay dynamism, and initial population composition. The model does not include explicit punishment or reward mechanisms, and does not report efficiency or group payoff as outcomes. Simulations show that large, weakly dynamic populations are more likely to sustain stable cycles, while small or highly dynamic overlays are prone to domination by a single strategy.

Its main relevance is in showing that, in a repeated game with voluntary participation (the 'loner' option), stable cooperation can emerge without explicit punishment or reward. The model is adjacent to PGGs but differs in key respects: it is a zero-sum RSP game, not a public goods game, and does not manipulate or analyze punishment, reward, or efficiency. - source: Cordes, C., Elsner, W., Graebner, C., Heinrich, T., Henkel, J., Schwardt, H., Schwesinger, G., & Su, T. Y. (2021). The collapse of cooperation: the endogeneity of institutional break-up and its asymmetry with emergence. *Journal of Evolutionary Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper argues that cooperation in groups and societies is fragile and subject to collapse due to endogenous factors such as group growth, increasing complexity, cognitive limits, and the ceremonialization of institutions. Collapse is often sudden and asymmetric with respect to emergence, meaning that the conditions under which cooperation breaks down are not simply the reverse of those under which it emerges. Theoretical mechanisms include the role of group size (with a cognitive upper limit for informal cooperation), the importance of network structure (with clustered and heterogeneous networks supporting more robust cooperation), and the increasing costs of monitoring and punishment as cooperation declines. The paper also notes that high punishment cultures may be associated with lower cooperation and socio-economic thriving. However, all findings are theoretical or based on secondary synthesis; no new empirical or experimental data are presented.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical context for how group size, network structure, and the costs of monitoring and punishment may affect the emergence and collapse of cooperation in public-goods-like environments. It suggests that as groups grow or as network structures become less favorable, cooperation may collapse suddenly and may not be easily restored. The paper also highlights that high punishment costs and cultures may undermine cooperation. However, because the paper is theoretical and does not report empirical data on efficiency or payoff outcomes, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in specific public goods game designs. Its main value is in highlighting potential moderators and mechanisms that could inform the interpretation of empirical results from other studies.

  findings: The authors develop a system-theoretic, utility-based model for the evolution of group fractions (cooperators, defectors, regulators) in self-organized societies. They show analytically that, under resource constraints, neither defectors nor regulators can exceed about 10% of the population in stable equilibrium. The model demonstrates that cooperation can persist without explicit punishment mechanisms, provided resources are finite. When regulators (punishers) are present, their fraction is also limited by resource costs. The model is applied to real-world data from countries, but only to estimate group fractions and stability, not efficiency or payoff. No direct predictions or measurements of efficiency, group payoff, or surplus are made.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar experimental designs. It offers a theoretical perspective that, in resource-constrained societies, both defectors and regulators (punishers) are limited in equilibrium, and cooperation can be stable even without explicit punishment. However, the model does not map onto the game design dimensions or efficiency outcomes used in experimental PGG research. Thus, it should not be used as direct evidence for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment in PGG-like environments, but may provide indirect, high-level context about the limits of punishment and defection in self-organized systems.

  findings: The paper theorizes that the universal presence of supernatural sanction systems (rewarding and punishing afterlives) in archaic civilizations functioned to curb individual selfishness and support group-level cooperation, overcoming the problem of genetic competition among cooperators. It suggests that such beliefs and associated costly rituals (e.g., wasteful royal funerals) reinforced the credibility of these sanction systems, thereby enhancing group cohesion and effectiveness. The argument is that these group-level cultural adaptations, including moral norms and invisible sanction systems, were selected because they increased the survival and success of social groups, even if they imposed costs on individuals. However, the paper does not provide empirical evidence or quantitative predictions about efficiency or payoff effects.

  findings: The paper argues that human cooperation at large scales is best explained by cultural group selection, where rapid cultural adaptation leads to persistent differences between groups. These differences, when linked to group-beneficial norms (such as those enforced by punishment and reward systems), can spread through group competition, imitation of successful groups, and selective migration. The authors posit that moral systems enforced by sanctions and rewards increase group welfare and the prevalence of prosocial motives. However, the paper is theoretical and does not present empirical data or experimental results on efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods games or similar environments.
